## Capítulo 1 — Security Officer Operations (págs. 1–61)

**Central idea:** The security officer is the most visible and highest-cost part of private security. It pays off only as part of a comprehensive protection plan. For that, it needs good selection, training, supervision, written orders, shift scheduling, reports, and an environment that supports the officer's vigilance.

### 1. Context and industry (chapter introduction)

- Evolution of the names: watchman (whose function was to protect property) → guard → today security officer or protection officer. In some settings: loss prevention officer.
- Uniformed service is the most visible element of the private sector and a worldwide industry worth billions of dollars. The public still sees it as “guards,” without knowing the variety of services.
- The attacks of September 11, 2001 led to unprecedented public and media scrutiny of airport security personnel. Result: a better appreciation of their functions.
- Today governments regulate both proprietary and contract officers. Some jurisdictions also regulate door supervisors, repossession agents, and locksmiths.
- Better-trained personnel = greater return on investment (ROI). With technology, fewer people cover more tasks, some outside strict security: communications, customer service, and transportation.
- There is additional growth in regions with civil unrest and war. Against armed adversaries, heavily armed security forces have been used.

| Item | Figure | Source |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Increase in security personnel in the US after 9/11 | 13%; more than 1 million jobs in 2002 | Bureau of Labor Statistics 2007 |
| Security officers in the US (2019) | 1.1 million; about 60% employed by contract agencies | Bureau of Labor Statistics 2019 |
| Police and sheriff’s officers in the US | About 665,000 | Bureau of Labor Statistics 2019 |
| Private security / police ratio in the US | “Almost two to one” | Book |
| Private security worldwide (2017) | About 20 million workers; an industry of about US$180 billion, forecast at US$240 billion in 2020 | McCarthy 2017 |
| India | About 7 million in private security versus 1.4 million police | McCarthy 2017 |
| China | About 5 million in private security versus 2.7 million police | McCarthy 2017 |

**Exam note:** with the book’s own figures (1.1 million versus 665,000) the ratio is 1.65 to 1, not “almost two to one.” If asked, the book’s literal phrase is “almost two to one.”

### 2. Use of security officers (1.1)

- They are best used as part of a complete protection plan, not as a stand-alone resource.
- Security management plans are based on understanding the risks that must be controlled. Officers are just one more strategy.
- Their use must be evaluated periodically together with the other protection resources (hardware and electronics).
- Today they work in more settings than ever and sometimes replace the police or the military. They patrol urban areas or military installations.
- They watch heavily frequented areas: stadiums, shopping centers, and large residential complexes. They also transport prisoners or detainees.
- They are increasingly the organization’s “eyes and ears”: they detect violations and also worrisome behavior that may herald a threat.

#### Number of officers required (1.1.1)

Factors that determine how many officers a facility needs:

1. Level of risk.
2. Physical complexity and size of the facility.
3. Number of employees.
4. Nature of the work.
5. Number of entrances (and the hours they are open).
6. Value and quantity of the assets protected.
7. Number of patrols needed.
8. Number of escorts and special assignments.

| Post covered 24 hours, 7 days (officers working 40 h per week) | Officers per post |
| --- | --- |
| Minimum (24 × 7 = 168 h ÷ 40 h) | 4.2 |
| Covering sick leave, vacations, and holidays | 4.5 |
| With longer vacations or more training demands | Up to 5 full-time |

- The supervisory force is usually completed with support staff: administrative, communications, training, and supplies.
- The amount of support staff depends on the size and complexity of the security force.

### 3. Basic functions (1.2)

Whether the force is proprietary or contract, the tasks it must perform are identified first. Possible basic functions:

1. Access control and control of pedestrian and vehicle movement.
2. Patrol of buildings and perimeters.
3. Escort of materials and people.
4. Inspection for security and fire risks.
5. Monitoring of assets from a control center.
6. Emergency response.
7. Handling disruptive people.
8. Observing and reporting worrisome activities or behavior.
9. Occupational safety and accident prevention.
10. Public relations.
11. Special assignments.

#### Access control (1.2.1)

**Definition:** access control is the regulation of movement into, out of, and within a building or designated area. It applies to people, means of transport, and materials. It is a primary function of the officer.

Typical instructions:

- Verify that employees are who they say they are and wear a visible badge, or comply with other security rules.
- Issue special passes to visitors and regulate their access with an established authorization process.
- Identify and report anyone without proper identification. Some may be temporarily detained.
- Report anyone who attempts to bring in contraband or appears to be under the influence of alcohol or controlled substances.
- Identify and question anyone removing property, to confirm that they are authorized.
- Watch the materials coming in and going out: check packages and bags and receive courier or parcel shipments.
- Watch the vehicles coming in and going out: vessels, aircraft, and land vehicles (trains, buses, heavy machinery, cars, motorcycles, and trucks).
- In crises or disturbances: take body temperature, detect inappropriate clothing (e.g., a heavy coat in hot weather), and identify erratic behavior.

#### Patrol (1.2.2)

- The patrol officer observes people, assets, and places. Reports risks and program deficiencies so they can be corrected.
- Responds to door alarms and other irregularities. The presence of a uniformed officer or an official vehicle deters crime.
- Two categories: foot patrol and vehicular patrol.
- Patrol systematically, but doubling back often and taking unexpected routes. An invariable route lets the aggressor find the best moment to attack.
- Know the area thoroughly: shortcuts, dead ends, construction, and everything that affects response. Also know the area’s risks and legitimate activities.
- Observation is the main task. When something is abnormal, assess the situation and act. Take special care when watching people.

Typical conditions the patrol officer looks for:

- Unsecured doors, windows, or other openings.
- Suspicious persons, vehicles, or circumstances.
- Disorder or unusual activity: people under the influence of alcohol or other substances, unusual gatherings, rowdy individuals, or fights.
- Hazardous conditions: leaks of water or other fluids, fire hazards, and broken equipment.
- Equipment running at odd hours.
- Problems with fire-fighting equipment or other safety equipment.
- Violations of the company’s security or protection policies.

#### Escort (1.2.3)

- General rule: security should not escort visitors or customers, because that diverts it from its protection tasks. The person who invites the visitor is responsible for escorting them.
- Exceptions, with trained officers: escorting anyone carrying large sums of money, information, or special property.
- Also: accompanying employees for their safety (e.g., to the parking lot at night) and assisting people who are ill or physically disabled. These tasks require special alertness.

#### Inspection (1.2.4)

- It can be done together with other tasks, such as patrol.
- Special inspections: fire hazards; unclosed doors, safes, and windows; other security matters.
- Because of their 24-hour presence, they are often expected to watch mechanical equipment and inspect fire-extinguishing equipment.

#### Monitoring (1.2.5)

- Increasingly it is done from centralized centers: security operations centers, monitoring centers, or global operations centers.
- A typical center controls: video surveillance, access control, fire alarms, elevators and other building functions, lighting, duress alarms, environmental alarms, and emergency call stations.
- It can cover a single site or sites across a whole country or several countries.
- Its staff must master electronic systems and computers, and tolerate an environment that is sometimes very stressful.

#### Emergency response (1.2.6)

Officers direct, coordinate, and carry out the responses set by security management. Possible emergencies:

1. Fire alarms, real and nuisance alarms.
2. Bomb threats (by telephone, electronic communication, or mail).
3. Bombings.
4. Suspicious packages.
5. Release of hazardous materials (HAZMAT), inside or outside the building.
6. Medical emergencies.
7. Mechanical and other equipment failures.
8. People trapped in elevators.
9. Extreme weather: floods, tornadoes, ice storms, wind, and heat.
10. Terrorist acts.
11. Other criminal acts: hostage-taking, shootings, or bombings.
12. Building collapses.
13. Building evacuations, partial and total.

#### Handling disruptive people (1.2.7)

- Cases: people who appear to have a mental illness, are under the influence of alcohol or illegal drugs, or lack self-control temporarily because of physical or emotional trauma.
- Also: any irrational behavior that may harm the person or others.
- It requires sensitivity. Two objectives: (1) prevent the harm their actions may cause; (2) control the person with a minimum of injury, discomfort, or embarrassment.
- The first objective is the immediate concern. The second is often forgotten during the response.
- Once the danger has passed, witnesses judge the treatment. If it was poor, they will criticize the officer and forget the risks of the moment.
- Complication 1: a gender or age difference between the person and the officer. Reluctance to make physical contact can cause a critical delay.
- Complication 2: misinterpreting or ignoring cultural factors. It damages the ability to build rapport and can unintentionally escalate the situation.
- That is why officers must be trained thoroughly in the employer’s policies, especially on the use of physical force. A written procedure is strongly recommended here.

**Most important consideration:** safety and de-escalating or eliminating the immediate danger. Other considerations:

- Legal liability of the security force and the company if an injury or other harm is caused.
- Legal liability of both if the person is not restrained and later injures others.
- Relations with employees, community, public, and media, which the officers’ conduct can affect.
- Worrying too much about legal liability leads to acting “too little and too late.”
- Adequate insurance protecting the company and security personnel must already be in place, reviewed with the insurance department.
- Even self-insureds or those who retain the risk must not decide the response solely for economic reasons.
- Publishing the policy and training partly reduces liability and image problems. It shows that the issue was given serious thought, although the officer’s conduct remains critical.
- It is advisable to explore crisis intervention training when practical.

#### Observe and report (1.2.8)

- Officers take part in the organization’s intelligence function. They help mitigate targeted incidents by criminals or terrorists.
- Even on patrol or in public relations tasks they can detect unusual surveillance and intelligence gathering, security risks, and suspicious packages.
- It is essential to recognize the signs of surveillance and pre-attack behaviors in order to mitigate the risk in time.
- If it is part of their mission, they must be ready to photograph or record suspicious behavior.

#### Public relations (1.2.9)

- They are often the first and last contact that visitors, customers, vendors, or employees have with the organization. Their manner sets the overall impression.
- They help create and maintain good relations between the security department (proprietary or contract) and the rest of the organization.
- Courtesy and efficiency are essential. Courteous, restrained officers with good judgment overcome the resistance of those who reject any authority figure.
- They must enforce the rules, but with fair and understanding treatment. An officious or careless officer damages the program or exposes it to legal liability.
- If an employee refuses to cooperate with an officer who acted correctly, the employee is referred to a supervisor for administrative action.

#### Special assignments (1.2.10)

- Since the force is usually on duty 24 hours, it may be the only personnel available. Examples: messengers, communications coordinators, or drivers.
- Diverting officers to miscellaneous services erodes the protection program.
- Another special task: promoting security awareness, by teaching or serving as a model. This way they encourage reporting risks and complying with the programs.

### 4. Officer requirements (1.3)

- They vary according to the assignment, the size and function of the facility, and the labor market. Regulations change by jurisdiction and sometimes apply only to certain industries.
- Every organization with assets to protect must set the requirements. They are based on the duties in the job description.
- Site characteristics to consider: working alone or in a team; complex or simple; routine or varied; indoor or outdoor; physically demanding or sedentary; technology-assisted or people-oriented.
- Personal traits that predict success: honesty, resourcefulness, knowledge of security principles, interpersonal skills, good presence, and dependability.
- In 2001 ASIS International (formerly the American Society for Industrial Security) recognized the need to raise standards. For that reason the guideline *Private Security Officer Selection and Training* was developed, updated in 2019.
- The guideline proposes minimum selection and training criteria for all officers, contract and proprietary.

#### Regulations (1.3.1)

| Country or region | Regulation |
| --- | --- |
| United States | Each state regulates. You must know the rules of each locality where you operate |
| Canada | The Canadian General Standards Board published standard CAN/CGSB-133.1–2017, *Security Officers and Security Officer Supervisors*. It is the minimum criterion for providing guard services to the government of Canada |
| European Union | Leaves regulation to each member state. It issued voluntary standards on training, ethics, and other topics (Button 2017) |
| China | *Regulation on the Administration of Security and Guarding Services*, January 2010 |
| India | *Private Security Agencies (Regulation) Act* of 2005 |
| Nigeria | Supervised by the Nigerian Security and Civil Defence Corps (NSCDC) |

- Before hiring officers in a jurisdiction, review the local and national rules.
- Preemployment background screening is essential.

#### Personal attributes (1.3.2)

| Attribute | What it implies |
| --- | --- |
| Character | Honest, brave, alert, disciplined, self-reliant, and loyal. This way the officer does not overlook rules or accept bribes. Responds courageously in emergencies, follows orders, reacts well to the unexpected, and looks out for the company |
| Behavior and communications | Courtesy, restraint, and interest at all times, especially in uniform. Act without haste or excessive emotion. Avoid abusive language, arguments and, if possible, force. Speak clearly and listen carefully |
| Coachability | Open to new ways of working, able to learn and adapt. Works as part of a team |
| Appearance | Neat and clean, in uniform or plainclothes. Generates respect for the officer and the organization |
| Knowledge of the job | Know the facility and the organization, for the public relations part. Know federal, state, and local laws, company rules, and criminal procedures. Know security, fire protection, emergency plans, and first aid. Requires ongoing training |
| Education | The level has improved. There are certificates and degrees in security in the US, Canada, and other countries |
| Ethics | ASIS defines ethics as a set of accepted principles that govern a group or profession. The International Foundation for Protection Officers (IFPO) created a code of ethics |

**Figure 1.1 — Protection Officer Code of Ethics, adapted from IFPO 2020:**

1. Respond to the employer’s professional needs to protect people and assets.
2. Show exemplary conduct on and off duty.
3. Protect confidential and proprietary information.
4. Maintain a safe and secure workplace.
5. Be professional in dress and behavior.
6. Enforce all legal rules and regulations.
7. Establish a working relationship with public officers.
8. Develop and show stewardship and serve as a mentor within the profession.
9. Strive for professional competence and improve professional knowledge.
10. Encourage high standards of ethics among officers.

#### Site needs (1.3.3)

- Choose candidates who fit the position they will fill.
- Example: an operations center officer does not need the same mobility as one who patrols the grounds.
- Example: someone who deals with the public needs better oral communication than someone who works without contact with them.

### 5. Training (1.4)

- The officer’s functions determine the training. The officer must be trained in all the equipment and all the procedures they will carry out.
- The topic is developed in Chapter 2.

### 6. Weapons (1.5)

- If an officer fires a firearm, the consequences are serious. The company is likely to be sued.
- At trial it is very rare for judge and jury to consider the use justified. It is almost always perceived as excessive force.
- Firearm claims often exceed policy limits. Whoever employs armed officers must purchase higher liability limits (Brownyard 2016).

**Arming an officer is justified if one or both conditions exist:**

1. Without the weapon there is greater danger to life.
2. It is reasonable to expect that the officer will have to use fatal force.

#### Firearms (1.5.1)

- Many jurisdictions require armed officers to be licensed by a government agency. They also set minimum standards for licenses or carry permits.
- Legal liability, the cost of training, and the time to complete it lead many organizations to use unarmed officers.
- Worldwide picture: in Latin America many countries allow armed officers, with highly variable training and qualification standards.
- In Europe and Asia armed officers are less common. Africa and the Middle East may allow them.
- Where no prior training is required, mishandling or misuse of weapons is a concern.
- Issuing lethal weapons is one of an organization’s greatest responsibilities. With proprietary officers the responsibility is obvious.
- Because of the smartphone, every use of force will probably be recorded and circulated on social media.
- **Contract provider:** some managers believe a good contract shifts almost all the responsibility to the provider. It does not: lawsuits usually include anyone remotely connected.
- Paying the provider means accepting its style and management practices. That is why the organization that hires it must set the crucial policies and protocols.

**If officers are armed, facility management takes on:**

1. Adequate training of the officers who will carry weapons.
2. Selection of appropriate weapons and ammunition.
3. Maintenance of the weapons by a qualified gunsmith.
4. Records of all of the above.
5. Civil liability insurance at an adequate level.

**Firearms training:**

- Complete mastery before carrying them: correct handling and live firing at a range to qualify.
- Qualification shooting is done with the weapon the officer carries. The record includes the weapon’s serial number.
- Each weapon has its own shooting characteristics. The officer must be able to state in court that they know it thoroughly.
- Train in situations that could occur at the facility. Include weapon retention. Provide periodic retraining.
- Private weapons: if permitted on duty, set and enforce standards for the type of weapon and ammunition. The alternative is to prohibit them.
- The employer’s weapons and ammunition are issued at the start of the shift and collected at the end.
- The insurer must be told that officers carry weapons.
- If a contract agency provides armed officers, all of the above must appear in the service contract.

**When firearms may be appropriate:**

| Situation | The book’s nuance |
| --- | --- |
| A lot of cash is regularly handled and there is a risk of robbery | Armed officers deter more than unarmed ones, and deterring robberies reduces the overall risk to life. But the risk of a shootout for third parties must be weighed |
| A single officer at a post or escort, in a place with felonious attacks that have occurred or are anticipated. Examples: remote booths and high-crime areas with exterior patrol | Alternative: do not use officers and replace them with remote surveillance or intrusion alarms |

- **Strategic value:** aggressors may desist if they know they will meet armed resistance.
- But the quality and quantity of defensive weapons must be a real obstacle. One or two officers with pistols may not deter if the target is very attractive and there are no other countermeasures.
- Stronger deterrence: layers of protection. They combine armed officers with vehicle control, fence and boundary security, and hardened locations.
- Shoulder weapons in hardened or defensible positions covering the attack route can deter more than pistols.

#### Less than lethal weapons (1.5.2)

- Equipping officers with less than lethal weapons requires the same thorough analysis as firearms.
- A full analysis must be done of whether the benefits outweigh the risks. Depending on the organization, a multidisciplinary team to assess risks and liabilities is advisable.
- Despite their name, they can kill. In 2017 Reuters documented more than 1,000 deaths in US police incidents involving TASERs (Reuters 2017).
- In most of those deaths other forces were also present, such as batons or pepper spray.
- An officer with a less than lethal weapon is better positioned to protect themselves and others from serious injury or death.
- In security they are only self-defense tools, not offensive weapons. They are not used to show force, intimidate, or apprehend.
- Every weapon of the officer has a single purpose: to defend their life and the life of another person.
- They are never deployed without two key elements: (1) a clear and concise use of force policy; (2) sufficient initial and recurring training.

| Most common type | Characteristics |
| --- | --- |
| Electroshock weapons | They use electricity to stun or incapacitate. Three common types: TASER, stun gun, and stun baton |
| — TASER (registered trademark) | Fires two barbed probes toward the subject with compressed gas. Affects the subject’s nervous system and usually causes loss of muscle control (Carli 2018) |
| — Stun gun | Requires direct contact with the subject |
| — Stun baton | Also requires direct contact, but is longer: the officer does not need to get as close. Stun guns and stun batons can cause pain without incapacitating |
| — Alternative names | Conducted electrical weapons (CEW) or conducted energy devices (CED). Temporary effects that can stop the aggression. Generally no permanent effects, but the subject may fall and suffer secondary injuries |
| Batons | Perhaps the most used in security. The most common is the extendable, collapsible baton. Variants: straight-handled nightstick and side-handled baton. Materials: wood, metal, or plastic-type resin |
| — Use of the baton | It is an impact weapon: its correct use almost always causes some injury. Only on large muscle masses, such as the thigh and shoulder. Never on joints or the head. Misused it can cause serious injury or death |
| Aerosol irritants | Commonly used. The most frequent compounds are CS, CN, and OC (oleoresin capsicum). They are mixed with a propellant in a compact container for quick, safe use. They are known as “tear gas” or “pepper spray” |
| — Effects and use | They irritate mucous membranes and eyes: tearing, coughing, difficulty breathing, and skin irritation. Today they last less and leave fewer residual symptoms. Their use obliges aftercare and custody of the subject |
| — Risk to third parties | They have the greatest residual impact on third parties. That is why their use is irresponsible in places like a crowded emergency room (Mesloh 2008) |

**Exam note:** the book writes “CS (2-chloroacetophenone)” and “CN (o-chlorobenzylidene).” This is reversed: CN is 2-chloroacetophenone and CS is o-chlorobenzylidene malononitrile.

#### Policy considerations (1.5.3)

- All use of weapons must be set out in the organization’s Use of Force policy.
- Training must be specific, comprehensive, and ongoing, with practice and drills. Since the officer will almost never use a weapon, lessons must be reinforced regularly (Salomon 2021).

**Aspects to regulate on lethal weapons, in addition to the officer’s competence:**

1. Conditions under which weapons may be issued.
2. Specific persons who may be armed.
3. Type of weapon and ammunition issued.
4. Quality and reliability of the weapon when issued.
5. Repair and maintenance by a qualified armorer.
6. Control and accountability of each weapon and its ammunition.
7. Specific training with that weapon.
8. Conditions under which they may be used.
9. Safety precautions when issuing and carrying them.
10. Return or handing in of the weapon when it is not needed for an authorized use.
11. Secure storage when not issued: unloaded, separated from ammunition, and locked in an approved container.
12. Accurate records of all the above points.

- Additional controls for large numbers of weapons, in areas prone to theft by terrorists or other adversaries, or highly dangerous ones (automatic).
- Measures to consider: reinforced building with intruder detection, armed patrols, and dual access, as part of a defense-in-depth.
- In those cases government standards and insurer requirements must be exceeded.
- Weapons not considered lethal (impact, electronic devices, and aerosols) must also be in the policy.
- Those statements record the company’s position on the use of weapons and are the basis of the training program.

**Use-of-force policy, according to the United Nations Office on Drugs and Crime (UNODC) 2020.** A complete policy:

- Sets the circumstances and limits of the use of force, including:
  - force in individual self-defense;
  - force to protect others;
  - force to protect property;
  - response to serious crimes.
- Sets the limits of force acceptable to the government and the organization.
- Serves to determine whether a specific use of force was reasonable and necessary.
- It must comply with the law of the jurisdiction. Individuals and companies may be held criminally or civilly liable for excessive or unjustified force, so legal advice is advisable.
- Many regulators require specific training and certification before carrying lethal or less than lethal weapons.

**Security force continuum, McGoey 2018.** A concept taught at most police academies. The example has six levels. Force can go up and down a level in seconds, as the situation demands.

| Level | Name | Content |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Officer Presence | A visible uniformed officer or a marked vehicle is usually enough to deter. Nonthreatening gestures |
| 2 | Verbal Communication | Together with presence, speech increases deterrence. Begin with a calm but firm message, seeking to de-escalate |
| 3 | Control Holds and Restraints | Use the hands to guide, hold, or contain. No blows, takedowns, or chokeholds. Handcuffs only if the officer is trained and authorized to carry them |
| 4 | Chemical Agent | Defensive weapon used only if lesser tactics failed or if there is an imminent physical threat |
| 5 | Temporary Incapacitation | Extreme, violent, and immediate situation. Nonlethal force from empty hand to an intermediate weapon: pepper spray, TASER, or baton |
| 6 | Deadly Force | Only if there is immediate fear of death or serious bodily injury at the hands of an aggressor |

**Exam note:** pepper spray appears at level 4 (chemical agent) and again as an example at level 5. It is an internal inconsistency in the book.

### 7. Uniforms and equipment (1.6)

#### Uniforms (1.6.1)

- Most organizations supply them. This way the appearance is truly uniform and each garment fits well.
- Complete seasonal uniforms are usually issued, with clothing for bad weather.
- The number of uniforms depends on the type of service and the laundry or dry-cleaning cycle.
- Trend: recognizable officers without looking authoritarian. This is achieved with casual or plainclothes attire with patches on the shoulder or pocket.
- The policy must say whether the uniform is worn off duty, including the commute to work.
- Allowing it only on duty avoids bad publicity if the officer is involved in an incident in their free time.
- Equipment according to the post: at a lobby desk, minimal. On mobile patrol: flashlight, means of communication, weapon, and notebook.

#### Body worn cameras (1.6.2)

- Police Executive Research Forum survey (August 2018): more than 80% of US police agencies were using body worn cameras or planned to do so soon.
- More than 85% of the departments already using them would recommend them.
- In favor: more accountability and they provide evidence.
- Against: the cost of storing video and the consequences when they do not record because of failure or misuse (Tarallo 2019).
- Before using them: consult legal counsel and the local police on their legality and on recording, storage, and use protocols.
- Create policies and procedures inspired by police protocols. Decide how long and where recordings are kept and how they will be used.
- Privacy: the officer must notify the public that they are being recorded. All interested parties must be informed so they can act accordingly (Torres 2019).

#### Handcuffs (1.6.3)

- They are not technically weapons, but they help subdue a subject.
- They can cause discomfort and injuries, so they require training.
- Do not leave the detainee handcuffed face down on the floor for long, to avoid positional asphyxiation (Stowell 2016).

#### Other equipment (1.6.4)

- Communications: cell phones, radios, tour devices, and personal alarms.
- Also metal detectors, body armor, and miscellaneous tools.
- The officer must know exactly how and when to use each item.

### 8. Organizational structure (1.7)

- **Definition:** organizational structure is the formal pattern of interactions and coordination designed by management. It links the tasks of individuals and groups to achieve the organization’s objectives.
- Formerly, many forces copied the military or police model: chief of security, captain, lieutenant, sergeant, and officer.
- Current trend: abandon the military or paramilitary model and use business supervisory lines. Entry-level position: security officer. Next level: supervisor. After that: assistant manager, manager, or director.
- The efficiency of the force depends on the sufficiency and skill of its supervisors.
- The manager may delegate operational authority to certain supervisors to see how they decide and supervise.
- Without adequate training and close supervision, officers may not provide the protection required.
- Supervisors are chosen for their knowledge of the job and their demonstrated administrative and leadership abilities.
- They must have the base skills of the level above and good people skills. They need special training to stay current.
- Some organizations rotate supervisors to avoid cliques and so that they learn all phases of the work.

#### Vertical model (1.7.1)

- Also called the hierarchical model. Authority originates with the senior manager and flows down through managers and supervisors to front-line personnel.
- Most effective supervisor style: recognize good performance and be objective when pointing out deficiencies.
- Must apply discipline as a corrective measure, not only as punishment.
- Managers and supervisors maintain high standards of their own to set an example and motivate.
- The art of delegating: give responsibility with the corresponding authority and demand full accountability for its use.
- The number of levels varies. The organization chart is a series of pyramids connected to other pyramids and within them.

#### Network model (1.7.2)

- Also called flattened, horizontal, or open. It is compared to a computer network.
- Each employee connects with their boss and subordinates, and also with many others. The security department connects with other departments.
- The emphasis is not on reporting lines, but on people who come together for a task and then separate or regroup.
- It may not be best for front-line personnel with fixed positions and responsibilities.
- It gives flexibility for one-off needs and allows the manager to contribute their skills to the good of the whole organization.

| Aspect | Vertical model (hierarchical) | Network model (flattened, horizontal, open) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Flow of authority | From top to bottom down to the front line | Multiple connections, also with other departments |
| Emphasis | Chain of command, delegation, and corrective discipline | Teams that form and dissolve by task |
| What the book says about its fit | Organization chart of pyramids with varying levels | Not ideal for front line with fixed positions; flexible for one-off needs |

#### Proprietary versus contract officers (1.7.3)

- Options: proprietary officers, contract officers, or a combination.
- In the 1990s, the trend toward downsizing and outsourcing favored contract. Example: General Motors converted its large proprietary force into a contract operation.
- Historically, this pattern is common when the budget is tight.
- **Hybrid force:** proprietary supervisors direct front-line contract officers. That relationship is called principal-agent.

| Arrangement | Advantages | Burdens or disadvantages |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Proprietary | Closer control of selection, screening, training, and supervision. Loyalty to the protected organization, which gives lower turnover, more knowledge of the job, and better performance | The company takes on recruiting, application management, personnel screening, benefits, performance management, discipline, and scheduling |
| Contract | Shifts part of the burden of hiring, training, and supervising to the service company. More flexibility in the number of personnel. A legal review will say whether the contract shifts part of the liability | Vendors lower prices to sell, which can mislead. Service awarded at the lowest price (low-bid) usually disappoints because the service level drops |

- To avoid the lowest-price problem, the client writes detailed bid specifications: wages, benefits, and performance expectations.
- With performance specifications it is easier to demand quality from the contractor.
- Regulating the activity requires licensing the business and also licensing or registering its security employees.
- Profile sought: the contractor looks for generalists who perform with any client. The manager of a proprietary force may look for special skills, such as detecting suspicious behavior or an affinity for electronics.
- Before opting for a proprietary force, analyze its operational and cost advantages compared with contract.
- The proprietary force would have to take on recruiting, applications, screening, training, benefits, discipline, and open post coverage.
- General civil liability, workers’ compensation, and labor relations would also have to be considered.
- **Final decision:** base it on the type of assets protected, the level of risk, and the expectations of the officers, not on cost.

**Figure 1.2 — Differences between contract and proprietary officers:**

| Contract officers | Proprietary / in-house officers |
| --- | --- |
| May be regulated by local licensing authorities | Regulated by the employer |
| Hiring criteria set by the licensing authority, the contract, and the employer’s policies | Hiring criteria set by the employer |
| Often receive less specific training and perhaps less supervision | Usually receive more training and supervision |
| Work in varied environments with changing schedules | Usually have constant schedules |
| Working conditions set by the contract | Working conditions set by the employer |
| High turnover typical | Generally, better retention |
| Loyalty to the contract provider | Loyalty to the organization that hires them |
| Tendency toward short-term assignments | Longer-term assignments |
| May report to several bosses; less clear chain of command | Clear chain of command |
| Less integrated into the workplace | More integrated and part of the workplace |
| May need to adapt to frequent retraining at different sites or assignments | More progressive site-specific training |
| Lower hiring and total costs | Higher hiring and total costs |
| More job oriented | More career oriented |

**Hybrid programs:**

- The hybrid force, with proprietary and contract officers, is gaining popularity. In some states and countries sworn police officers and licensed officers even work together.
- Hybridization always worked well between officers and technology. Joining proprietary and contract “under one roof” is an effective compromise: it saves costs and maintains reasonable control.
- Hidden cost: the natural competition between proprietary and contract. It is disruptive if the differences in pay, personality, knowledge, and ability are not resolved beforehand.
- Solution: place the account manager, the site supervisor, and the lead officers, better paid and higher performing, from the proprietary company.
- They select and hire the officers the contractor provides. This way the good relationship is created inside before the external change and improves buy-in on both sides.

Main efforts to make a hybrid program work:

1. Build a unified, standardized, sustainable, positive, and productive work culture that fosters commitment, satisfaction, and retention.
2. Establish a defined chain of command and clear communication, to defuse problems and give instructions.
3. Create a strong sense of fit, belonging, and loyalty to the work under one roof.

- This may require the contractor to give up some control over its employees, who work very close to the proprietary ones.
- The proprietary side must consciously avoid “us against them” attitudes. An open mind and clear purpose improve the odds of success.

### 9. Legal aspects (1.8)

#### The security officer as a peace officer (1.8.1)

- Security executives are divided on giving officers peace officer status.
- Against: it may give them police authority outside the facility and broaden the employer’s liability.
- Some tasks may require deputization: directing traffic on the public road or enforcing parking rules.
- If delegated, the usual and prudent course is to limit that authority to the officer’s actual functions.
- The manager must know state civil law on labor matters.
- Risk: a local police chief could mobilize all officers, including officers with peace officer status. That would reduce the guard force in the middle of a public emergency.

#### Insurance (1.8.2)

- Most organizations assume their probable liability for the acts of their officers on duty.
- But they cannot prevent the officer from being sued or criminally charged separately. The officer remains primarily responsible for their own conduct.
- In practice, the plaintiff sees the employer as having more resources and seeks to make it the principal defendant under any theory of liability.
- With officers who have deputized authority there is usually limited additional coverage: the indemnity bond required by the local government.
- That bond only protects the public. If it is collected, the surety can sue the company for what it paid, if the company was civilly liable for the harm.
- Many companies have no special insurance for officers because their commercial general liability policy covers them.
- Many contractor clients now require specific coverage for employee dishonesty and data breaches.
- With a contractor: verify that insurance for the assigned employees exists and is sufficient. Include the client’s insurance requirements in the contract.
- Being an additional named insured depends on the policy wording, which varies by insurer.
- **Minimum required:** current insurance certificates issued by the contractor’s insurer. They must show the agreed limits and include the commitment to notify the client of any change or cancellation.

### 10. Scheduling and managing the officer force (1.9)

- Despite technology and staff reductions in some settings, officers remain the largest segment of employees in the sector.
- They are also the largest cost element in most security operating budgets.
- Deployment must be guided by rational, objective business criteria, with a thorough assessment of the asset protection needs.
- Once the need is established, the next step is to schedule and assign officers in a cost-efficient way.

#### Staffing requirements (1.9.1)

- The security manager determines how many posts and officers are needed.
- Post orders are based on standard operating procedures (SOP), which must be site-specific.
- SOPs guide daily operations and may include emergency response procedures. By setting expectations, they help define the requirements of each post.

**Definition:** a security officer post is any location or combination of activities that requires a trained human being. It has three key concepts:

1. A location or combination of activities.
2. A necessary human being.
3. Training and competency to perform the activities.

**Location or combination of activities:** it can be a stationary or fixed post or a roving patrol or patrol post. Also a predetermined point: control of employees, visitors, or contractors, or a control center.

**Necessary human being:** the characteristics and reactions of the post require human capabilities. Each post combines cognitive (knowledge), psychomotor (physical), and affective (attitudes) skills. A person is needed if the post requires:

1. Distinguishing between events, people, circumstances, or objects using complex and changing criteria.
2. Conversing rationally face to face and deciding the next step based on that conversation.
3. Using physical force, or the threat of it, to restrain one or more people.
4. Exercising judgment to choose a logical mental or physical action in circumstances that vary at random.
5. Preparing continuous reports of the activities.

- If those capabilities are applied from another location, the post may not need personnel.
- Example: a person-trap with interlocked or revolving doors to control entry or exit.
- Example: fully automated access with video, intercoms, and card readers, with a human response only in case of a problem.
- If personal recognition is required and the queues are low density, a remote operator can control three or four accesses.

**Training and competency:** possessing the skills and knowledge for the assigned task.

- Without post-specific training, the officer’s effectiveness drops and the need for the post is called into question.
- If possible or actual losses require the post, appropriate training must be designed.
- Competency = the person’s ability, skills, and qualifications to perform the duties.
- It requires mental capacity (administrative tasks and judgment) and physical capacity (observing, listening, reporting, and acting in emergencies).

#### Salaries (1.9.2)

- There is growing awareness that salary must match the responsibility of the position.
- Officers protect people, physical assets, and information, and the demands have never been higher.
- They deserve salaries that let them concentrate on the work without worrying about their basic needs. Many work long hours, chain shifts without rest, or hold a second job.
- They need education, training, and experience. A fair salary helps obtain them: everyone wins.
- Better salaries lead to lower turnover and, therefore, lower turnover costs.
- **Turnover costs:** advertising, interview time, background checks, hiring, onboarding of the new employee, and training. It is usually 25% or more of the employee’s annual salary.
- Additional costs: lost productivity until the new hire performs, covering the departed employee’s hours, and a drop in customer satisfaction.
- High turnover also weakens the performance of the security operation.

#### Shift scheduling (1.9.3)

**Post hours:**

- Once the need for the post is established, it is decided when it operates. It may cover only business hours, only peak hours, or only when the need arises.
- The manager analyzes the requirements with the main internal clients affected by the post, to understand their operational needs.
- It is advisable to graph the post’s activity over 24 hours and seven days.
- If there is a lot of movement in short periods and little or none in long periods, it is more cost-effective to open only at the peaks.
- Some off-peak activity can be moved to another post. The same applies to vehicle control posts, movement of goods, and other activities.

**Post schedule:** lists the hours of each shift during which the post is covered. If it opens or closes outside the start or end of the guard shift, the actual time is noted.

**Security officer schedule — two techniques:**

| Technique | For which force | Content |
| --- | --- | --- |
| First | Contract service | Indicates whether each post requires a full-time or part-time officer. Restates the hours required per shift and per post |
| Second | Proprietary force | Typical North American week: 40 straight-time hours over five days. Under fair labor standards and wage and hour laws, going over 40 hours or five days requires premium pay. The same in the usual collective agreement. Each officer is represented by a number in the week |

- Unnecessary overtime must be avoided, but vacations and other replacements may require overtime.
- The numeric representation shows the post’s coverage and reveals errors: split days off, more than five consecutive days, and chained shifts.
- It also indicates the minimum number of officers to cover the force properly.

**Rotating shifts:**

- In many facilities the force rotates through the three traditional shifts. At fixed intervals, each officer moves from day to evening to night, or to an intermediate relief shift.
- At each change, the officer needs sufficient rest: several days off, preferably three or more consecutive (Miller 2010).
- Without adequate rest: lack of attention, falling asleep, accidents, or loss of cognitive capacity.

#### Computer or automated scheduling (1.9.4)

- The software is usually modular. The basic module assigns personnel to posts.
- Additional modules: time and attendance recording or payroll administration. For the contractor: client billing or cost control by service.
- It generates reports, charts, and graphs, and usually exports data to other programs.
- Security: authorization levels, from read-only to permission to change data.
- It analyzes the job requirements and cross-references them with the available personnel base.
- Remote access: employees can see their schedule at any time and managers can consult it outside the office.
- It allows controlling overtime, complying with labor law, and seeing who is available if an officer cannot report.

**Work site requirements** — mandatory analysis, manual or with software:

1. Identification of each post by type (fixed, vehicle, or foot) and by number.
2. Start and end hours of each shift, by day.
3. Number of officers per shift and per day.
4. Special staffing requirements per shift, if any.
5. Names of personnel not assigned to the post.

**Personnel database** — profile of each employee:

1. Name, address, and telephone numbers.
2. Employee number.
3. Shift and overtime preferences.
4. Maximum hours the employee can work.
5. Availability (key for part-time officers).
6. Special training, certifications, and qualifications: the skills inventory.
7. Vacation entitled and days requested.
8. Scheduled training days.
9. Posts from which the employee is excluded.
10. Other considerations, such as performance limitations.

**Generated schedules:** the program combines site requirements and personal data to optimize staffing. It avoids overtime. If an officer is missing, it considers travel time from home, scheduled hours, and the overtime policy.

**Proprietary force:**

- The paid holidays of each officer must be calculated. Some collective agreements allow refusing to work a holiday: pay attention to the text of the agreement.
- Include sick leave. The force’s average over the last two years serves to estimate future leave.
- Look at its actual distribution: more time off is taken in the winter months.
- Other absences: jury duty, personal reasons, or emergencies. In a proprietary force they are usually covered with overtime.
- In small forces that is expensive. Besides the extra pay, it costs fatigue, damaged morale, and less alertness.
- Those costs may not justify the apparent savings of a very tight base schedule.

**Contract force:**

- Client and agency agree on the base schedule: the total officer-hours per week. The agency must place officers at the posts to cover them.
- The problem passes from the client to the agency, but resolving schedule conflicts and absences is not necessarily easier.
- In theory, the agency has a broad personnel base and pulls officers from other clients to cover unscheduled absences.
- In practice, the replacement may never have been at that site. They do not learn the post in an hour or two and an inexperienced officer can do more harm than good.
- If relief is required from officers regularly assigned to the site, overtime helps.
- On large accounts, the agency can periodically assign personnel from other clients to occasional relief shifts, so they gain experience at the site.

**Maximizing the straight-time schedule:**

- Overlapping shifts in a planned way covers peaks without overtime.
- If the eight-hour shift includes a 30-minute or one-hour meal, the presence is extended by that same time.
- Example: a 0700 to 1500 shift with a one-hour meal ends at 1600. If the second shift is from 1500 to 2300, the two shifts coincide between 1500 and 1600.
- That overlap covers peaks such as plant shift changes or office breaks. But during the meal the officer leaves their duties.
- The meal may be unpaid, depending on company policy or the collective agreement.
- Planning meals is difficult in small forces or ones with only fixed posts. Solutions: creative use of patrol officers, temporary closing of fixed posts, or a supervisor covering the breaks.
- With a collective agreement, regularly using the supervisor for officer work can lead to a formal grievance.
- Officers must note their meals in the log. In the event of an incident, that makes it possible to know whether they were eating or should have been at the post.

### 11. General orders (1.10)

- General orders have been used by the military police, municipal and state police, and contract security companies.
- They are canons or bodies of principles for protection officers.

**Common general orders:**

1. Familiarity, fraternization, and unnecessary conversation on duty are prohibited. The professional is objective and favors no one. At a post or on patrol the officer must be vigilant, and hanging out with friends does not help.
2. Maintain military or professional bearing and keep the uniform neat. Applies mainly to uniformed personnel.
3. Plainclothes officers must also have professional bearing (retail loss prevention, doormen, and executive protection specialists). Friendly with the customer, but projecting knowledge, precision, and commitment.
4. Smoking and eating on duty are prohibited. It is done in a private, secluded place: dining room, break area, or ready room.
5. Every statement to the media is made by the designated spokesperson or the public information officer (PIO). Officers do not make statements.
6. Officers must know the operation and structure of the organization and support it. A poorly made statement can cause panic, ruin reputations, or sabotage investigations.
7. Enforce the rules with diplomacy: respect, civility, and authority only when needed. Without abusing the position.
8. The officer is both a compliance agent of property management and a representative of management.
9. Use the employer’s uniform and equipment only when and where authorized. It is a problem if they work for a second employer or stop at public places on the commute.
10. Report any criminal conviction to the employer immediately. It may be a license requirement: a state board may revoke it.
11. Remain at the post unless properly relieved or otherwise instructed. No post is left “open,” except perhaps in a serious emergency. Covering certain critical posts without a break is an operational necessity.
12. When leaving the post, fully brief the relief and pass on the orders received. This way all the intelligence and the day’s instructions are handed over.

Note: the numbering above is from this summary; the book presents nine orders in bullets (bearing, media, and diplomacy are broken out here into two points each).

### 12. Post orders (1.11)

Post orders are the most important written instructions of the security force. Functions:

1. They express the policies of the protected company.
2. They summarize the officer’s required duties.
3. They avoid the problems of verbal instructions.
4. They provide the basis for site-specific training.

- They are key in serious incidents that call into question an officer’s integrity, competence, or ability.
- Not every scenario can be described. Innovation, flexibility, and improvisation are valuable qualities.
- But it is a sign of poor management when an officer acts with poor judgment in a foreseeable incident for which there were no clear instructions.

**Criteria for drafting them:**

| Criterion | Detail |
| --- | --- |
| One subject per order | Makes it possible to find a policy or procedure quickly. Makes it easier to revise or cancel it when circumstances change |
| As brief as possible | It is an action document: what must be achieved and when. The why is explained in other parts of the training |
| Simple, easy-to-understand terms | Basic version of the local language. Reading time is inversely proportional to comprehension. That time depends on the structure of the text and the reader’s skill |
| — Vocabulary | Plain meaning, without jargon, nuance, or ambiguity. **Three-reader test:** if three people who know the company do not agree on the meaning, rewrite it |
| Detailed index | Cross-referenced index, with each subject listed in several ways. Example: “Snow removal, emergency procedures” and “Emergency procedures, snow removal” |

- They must be at every post, up to date and accessible. They are the vital link between the client’s requirements and the officer’s ability to meet them.
- Fixed post: in a designated place. Vehicle patrol: inside the vehicle.
- Foot patrol: carrying many orders is awkward. The officer can use company electronic devices with access to the orders, reports, and information exchange.
- If on paper: a reduced version, only for that post, pocket size for the uniform.
- Each set of orders is reviewed periodically to keep it current and complete.
- They require a coordinated effort to avoid contradictory orders. The risk grows with complex systems, electronic reports, or many posts.
- Management must always maintain a single coherent set of instructions.

### 13. Supervision (1.12)

- Shift supervisors train and direct the shift’s officers. They are front-line managers and key in mentoring, staff turnover, and satisfaction.
- They lead teams that respond to highly varied situations. They must understand the site requirements, management’s needs, and their own role.
- **The quality of asset protection is a direct function of local training and local supervision.**

Forms of on-site training:

- The supervisor observes the officer and gives feedback when something was not right or the officer hesitated.
- New material for one officer or the whole shift. Common practice: a staff training at the start and end of each shift. In those minutes a topic is explained, such as the essence of a new order, and the officers are asked to read it during the shift.
- New personnel: if there is opportunity and budget, pair them for a long time with the supervisor or an experienced patrol officer. Responsibilities do not decrease when someone new arrives.
- **The quality of training is directly proportional to its quantity.**
- Training log sheets: they break the site’s responsibilities down into tasks. Trainer and trainee initial each completed task.
- Those sheets can serve as the basis for post order checklists that patrol officers carry with them.

**To check that a new order was read and to reinforce it, on the visit to the post the supervisor:**

1. Asks one or more specific questions about the order.
2. Observes the officer in a real situation where it applies.
3. Poses a hypothetical situation that requires showing practical knowledge of the order.

- It takes a few minutes and should be a routine part of every post visit.
- The supervisor must visit every post. If they stay at the command post for most of the shift, performance at the other posts worsens and problems increase.
- Radios, public address, and cell phones eliminate the need to stay at a fixed post.
- Regular but unscheduled visits to all posts, with instant communication with the force.
- The supervisor observes the condition of the post, the officer, and the environment and the level of activity. By watching the route between posts, the supervisor also performs a complementary patrol.
- **Better alternative**, unless the collective agreement prohibits it: the supervisor relieves at the post while the officer is away. It gives the best view of what happens there.
- Supervisors need special training in management, human relations, interpersonal communication, labor and criminal law, emergency response, and other topics.
- That training should be given, where possible, before promotion, and continue while they hold the position.

#### Evaluations (1.12.1)

- Constant challenge: without an incident that shows very good or very poor performance, a shift’s work may never be evaluated. That complicates salary review.
- Partial solution: the supervisor evaluates and records performance after each post visit.

The evaluation must include at a minimum:

1. Personal appearance and condition of the officer.
2. Physical condition of the post.
3. Availability and condition of all required personnel and equipment, including the post orders.
4. Quality of the officer’s response to questions or training situations.
5. Quality of their response to real situations that arise during the visit.

- Everything is recorded and passed to a force management program.
- When officers or shifts change, different supervisors make independent observations.
- The manager reviews performance and ratings at regular intervals to decide whether more training or counseling is needed.
- Evaluations must give regular feedback, positive and negative, to officers and supervisors.
- **Coaching:** a two-way communication process to influence and develop the employee’s skills, motivation, attitude, judgment, ability to perform, and willingness to contribute.
- Coaching and leadership go hand in hand. Two-way communication gives the employee a voice and raises commitment and retention.
- Conscious, regular coaching shows that the manager cares about the person and builds trust (Tarallo 2020).

### 14. Officer reports (1.13)

It is said that officers are the “eyes and ears of management,” but their information often does not reach it. Causes:

1. Those who choose security may be action-oriented, not word-oriented.
2. What is reported does not seem to be acknowledged or acted on.
3. The reporting process does not encourage processing the facts carefully.
4. The organization lacks the infrastructure to collect data.

- Typical empty report: “Came on 0700. Nothing unusual. Relieved 1500.”
- Several days like that suggest the post is unnecessary, that the officer is not trained, or that the officer does not report well.
- It is advisable to ask the officer not to write that the area is secure, but what they did to secure it.
- Post orders must require reporting specific incidents: key handovers, escorts, and assistance to contractors.
- Forms that require positive statements are better than those that ask for writing a narrative.
- Reports are filed electronically so they can be searched and sorted. A web platform improves access and makes the program more transparent to management.
- This matters especially with contract security: the client sees exactly what happens. It also motivates the force.
- The manager periodically uses the accumulated information to evaluate and reinforce performance.

#### Security log (1.13.1)

**Definition:** the security log is the central reporting document. It is the primary record of events significant to the protection of the facility, made by the officers contemporaneously.

**Database log:**

- It is configured in incident management software. The force’s database can be relational to the main incident database.
- It allows retrieving all events for a place, date, and time in an easy-to-understand format.
- Trick: a drop-down menu of incident types, which the manager refines over time according to the incidents that actually occur.
- Automated format: date and time are generated automatically. The officer logs in with a unique identifier and password.
- Each event receives a unique, consecutive number. It is recorded in real time and is immediately available to supervisors and managers, contract and proprietary.
- It is captured from a desktop or laptop computer, terminal, or handheld device.
- The officer can view their entries, but not edit them. Changes or corrections go in as later entries. Editing is reserved for higher-level supervisors.
- Database and reporting functions can be expensive, but they are a great tool. They should be included if possible.

**Manual log format:** uncommon, but it still exists. Whether manual or electronic, there are two kinds:

| Kind | Content |
| --- | --- |
| Main or control log | Cumulative, consecutive history of the significant events of all shifts |
| Post log | Local record of the events of a specific post |

- Example: if the post must count people or vehicles, the counts go in the post log, as a historical fact.
- The main log notes that counts were due that day and refers to the existence of that record.

Each entry must include:

1. Entry number: makes it possible to locate or cite it.
2. Day of the week: normally abbreviated.
3. Time of day: in military time.
4. Category label: one or two words on the nature of the entry, for example “Weather.” Allows scanning the page quickly.
5. Description of the incident or event: brief, often with symbols or codes.
6. Reference: name, date, number, or other identification of another document with more information, for example a detailed complaint.

| No. | Day | Time | Category | Event | Ref. |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 36 | Mon. | 0700 | Weather | 75 °F, clear and sunny | None |

- So that they are not altered or deleted, manual entries are written in ink and in consecutive order.
- Every correction is made with a new event number, as a separate entry.

#### Use of reports and logs by management (1.13.2)

In addition to their historical and audit value, they serve general management as an authoritative source to prove:

1. Weather conditions on a date.
2. Receipt of a telephone call.
3. Exact time of an event.
4. Presence of specific people at the facility.

**Legal admissibility:** they are admissible as records made in the regular course of business. That is why they are exceptions to the rule excluding hearsay testimony. This matters in litigation, arbitration, or administrative matters where the entry is the only evidence. Requirements:

1. Kept regularly.
2. Kept by a person as part of their regular duties.
3. Recording a fact that the recorder knew personally, or that was reported by someone with personal knowledge and a duty to report.

**Data capture and trend analysis:**

- They help the manager measure the program’s effectiveness and adjust it: they show what works and what does not.
- They can justify the department’s existence by detailing incidents and staff responses.
- Trend analysis indicates whether events form a trend. If so, the program is modified to address it.

**Failure to use reports and logs:**

- Management appearing not to read them is a source of officer dissatisfaction.
- The manager must read them on time, recognize what is important, and order the correction.
- Case: an officer reports a broken exit light and it is still not fixed weeks later. The officer will think no one cares and will stop reporting.
- Scheduling and controlling the force is of utmost importance. Allocating human resources well is a fundamental responsibility of the manager.
- As personnel costs keep rising, the maximum value must be obtained from them.

### 15. Improving officer performance (1.14)

- Functions range from the routine to the highly complex. Officers usually work alone and with little direct supervision.
- They take risks that other employees are asked to avoid and deal with the full spectrum of human behavior.
- Their decisions affect the well-being of people and millions of dollars in physical assets.
- Because of the weight of first impressions, an inefficient or indifferent officer harms visitors, coworkers, and staff.
- Good officer performance is critical fiscally and humanly. The consequences of poor personnel development become apparent as soon as a serious incident occurs.
- **Excellence requires:** the traditional (pay, supervision, and deployment) plus job analysis, training techniques, the work environment (including corporate and departmental cultures), and ergonomics.
- The manager and supervisor must detect deficiencies and decide what correction to apply and when.

#### Vigilant performance (1.14.1)

The term vigilant performance has a special meaning in security. Vigilance is described in three contexts:

| Context | Definition of vigilance |
| --- | --- |
| Dictionary | Keen attention to detect danger; caution |
| Physiology | Overall responsiveness of the nervous system to external (sensory) and internal (muscles, tendons, and joints) stimuli |
| Psychology | Unspecified function of the central nervous system. Allows responding effectively to specific, infrequent, uncertain, and often low-intensity stimuli, in a monotonous environment |

- In part it is an expectation that needs cultivation and opportunities. But it is the officer who must recognize, assume, and fulfill it. That is why personnel selection is vital.
- **Vigilance fatigue:** occurs when the brain becomes saturated with the task. Main theory: cognitive resources are depleted.
- Letting the mind wander, that is, activating the default mode network, helps restore blood flow to the area of the brain that does the task.
- When nothing happens during an entire shift, fatigue can increase (Stowell 2017).
- **Stress:** generates behaviors that damage performance and physical and mental health.
- A study for the US National Institute for Occupational Safety and Health (NIOSH) was carried out by the University of Michigan’s Institute for Social Research.
- That study linked stress with: low use of one’s own abilities, low participation, low job complexity, few opportunities for advancement, and the lowest level of social support.
- According to the book, that description fits many security officer positions.

**Elements that affect vigilant performance:**

1. Design of the work area, especially space, light, heat, and noise. Example: in a booth without good heating or air conditioning, the officer thinks more about temperature than the task. It also requires suitable chairs and desks.
2. Human engineering of the tasks and equipment handling. People often perform only as well as their equipment or the design of their task allows.
3. Visual and auditory acuity: the physical ability to observe an area by sight and hearing.
4. Detection, identification, and recognition abilities. Frequently reviewing expected events improves vigilance and detection.
5. Attention and information processing: the ability to detect an event and respond as desired.
6. Job analysis: systematic collection and recording of information about the purpose of the job, its main functions, its conditions, the contacts needed, and the knowledge, skills, and abilities required (Bartol 1998). One detailed analysis per post; there must be a fit between officer, post, and company expectations.
7. Training: general (report writing, patrol techniques, emergency response) and site- and equipment-specific, with testing. Neglecting it certainly harms vigilance.
8. Work environment, including corporate and departmental cultures. Since each department is managed differently, the officer may receive contradictory messages or see unequal treatment. It happens especially in departments with both proprietary and contract officers.
9. Quality control: sets the expectations of the department and the organization. Vigilant performance is defined through expectations.
10. Morale: personnel must feel good about themselves and their work. Low morale causes complacency, inattention, more turnover, tardiness, absenteeism, and excessive sick leave.
11. Management advocacy: management reinforces vigilant behavior by advocating for the officers.

**ASIS CRISP (Connecting Research in Security to Practice) report: *Fatigue Effects and Countermeasures in 24/7 Security Operations* (Miller 2010).** Finding: automation often puts the person in a monitoring role, which is not handled well. Problems of automation and human monitoring:

1. More monitoring load for the operator, with associated attention lapses.
2. A lot of operator responsibility with little to do.
3. Loss of the operator’s manual skill.
4. “Out-of-the-loop” operator problems.
5. Automation replaces human pattern recognition with less competent sensors.
6. “Cry wolf” syndrome: many false alarms make the operator ignore signs of system failure. Failure detection falls to almost zero.
7. Inadequate displays that support neither optimal performance nor individual differences in staying alert.
8. Automation problems that are misdiagnosed, leading to erroneous corrective measures.
9. Not recognizing subtle vigilance problems until there have already been many automation-related accidents.

**Miller’s findings on fatigue (police and other shift workers):**

- Humans are incompatible with night work; schedule changes cause problems; fatigue can be acute or chronic.
- The accident rate rises sharply around 0300. It also rises at 1500, because of circadian rhythms.
- Anyone who has been awake for more than 17 hours probably reaches a dangerous level of mental fatigue.
- Chronic fatigue appears when sleep debt accumulates. Both kinds of fatigue reduce alertness and cognitive ability.

**Countermeasures:**

- Reassess how officers are viewed as a human asset.
- Review their interaction with technology, so that it improves their performance and does not reduce it.
- Train managers and subordinates on the challenges of fatigue and its countermeasures.
- Schedules with real rest: at least two nights of unrestricted sleep between shift changes, and limits on consecutive days and hours per shift.
- Raising lighting at night and keeping the environment cool increases alertness.
- Consider allowing naps where possible, although it clashes with traditional management in much of the world.

**Fixed post, environment, and sleeping on post:**

- The space of a fixed post can reduce vigilance. Often the design and location of the perimeter booth do not help seeing and hearing.
- Poor ventilation causes large changes in indoor temperature: unhealthy at worst and uncomfortable at best.
- Prolonged high temperatures can cause a serious loss of vigilance and drowsiness.
- The officer usually works alone and sometimes speaks only with the supervisor by telephone. Inactivity and isolation induce sleep even in the healthiest person: it is a natural process.
- There may be mitigating factors when an officer is found asleep. If discipline is warranted, the supervisor must act promptly and sensitively.
- In such cases a simple failure analysis checklist helps. The manager adapts it to achieve equitable discipline and to know what to change in the work area.

**Figure 1.3 — Checklist for analyzing performance failures: Sleeping on Post:**

1. Is the officer healthy?
2. Did the officer take any medication that causes drowsiness or reduces alertness?
3. How many hours did the officer sleep in the 24 hours before taking the post?
4. Were there shift schedule changes that affected the officer?
5. How many hours had the officer been awake before reporting for work?
6. How many hours had the officer been at the post before falling asleep?
7. What was the indoor temperature in the area when the officer was found?
8. What was the lighting level in the area when the officer was found?
9. Did the officer have active duties at the post or was there forced inactivity?
10. How was “sleeping on post” detected or reported?

- Even a well-designed system can cause failures if it ignores human behavior.
- Example: if several signals or response functions coincide, the officer gets confused and may make a serious error. Behavioral theory and practice confirm it.
- Passive, constant monitoring of video surveillance quickly reduces vigilance and can prevent detecting events.
- Corrections: simulate the worst case and create a drill response sequence to ensure an adequate response.
- Other measures: periodic patrols, movement or rotation; having the officer call the supervisor to check in; automated calls to confirm status; other strategies that vary the routine.

#### Behavioral theories (1.14.2)

Theories useful for motivating officers and keeping them engaged:

| Author | Theory |
| --- | --- |
| Abraham Maslow | Hierarchy of needs: each person is motivated according to the level they are at, starting with the most basic. A satisfied need no longer motivates, so giving more of the same may improve nothing. Recognition of the employee can be a powerful positive influence |
| Douglas McGregor | The supervisor treats subordinates according to contradictory assumptions. Theory X: the worker is lazy by nature and avoids responsibility. Theory Y: work is natural and the worker can be self-directed, motivated, and creative |
| Frederick Herzberg | Two-dimension model: hygiene or maintenance factors and satisfiers or motivators. Hygiene factors appeal to the person’s avoidance mechanism. Motivators make the work more challenging and lead to personal growth. The manager balances both and respects individual preferences |
| Chris Argyris | Traditional principles, structures, and procedures are incompatible with the employee’s mental health. They make the employee passive and submissive, with little control over the work. The employee then becomes apathetic, uses self-protective defenses, or fights the system |
| Warren Bennis | The organization can be an adaptive, innovative system that solves problems in highly changing environments. Future organizations will be more inclusive and will have five traits (below) |

**Maslow’s levels (from base to top):**

1. Physiological: survival, such as food, shelter, and sleep.
2. Safety and security: economic, physical, and mental well-being.
3. Social or affiliation: desire to be loved and accepted.
4. Ego: knowledge, independence, achievement, status, self-confidence, and the respect of others.
5. Self-actualization: inner drive to develop one’s own potential.

**Traits of the future organization according to Bennis:**

1. Integration: synthesize the employee’s needs and management’s objectives.
2. Social influence: distribute authority and power effectively.
3. Collaboration: manage and resolve conflicts.
4. Adaptation: respond well to the changes imposed by the environment.
5. Revitalization: handle the problems of growth and decay.

- The officer’s knowledge, competence, and personality must be matched to the assignment.
- One method is to consider whether the officer is introverted or extroverted. The introvert may adapt better to monotonous tasks; the extrovert likes to move around.

#### Work environment (1.14.3)

- Officers operate in highly varied and changing situations. They use automated systems: visitor management, geographic information systems (GIS), and communications.
- There are sedentary posts and others with long foot or vehicle patrols.
- All share environmental factors that may or may not be modifiable: space, heat, light, and noise.

**Mechanical environment:** control centers can be the core of one or several sites. Many workstations and equipment that monitor security and building systems make them very demanding.

| System | What the book says |
| --- | --- |
| Video surveillance systems | Extends the officer’s effectiveness, but watching monitors is passive, almost hypnotic. At the same time, observing 20 monitors can be very stressful. Always consider the ergonomics of the console |
| — System design | Include display and annunciation functions. Showing several cameras in sequence reduces the number of monitors and the hypnotic effect. A video motion detector alerts to changes in the image. Rotate personnel out of this environment: no one stays alert forever |
| — Artificial intelligence | Artificial intelligence and machine learning improve video analytics: object detection, image classification, visual tracking, and action recognition. Advanced learning creates behavior probability maps, for example to detect fights and count people (Guan 2018) |
| Access control systems | Symbol of technological advance. Connects with video surveillance and building management. Its design must favor surveillance: if there is no way to verify door alarms, officers soon learn to ignore them |
| Life safety systems | Poorly managed fire alarms quickly reduce vigilance. They raise legitimate and illegitimate alarms to unacceptable levels. Faced with false alarms, people learn to ignore danger. Managing them requires diligence |
| Other systems | Because it is on duty 24 hours, security ends up monitoring other departments’ systems by default or serving as after-hours backup. Examples: elevator monitoring, lighting control, inspection of mechanical areas, and heating, ventilation, and air conditioning |
| — Internet of things | With the Internet of things (IoT) systems interconnect and generate enormous volumes of data. Many organizations monitor them remotely or in central systems where officers assist |

- Without training, testing, and an assessment of whether personnel can take on the extra load, the officer feels overloaded and undervalued.
- Those two factors can lead to performance failures.

**Physical environment:**
