- When analyzing the operation, physical stress must be considered. Standards vary by assignment, but the factors in Figure 1.4 occur in almost all posts.
- The physical capacity of the uniformed officer is assessed with those standards. When hiring, the prior screening is fairer and more efficient with a detailed list of physical requirements.
- Before applying job enrichment, each post is analyzed and those with similar physical demands are grouped.
- Example: several fixed posts may share factors, while foot or vehicle patrols may be very different.
- Then each physical demand factor is analyzed according to the average exposure in an eight-hour shift or in an average week.
- If on one shift there is a lot of standing and on another a lot of walking in the same post, both factors can be noted with the maximum of eight hours.
- A complete analysis documents requirements based on the core content of the job. Without it, they could appear contrary to the anti-discrimination provisions of the Americans with Disabilities Act (ADA).
- Stress is reduced by modifying the post. Example: split patrols so the officer spends four hours at a fixed post and the rest patrolling.
- Other times it requires larger changes or improving the environment: reducing noise, heat, cold, wind, or dust.

**Figure 1.4 — Physical Demand Analysis of the officer post:**

| Physical or sensory factor | Value in the figure |
| --- | --- |
| Total hours on feet | 6 |
| Standing | 4 |
| Walking on level surfaces | 2 |
| Climbing stairs or ramps | 1 |
| Stooping or squatting | <1 |
| Back and hip flexion (30°–90°) | <1 |
| Elbow flexion (both arms) | 2 |
| Reaching above shoulders | 2 |
| Reaching below shoulders | 6 |
| Knee flexion | 2 |
| Lifting, carrying, pushing, or pulling 16 to 40 lb (7 to 18 kg) from the floor to four feet high | 1 |
| Fine manipulation (fingering: pinching or manual dexterity) | 4 |
| Gross manipulation, grasping, or hand movement | 4 |

- Figure note: the officer may have to help lift or move up to 200 lb (90 kg) if an employee becomes ill or injured in an emergency.
- Minimum requirements: binocular vision correctable to 20/30; speech without serious impediment; three-dimensional vision; hearing a voice at 20 feet (6 m) and a whisper at 10 feet (3 m); color vision.
- Common exposures: operating (motorized equipment and vehicles; hand-held power tools); walking (unprepared walking surfaces; obstacles); heights (ladders; elevated test platforms).
- More exposures: temperatures above and below normal, from indoor and outdoor patrols at any hour and in any weather; skin irritants (solvents; cleaning products); respiratory irritants (dust from surfaces and roads).
- Pending: in the extracted text the values column has no legible unit label (its header appears as “Proprietary/In-house security officers,” apparently a layout error). The figures follow the order of the text and appear to be hours per shift (6 on feet = 4 standing + 2 walking).

**Fixed or static posts:**

- Functions that maintain vigilance can be created: alarm test sequences, badge making, key control, and record keeping.
- **The most effective way to maintain vigilance is to rotate the officer between fixed post and patrol, on foot and mobile.**

**Foot patrols:**

- They are physically demanding and are sometimes seen as punishment or an unrewarding assignment.
- Typical failures: the skipped or half-done round. Causes: fatigue, or relying too much on electronic alarms and believing the area does not need a human patrol.
- Another failure: the officer does not know what is expected of them in an incident, or decides not to act according to policy.
- Every organization has many minor functions. The manager can broaden the officer’s role to non-security tasks if that increases interest and improves performance.

**Vehicular or mobile patrols:**

- They involve stopping, starting, and getting in and out of the vehicle frequently. Many injuries occur this way, from falls in the dark.
- Inside the vehicle, observation is very restricted and hearing is almost nullified.
- It is mitigated by using electric golf carts, bicycles, or other special vehicles.

**Human environment:**

- There is no magic selection formula, because of the great variety of functions. Someone who succeeds in one assignment may fail in the next if knowledge, competence, and personality are not matched.
- The face-to-face interview remains the most important. But more and more companies invest in personality inventories, assessments, and tests.
- With those assessments, the officer understands their strengths and weaknesses and learns to be a better learner, employee, and communicator.

**Myers-Briggs Type Indicator:** defines four pairs of mental preferences. According to the book:

| No. | Preference | Poles |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | How information is received | Sensing: clear, tangible information, tied to direct experience. Intuition: abstract, conceptual information, with a big-picture view and future possibilities |
| 2 | How one judges or decides | Thinking: objective, logical, and analytical, focused on tasks and results; in a dispute seeks clarity and objective principles. Feeling: global, visceral, and value-based, attentive to the impact on others; in a dispute seeks harmony and collaboration |
| 3 | Energy consciousness | Extroversion: energy from the outer world of people, things, and action; enjoys large groups; acts before reflecting. Introversion: energy from the inner world of ideas and concepts; prefers quiet activities; reflects before acting |
| 4 | Life management orientation | The book says “sensing versus intuition”: attending to the five senses and seeing the world as it is (practical, grounded, experience-based) versus interpreting and giving meaning (visionary or idealist, interested in the future or in timeless principles) |

**Exam note:** the book repeats “sensing versus intuition” as the fourth pair. In the real Myers-Briggs indicator, the fourth pair is judging vs. perceiving; sensing versus intuition is only the first.

**Big Five Model of Personality**, Lussier and Achua 2004, p. 30. It classifies traits into five dimensions; each dimension groups several traits:

| Dimension | Content |
| --- | --- |
| Surgency | Leadership and extroversion. Also called dominance: wanting to be in charge and advancing through competition and influence. Someone weak in surgency is a follower |
| Agreeableness | Ability to get along with others. Ranges from warm, calm, compassionate, friendly, and sociable to cold, difficult, uncompassionate, unfriendly, and unsociable |
| Adjustment | Emotional stability, from strong to weak. High: calm under pressure, relaxed, secure, and positive. Low: loss of control, poor handling of pressure, nervous or insecure |
| Conscientiousness | Achievement traits. Ranges from responsible and dependable to irresponsible and undependable. Also suggests credibility, conformity, organization, and willingness to work hard for goals |
| Openness to experience | Willingness to change and try new things |

- Personality inventories do not give definitive answers. They only indicate possible strengths and weaknesses.

**Personal communication, training, and supervision:**

- Officers depend on written instructions because they may not see a supervisor every day. Those instructions must be reinforced with personal communication.
- The supervisor organizes his or her schedule to have daily personal contact with the officers.
- If the officer's knowledge and competence are not constantly tested, it is impossible to ensure the protection of the facility.
- The inspecting supervisor asks about the details of the assignment and about the correct responses to incidents.
- People listen more when ideas touch their interests, desires, family, and work.
- Direct communication matters: with more intermediaries, the message is distorted, delayed, or misunderstood.
- What is used is better retained. If the supervisor demonstrates a task and the officer repeats it, recall lasts much longer than with verbal instructions alone.

**Recommendations for communication and training:**

1. Base training on needs: the supervisor detects gaps in knowledge and corrects them.
2. Recognize individual differences. They influence the pace of learning: intelligence, desire to learn, knowledge, aptitudes, interests, motives, attitudes, emotions, and learning capacity.
3. (Continuation of point 2) Set reasonable standards for everyone, knowing that not everyone performs equally. Not everyone learns the same thing at the same age; individualized instruction helps each person give his or her best at the time.
4. Maximize motivation: understand drives and desires; set meaningful standards together; show personal interest; give suitable incentives; set a good example; use praise, reward, and recognition; instruct clearly and completely.
5. Adopt the learner's point of view: put yourself in the learner's place and adapt the instruction. Asking learners what they feel they need to learn also gives good results.
6. Give timely feedback: everyone wants to know how they are doing. Systematic progress reports can greatly speed up learning.
7. Expect difficulties: learning is uneven; it rises quickly, plateaus, and rises again. Causes: motivation, effort, physiological conditions, or training method.
8. Follow up: repeat and summarize. Give the information and then recap what was said.
9. Learn to instruct: knowing how to do a job is not knowing how to teach it. It requires preparation, practice, and patience; knowing the principles of learning; mastering the job; applying the fundamentals of instruction.

Note: the book gives eight recommendations; here the second one is split into two points (2 and 3).

**Changes in the work environment:** many managers have problems when changing shifts. An officer who performed well for months or years on one shift may not adapt to the same post on another shift. Post transfers, even on the same shift, cause the same thing. The officer usually adapts in a few days.

**Interaction with other employees:** it must be facilitated on an ongoing basis. The officer may be seen as something negative because of being an authority figure. Minor, positive daily interactions reduce hostility and alienation.

**Improving observation capability:**

- The post must be designed to maximize observation of the protected area. Alarms and video surveillance extend the reach.
- The type of glass matters. Glare from the sun or artificial light is an important factor in fatigue, and a very common one.
- It gets worse at night: glare from artificial light greatly limits the field of observation.
- High-efficiency anti-glare coatings practically eliminate glare as an obstacle.

**Testing and post-testing:** competence in a skill is not maintained without random performance tests and feedback. They can be simple or complex.

**Figure 1.5 — Test/Post-Test Routine:**

| Phase | Step |
| --- | --- |
| Test 1 | An external source sends the test signal at random times, but at least once per shift |
| Test 2 | The sender records the exact time at which the signal is transmitted |
| Test 3 | The officer who receives it presses the acknowledge button, which records only the date and time of receipt |
| Test 4 | The officer calls a designated internal number and reports the test to a message recorder, using a drill checklist. Or calls the sender directly |
| Post-test 1 | The transmission time is compared with the reception time recorded on the alarm printer tape |
| Post-test 2 | The accuracy and clarity of the message recorded by the officer are reviewed |
| Post-test 3 | The accuracy and completeness of the drill checklist are reviewed |
| Post-test 4 | The results are discussed with the officer no later than his or her next scheduled shift |

**Analyzing job performance:**

- Performance evaluation is essential. Common criteria: task performance, general job conduct, and positive and negative traits related to performance.
- Recommended minimum: one formal individual evaluation per year and an informal review at six months. This way the employee knows how management sees him or her.
- Afterward: examine each post, identify frequent deficiencies and their causes. Correct first those with the greatest effect on performance.
- Only a well-identified skill deficiency is corrected or modified with additional training.

**Serious misconduct** that requires immediate attention:

1. Misconduct involving weapons.
2. Use of unreasonable force. Example from the book: physically restraining a youth because a garden hose is missing.
3. Conduct that takes the officer away from his or her post without justification.
4. Accusations of criminal conduct: fraud, theft, or searching a person who revoked consent.
5. Conduct that significantly interferes with the ability to perform duties.

- Among the most serious offenses: alcohol or drugs on duty. They are especially serious if the officer carries weapons.
- Leaving the post without adequate notice and without sufficient reason is serious misconduct. If the officer becomes ill and needs urgent care, he or she must first notify a supervisor.
- All of these offenses can be covered in a single policy statement: failure to comply with any of them will be treated as serious misconduct.
- That statement lays the groundwork for dismissal or other serious sanctions.
- Under common law, deliberate acts of employees, including officers, can be sued over as intentional torts.
- If a union represents the force and the contract has a just-cause discharge clause, a clear statement is needed to support severe sanctions.
- Appendix 1A is a model disciplinary policy (violations and consequences). Appendix 1B is a simplified version to verify that the officer knows the rules.

**Offsetting the effects of task simplification:**

- Reducing distractions made many posts too simple and monotonous. Officers need variety.
- The supervisor can assign brief, practical special tasks. Example: inspect all the locks in a building and report faults.
- Even on a fixed post there is room for tasks that keep vigilance up: alarm testing, badge making, and record keeping.
- If the manager covered each post for several shifts a year, he or she would detect and solve many of these problems early.
- Organizations change constantly and that affects the officer's duties. Interesting and rewarding posts must be created.
- Most officers respond well to a manager who communicates the value of their work to them.

### 16. Reinforcing Officers with Robotics (1.15)

- Every technology has pros and cons. The security professional must understand it and evaluate the risks and benefits of incorporating it into the program.

| Better for humans | Better for technology |
| --- | --- |
| Tasks that require deciding, perceiving, or changing course on the fly (pivot) | Monotonous, repetitive, simple tasks, in which humans get bored or become complacent |

**Definition:** robotics is the branch of technology that deals with the design, construction, operation, and application of robots. It includes:

- Unmanned vehicles:
  - UAV (Unmanned Aerial Vehicle), also called UAS (Unmanned Aerial System) or drone;
  - UGV (Unmanned Ground Vehicle);
  - USV (Unmanned Surface Vehicle, that is, waterborne).
- Robots: essentially a type of UGV that resembles a human (although this is not required).
- All of them can be remotely operated or autonomous (they guide themselves, without a pilot).
- Risk-benefit of the autonomous: the advantage of not requiring human intervention versus the risk of damage or injury if the autonomy is not perfect.
- The decision depends on each organization's risk appetite and on the perception of its stakeholders, for example whether they believe a human pilot raises or lowers the risk of an accident.

**General use cases for robotics:**

1. Patrolling the perimeter or outdoor areas of a campus (university, commercial, or government).
2. Static post (e.g., main entrance) that transmits video and monitors chemical, biological, radiological, nuclear, and explosive (CBRNE) materials.
3. Video patrols of rights-of-way, pipelines, or national borders.
4. Video monitoring of small areas: prison yards, parking lots, or substations.
5. Investigation and confirmation of CBRNE materials.
6. Mobile support to first responders.
7. Investigation and triage of alarms at remote locations.
8. Remote inspections of assets.

**Functions more common in robots than in other unmanned vehicles** (robots can also be remotely operated or autonomous and carry similar sensors):

1. License plate recognition.
2. Facial recognition.
3. Object detection and recognition.
4. Other video analytics (effective when the robot is not moving).

- Audio: it is common in robots, although not always live and two-way with a human, as in unmanned vehicles. They usually have prerecorded messages according to what they detect.
- Examples: if someone tries to lift it, it announces that it is reporting the problem and sends a human response. If it detects a chemical or biological release, it repeats a prerecorded warning.
- Common use: in controlled environments (inside the perimeter, often indoors) to complement officers.
- Their “cool factor” attracts more attention than a traditional officer. They are force multipliers that increase the visibility of security.
- It is so common that some robots are sold or rented by the hour to guard companies, matching their per-officer pricing model.

### 17. Conclusion (1.16)

- Security personnel are a vital component of almost every asset protection program.
- Their selection, training, and management determine the level of protection they can provide.
- Today more committed, better trained, and better qualified people are required than before. Getting the most out of them requires good management and comprehensive policies.
