## Capítulo 2 — Training the Security Officer (págs. 69–100)

**Central idea:** Training is the foundation of the officer's professional performance and must be continuous, from before the officer is assigned to a post and throughout his or her career. The chapter covers learning concepts, benefits, needs assessment, design, methods, evaluation, obstacles, low-cost strategies, officer roles, and future trends.

### 1. Introduction: Training and the Apprenticeship Model

- An employee cannot be expected to perform duties he or she was not trained for. Demanding professional performance requires ongoing training and development.
- Training and educating is an increasingly important and relatively new function of security departments.
- The complexity of the role demands extensive training in two phases: pre-assignment and on-the-job.
- U.S. police have long used apprenticeship programs: law enforcement explorers, cadets, community service, and internships. They serve for recruiting and as community outreach.

Four parallels between apprenticeship and security training:

| # | Parallel | Why it resembles apprenticeship |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | On-the-job training (OJT) | Abbreviated form: easy to structure; it only requires an instructor and the tools or equipment of the job. It must be documented to ensure that critical knowledge is transmitted. |
| 2 | Professional security certifications | Simplified form: in general they do not require a master, but they do require and document a certain level of experience. |
| 3 | Field training officers (FTO) | Common in the police: they mentor the new officer and assess whether he or she is suitable. Because they evaluate as well as teach, they require extensive planning and preparation by the instructor. |
| 4 | Formal training based on the apprenticeship model | Example: the British Security Industry Association (BSIA) helped create alarm installer apprentices. It is not for officers, but it shows how it could be done. Applicable to highly specialized officers, such as executive protection for celebrities or other VIPs. |

### 2. Key Training Concepts (2.1)

- Setting up a training program requires persistence, humility, empathy, and creativity.
- The key to success when designing and implementing training is to understand and apply learning concepts well. Whoever trains must keep studying teaching, training, and adult learning theory.
- A dedicated trainer position allows the security department to provide training to other departments:
  - Hospital: certified training for nursing staff in nonviolent crisis intervention or management of aggressive behavior.
  - Hotel: TIPS (Training for Intervention Procedures by Servers of Alcohol) certification for restaurant employees.
- Concepts in this section: cognitive, affective, and psychomotor learning; retention and transfer; socialization; and education, training, and development.

#### 2.1 Learning (2.1.1)

- **Definition:** a positive change in a person's knowledge, skill, ability, or outlook.
- Basic principle: there are many learning styles and each person learns something different. There are visual learners, readers, and active learners (who need to participate in order to learn).
- People learn differently depending on life and career stage. A veteran or supervisor sees things differently than a novice; older people “see” differently than younger ones.
- An officer with a graduate degree understands management theory better. One with only work experience adapts ideas to the practical better.
- Instructional design cannot be adjusted to each individual, but it must combine several means of delivering information. It must be applied systematically and continuously throughout the employment relationship.

Three domains of learning:

| Domain | What it is | Content and how it is achieved |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Cognitive | Knowledge-based: the intellectual part; amount of material learned and its theory. Key to solving problems: understanding a theory and applying it to practice. | Requires a complete explanation of the topic. Classroom or video lectures must be supported with active learning: questions for reflection, pretest, valid and demanding posttest, and instructor or supervisor guidance. |
| Affective | Attitudinal or perceptual: changes how the person sees a situation. | Understanding other cultures; dealing with people with disabilities; workplace safety compliance; adversaries' methods for obtaining information. Understanding loss and its effect on the organization; appreciating the adversary. Understanding aggressive behavior, the criminal mindset, and the speed of an assault (whoever does not understand it may become a victim). Essential in the face of terrorism, espionage, workplace safety, and relations with the various publics: customers, employees, vendors, police, emergency medical personnel, and hazardous materials (HAZMAT) teams. It must be integrated into the cognitive and the psychomotor. |
| Psychomotor | Physical or practical skills. | From equipment handling to defensive driving and emergency response. Requires repetition, practice, and refresher courses: regular defensive tactics and firearms, fire extinguishers every year, and first aid recertification. The person responsible must research the recognized refresher frequency for each skill and include it in the calendar. |

**Retention and transfer**

- **Retention:** the amount of what was learned that the learner keeps over time. It requires reiteration.
- The greatest retention is achieved when the learner **performs the task while listening to the instructor**.
- Continuous learning favors retention and transfer.
- **Transfer:** application on the job of the knowledge, skills, and abilities obtained in training. It is the relevance of what was learned to the work environment: it ensures that what is taught in the classroom is used.

#### 2.2 Socialization (2.1.2)

- **Definition:** the process by which the employee learns and adopts the values of the workplace.
- **Formal:** training is an important part (occupational or professional socialization); supervision is another key part. Management must convey a single, consistent message.
- **Informal:** it always occurs and usually has more influence than the formal. Negative socialization from the peer group must be watched.

#### 2.3 Education, Training, and Development (2.1.3)

Formula from the book: **Education + Training + Guided Experience = Development**.

- Supervisors and managers guide the officer's experience so that it is formative. That is why teaching, counseling, and coaching are crucial.
- The three concepts are distinct, related, and reinforce each other. All produce learning: a positive change in work behavior.

| Concept | Definition | Key features |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Education | Acquisition of knowledge and information that broadens perspective and appreciation (affective learning). | Concerned with the “why” more than the “how.” May not apply directly to the post. The more education, the easier it is to learn new things: an educated force trains more easily. It is the foundation of training in the cognitive and affective domains. The educated accept change better, read faster, write better, and solve problems better (the officer's greatest challenge). |
| Training | Acquisition of knowledge, skills, and abilities directly tied to performance; a formal process that facilitates learning. | Defined purpose: for example, operating detection equipment, conducting interviews, or monitoring alarms. Specific preparation with practice and repetition: it must be continuous. True training resembles indoctrination: it instills basic principles and changes behavior. Example: always using long pauses and paraverbal notes (“uh-huh”) in interviews makes the officer skilled; once the basics are mastered, one moves on to the advanced. |
| Development | Competence and professional growth of someone who already meets expectations: turning a good employee into an excellent one. | Culmination of education, training, and experience plus a strong dose of professional commitment. |

- “Experience is the best teacher” is not necessarily true. Experience must be guided with education and training; without them the employee learns the wrong lessons and repeats mistakes.
- Experience without education or training is **the most expensive teacher**.

What development contributes:

1. Adds new skills or competencies.
2. Sharpens or improves existing ones.
3. Improves communication.
4. Encourages applying knowledge or skills to the job.
5. Increases performance: the employee is more efficient and works smarter.
6. Prepares for a promotion.
7. Prepares the subordinate for new challenges.
8. Helps the officer or investigator solve problems.

- Possible challenges: new tasks within his or her category (investigations, special events, emergencies) or temporarily covering for a supervisor.
- Flexibility is critical: without cross-training, covering certain posts is a problem. Problem solving is a central part of the protection mission.

### 3. Benefits of Training (2.2)

Training costs money. Costs and benefits must be weighed to achieve the greatest return on investment. Six main benefits:

| Benefit | Content |
| --- | --- |
| Improved job performance (2.2.1) | True training makes officers competent; without it, competence cannot be expected. |
| Ease of supervision (2.2.2) | Well-trained officers are supervised more easily; managers devote their time to other matters instead of teaching the basics. Some experts hold that it is more effective to discipline with additional training than with punishment. |
| Procedure review (2.2.3) | The task analysis done before training is the same one needed to write procedures. McGlaughlin case (2003): after writing and implementing a program for operating a hospital's elevators, all problems with them ceased. That is why training is often located in the planning or research department. |
| Staff motivation (2.2.4) | It increases self-esteem and satisfaction. Knowing what is expected improves performance, and employees and visitors see them as professionals. A professional identity leads to more professional performance. |
| Reduced turnover (2.2.5) | See below. |
| Legal protection (2.2.6) | See below. |

**Exam note:** the initial list calls this benefit “revised procedures,” but the section is titled *procedure review*. It is the same benefit.

**Reduced turnover (2.2.5)**

- When properly applied, training reduces turnover: it lowers stress in the face of new challenges and shows management's interest in the officer.
- Knowing the environment, management's expectations, and problem-solving techniques reduces the newcomer's anxiety.
- Steps to retain the employee:
  1. Open communication to set realistic expectations (new and current employees).
  2. Proper orientation to the work environment.
  3. Training on the organization's products, services, culture, and expectations.
- Goodboe case (2002): Wackenhut Corporation program for supervisors to train their subordinates better. Three years later, turnover had dropped substantially.
- Social justice: development opportunities are open to everyone. Training provides recognition, something traditionally lacking in the industry. Pins, certificates, and rank promotions offer many occasions for recognition.

**Legal protection (2.2.6)**

- Failure to train can generate union complaints or negligence lawsuits.
- Documentation measures:
  - Document all training.
  - List of attendees and activities of each class.
  - Tests signed by the instructor and by the employee evaluated.
  - Centralized database of all officer training.
  - OJT checklists and evaluations that record the instruction given, because deficiencies usually come from training and supervision failures at the same time.
- Documenting each step ensures accountability. Repeating training undermines allegations that there was no frequent or recent training.

### 4. Identifying Training Needs (2.3)

Before any instruction, needs must be identified. Sources, in the book's order:

1. **Legal requirements (first step):** imposed by government or by contract. Examples: government contracts that require standards such as those of the Canadian General Standards Board; client contracts; union agreements; insurer requirements.
2. **Industry standards, guidelines, and best practices:** they set parameters. Example: the ASIS guideline *Private Security Officer Selection and Training* suggests topics.
3. **Community standards:** what training similar facilities in the area provide (hotels, universities). Reviewing their schedules reveals one's own gaps, innovations, and resources.
4. **Performance deficiencies:** in after-action reports or in performance evaluations. Training must prepare for routine and emergency tasks.
5. **The officers themselves:** they identify and rank the topics they need. It is a form of job task analysis and can reveal unknown needs. It validates their self-esteem and encourages their participation, something vital if the training alters schedules or challenges their abilities.

### 5. Program Assessment and Design (2.4)

Before designing, developing, and evaluating the program, four questions must be answered:

| Question | The book's criterion |
| --- | --- |
| Is it needed to solve a current performance problem? | Only if there is a **documented** deficiency in knowledge, skill, or ability. Using it for problems unrelated to that (poor job design, inadequate equipment, low morale) is inappropriate and wastes funds. |
| How will it be funded? | Identify and quantify each source. Also calculate the total cost to the company of **not** training: in the long run it can exceed the short-term expense. Saying: “If you think education is expensive, try ignorance.” |
| When must it be completed? | If immediate: off-the-shelf program or outside trainer. If the program already exists or can be developed within **two years**: in-house instructors. Rushing is problematic: runaway costs and clashes with the routines of trainers and learners. Best is an established training structure, with a person in charge who tracks state and insurer requirements, which change often. |
| Who needs training? | Distinguish basic pre-assignment training from topics for veterans, and tailor the content. Investing in pre-assignment training can yield more loyal and competent employees. Everyone needs to be updated from time to time. |

Forcing people to train in order to keep their jobs should be avoided (Stroberger 2000). Three reasons:

1. It contradicts the “principles of andragogy” of **Malcolm Knowles**: a theory of adult learning that assumes the adult is self-directed and takes responsibility for his or her learning needs.
2. It detracts from the program's credibility with those who receive it; management, as provider, may also lose credibility.
3. Requirements, especially governmental ones, only set minimums. Meeting them does not guarantee reasonable professional competence.

**Exam note:** internal tension. Here mandatory training to keep a job is discouraged, but in 2.7.1 it is proposed that outside courses be a “condition of employment.” If asked about mandatory training, the book's position is to avoid it (Knowles, Stroberger 2000).

#### 5.1 Behavioral Objectives (2.4.1)

- They are written based on the need detected.
- Each objective describes three elements: the desired **task**, the **conditions** for performing it, and the **criteria** for acceptable performance.
- They guide the development of instruction and tests; they are more effective if written with the help of a consultant.
- At first they can be simple (“conduct vehicle checks,” “answer the telephone”). In design they become very specific.
- Example: “The learner will identify the contraband items shown in photographs of the facility with 100 percent accuracy.” Task: identify. Conditions: photographs. Criterion: 100 percent accuracy.

#### 5.2 Instructional Design (2.4.2)

- Creating modules takes time and expertise. Keys: knowing how to design the instruction and then preparing tests and documentation.
- Options: appoint an employee to oversee training, hire an outside consultant, buy programs, or subcontract instructors.

| Option | Advantages | Disadvantages or conditions |
| --- | --- | --- |
| In-house staff | Knows the organization. | May lack technical expertise to design training. As an instructor may have a narrow perspective and ignore new research or court rulings. |
| Outside consultant | Experience in training and a network of professionals to turn to. | Does not know the organization in depth. |
| Off-the-shelf programs | Sensible if time, expertise, or money are lacking. | Only if they cover the organization's needs. |
| Subcontracted instructors | May be necessary when starting a program. | Careful selection (see below). |

- Few master all the phases of developing a program: a **training team** is usually needed.
- Selecting instructors (they greatly influence the officer's socialization):
  - Assess their qualifications to teach: education, training, experience, and certifications.
  - Explore their view of the officer's role. Those from the police may not understand officer roles, and some are condescending toward learners.
  - Beware of **Certified Trainer Syndrome**: the instructor claims to be certified in a subject but has no basis for teaching it.
  - Instructors must advocate for their learners and be well prepared for that role.

#### 5.3 Evaluating Training (2.4.3)

- Training is expensive and takes time: it must be evaluated closely.
- **Learning contact:** the number or proportion of employees exposed to the training. A program in which not all security staff participate has limited value, perhaps doubtful.

| Tool | Use | Limits or requirements |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Participant evaluations | Always use them: they measure how the training is perceived and give feedback to the instructor. | Poor for validation: they do not measure actual learning, but what the learner believes he or she learned. A form that requires describing what was learned and how to apply it is better: it measures transfer. |
| Tests | As objective as possible: the answer is either correct or not. | Essays and multiple choice give more information, but must be designed carefully and graded objectively. In test design it is wise to consult outside experts. Off-the-shelf programs usually include tests (they save time and money), but you must verify that they are valid and demanding. |
| Rating forms | The evaluator indicates whether or not the learner performed a specific task. Easy to create. | The task must be clear to everyone and there must be room for narrative comments. |
| Drills and scenarios | Final exam for the entire force or for each officer. They motivate in periods of low activity and teach problem solving. | They must be safe and valid, and evaluate only training already received. |
| Incident review | After a crisis, how the officers and the organization responded is assessed. Policies and procedures are analyzed and equipment and training are tested. | If the response was inadequate, revised training may be required. |

### 6. Training Methodology (2.5)

- The choice of method depends on: schedules, available technology, the instructor's comfort, and the number of people to be trained.
- Options listed by the book: lectures, job aids, distance learning, on-the-job training, and mentoring.

**Exam note:** the initial list in 2.5 gives five methods, but the section develops six: it adds the case study (2.5.2).

| Method | What it is and when it works | Advantages | Limits and tips |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Lecture (2.5.1) | Traditional method for a group to receive a common body of knowledge. Works to introduce a topic and set direction; follow-up is done with role-playing, video, and physical skills. | Ensures common knowledge. | Not suited to individualized instruction. Ineffective unless short and dynamic. After lunch: have them drink water before sitting down, slightly brighter light, and get them moving. Use visual aids (flip charts, overhead projector, white and black boards, digital slides, videos) and change the lighting when projecting or using a flip chart. Fill-in-the-blank handout: key words are removed from the lesson plan and the learner writes them in (on paper or online). |
| Case study (2.5.2) | For topics that require discretionary judgment: crisis management, use of force, and ethics. | Brings the class to life: the class presents the facts and then discusses them; video shortens the presentation of the facts. | Works best in groups with active discussion. It also works for individual tutoring, one-on-one instruction, and online courses. |
| Job aids (2.5.3) | Reminders of how to do a task; also called takeaways. They can be detailed (an equipment operator's manual) or simple (laminated cards with key points for answering the telephone). | Ensure that the procedures taught are followed. | They are a complement, **not a substitute**, for training. |
| Distance learning or distance education (2.5.4) | Learner and teacher in different places but in communication. Formats: from the correspondence course to computer-based instruction. | If individualized instruction is needed, it is probably the **only** means. Certifications use it (individual study and exam); even a university research paper counts. Security service providers use it to deliver uniform training at many sites. | Some trainers reject it. But whoever does not use all techniques, including this one, almost certainly will not train all subordinates well. |
| On-the-job training (OJT) (2.5.5) | Easy to develop. Very useful to ensure transfer from instructor to learner. | Works with specified procedures and a competent coach. The written part can be a list of key topics with room for comments. | If not well structured, its effectiveness is questionable. |
| Mentoring (2.5.6) | See next table. | Reinforces socialization. | — |

**Mentoring versus the field training officer (2.5.6)**

- Many current officers are management representatives. They must know management's philosophy and the corporate culture: they must be highly socialized.
- Mentoring and FTO programs reinforce that socialization.

| Aspect | Mentoring | FTO (field training officer) program |
| --- | --- | --- |
| How it works | After pre-assignment training, the new officer works with a mentor who coaches him or her on the post. It joins the classroom with the reality of the job. | The mentor is also a supervisor and evaluates the rookie's performance. |
| Does it evaluate? | **No:** it teaches but does not evaluate. | **Yes:** at the end the FTO recommends retaining or dismissing the employee. |
| Complexity | Lower. | Complex role: requires extensive planning and training. |
| Where it works best | In most security environments. Mentors can receive a raise and a title such as lead protection officer or senior security officer. | Common in the police. |

### 7. The Training Process (2.6)

- Training is a process of structured sessions that support each other; each is based on the previous one.
- Ideal: lifelong learning, constant throughout the employment relationship.
- Design a comprehensive process, not isolated episodes that get cut from the budget or that the next security director replaces.
- Method: divide the process into segments. Each topic has an **introduction, body, and closing (summary and test)**.

| Segment | Example from the book |
| --- | --- |
| Introduction | At orientation the topics are presented: patrol duties, fixed posts, executive protection, vehicle security, and fire prevention. |
| Body | OJT, study of the Certified Protection Officer (CPO) program of the International Foundation for Protection Officers (IFPO), web programs, or in-house courses. |
| Closing | Supervisor sign-off on an OJT form, commercial exam (such as those for the CPO), or a course's own exams. |

- There must be some redundancy and a learning sequence.
- Review whether the current training for each topic has sufficient introduction, body, and closing.

### 8. Obstacles to Training (2.7)

Launching a program blindly usually derails it, and officers are left without training. The five most common obstacles: **budget limitations, schedules, management's lack of training expertise, prejudice, and ego**.

#### 8.1 Budget Limitations (2.7.1)

Costs that must be identified: pay of those being trained, overtime for replacements, instructor fees, teaching aids and equipment, purchase of materials, and administrative development.

Seven ways to stretch the budget:

1. **Buy off-the-shelf programs:** much cheaper than custom development. They must be relevant, not perfect, especially if the alternative is no training or slow development. They can be supplemented with in-house material.
2. **Subcontract to a community college or technical institute:** can be cheaper than training in-house. It can be a condition of employment or part of a lateral promotion program.
3. **Seek grants:** state and federal programs fund job training.
4. **Join professional organizations:** International Law Enforcement Educators and Trainers Association (ILEETA), ASIS International, and IFPO give discounts to their members. They provide access to instructors and materials that are difficult and expensive to replicate.
5. **Share materials:** with other sites or neighboring facilities. Borrow a video from a school or university; buying one and lending it to a school brings good publicity and goodwill.
6. **Use distance learning:** reduces classroom and instructor time and cost.
7. **Turn to in-house trainers:** only if they are truly qualified and experienced.

#### 8.2 Schedules (2.7.2)

In 24/7 operations each class must be repeated many times. The most useful are distance education or self-study. Options:

| Option | Description | Advantages and notes |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Pass-along media | CDs, DVDs, videotapes, audiotapes, or other audio. They are viewed or listened to whenever convenient and then questions or exercises are answered. | Easy and cheap, especially for small organizations or groups preparing for a certification. |
| Online learning | Synchronous (instructor and learner in real time) or asynchronous (without immediate feedback). Formal (company, institute, association) or informal (articles, short awareness videos, social media). | Deployed at many sites with a uniform experience. Can be commercial or in-house (if there is a lot of staff). An organizational learning needs assessment helps decide. |
| Written procedures | They are handed out and their content is then tested. | Cheap, tailored to the facility, and easy to administer. Requires some human interaction to ensure correct interpretation. |
| Pass-along logs | Procedures, instructions, or articles that pass from one person to another: by mail, web, or a document with a routing sheet. | Cheap; keeps learning alive; reminds and reiterates important information according to current needs. |
| Distance learning | Online programs from ASIS International or universities. | Used mainly to train supervisors or managers; they also serve investigators. |
| Certifications | See list below. | Based on distance or home study, which minimizes scheduling problems. |

Certifications cited:

- Associate Protection Professional (APP) and Physical Security Professional (PSP®), from ASIS International.
- Certified Protection Officer (CPO), from IFPO.
- Certified Institutional Protection Specialist (CIPS), from the International Foundation for Cultural Property Protection.
- Tiered specialties: International Association for Healthcare Security and Safety (IAHSS) and the American Hotel and Lodging Association.
- For the employee: a great development opportunity. For the employer: a recognized educational standard, a benchmark, and a promotional tool. They add an international character in a global economy.

#### 8.3 Management's Lack of Training Expertise (2.7.3)

- Most managers can teach some topics, but without preparation in design and delivery they struggle.
- Often outside their expertise: assessing needs, writing instructional objectives, developing materials, and designing tests.
- They must learn the whole process or hire someone who knows it. Even knowing it, they often lack time. It is crucial that they self-assess their strengths and limits.
- Instructor certification: **Certified Protection Officer Instructor (CPOI)**, from IFPO. Those who are already CPOs and have teaching experience can apply.

Designing a program is a group effort with at least **three prongs**:

1. **Protection managers:** guide and initiate the process; they probably also teach.
2. **Designated staff** as training director, coordinator, or officer: handles the details and links management, supervision, line officers, and outside resources.
3. **Outside resource** (almost certainly needed): off-the-shelf program, contract with a nearby institution, or consultant. The consultant brings an objective perspective, resources, and current research that can save money.

- Consultants can also teach highly specialized topics, such as violence or crisis management.
- It may be worth paying more on high-risk topics with liability exposure. The contracted instructor must be accredited, have real experience, and be able to testify effectively if needed.

**Stereotypes (prejudice)**

- Society tends to view the officer negatively; the media show him or her as lazy and ignorant. Managers may believe it.
- The slightest sign of prejudice when teaching can derail a program. The manager must review his or her own feelings toward subordinates.

**Ego**

- **Frog syndrome** (IFPO 2015): the manager decides to personally train everyone, jumps into the project, and, on seeing the real workload, jumps out. The training is left incomplete, stops, and organizational development stalls.

**Exam note:** the list in 2.7 says “prejudice,” but the section is titled *Stereotypes*. In addition, stereotypes and ego appear unnumbered, within 2.7.3.

### 9. Training Strategies (2.8)

Cost-effective strategies listed by the book (12): off-duty training, tuition reimbursement, recognition programs, integrated training, videos, selling security services, supervisory training, internships, previous experience, officer selection, job rotation, and professional memberships.

**Exam note:** the list names 12 strategies, but the text develops only nine sections (2.8.1–2.8.9). “Previous experience,” “officer selection,” “job rotation,” and “professional memberships” are not developed. Instead, “formal and informal efforts” (2.8.9) appears, which was not in the list.

#### 9.1 Off-Duty Training (2.8.1)

- **Voluntary** off-hours training has great individual and organizational potential: it motivates learning new topics.
- Mandatory training inspires less and seems repetitive if it covers what is already mastered.
- It allows the most motivated to stand out and generates positive peer pressure from peers who see the benefits.
- Example: Saturday seminars on executive protection, counterterrorism, and defense against multiple attackers. Providing food and a certificate creates interest and eliminates the payment of overtime.
- Reduced or no fees if associated with high school protective services programs. In return, the company offers: guest speaker, job shadowing for students, or visits to the facility.

#### 9.2 Tuition Reimbursement (2.8.2)

- Programs such as emergency medical technician, state firearms qualification, and CPO are paid for from that budget (not from the security training budget).
- Small cost compared with great professional growth. If the employee trains on regular pay and without a replacement on overtime, the main cost is avoided.
- These milestones can be a criterion for promotion to supervisor.

#### 9.3 Recognition Programs (2.8.3)

- They reinforce the officer's value. They must be conceived to project the department's right image: customer service, enforcement of rules, or emergency response.
- Examples: uniform patch for advanced first aid; CPO pin; plaques with photo and achievements in visible places; online certificates or plaques; company newsletter; dinners or lunches for training milestones.
- **Lateral promotion or “promotion-in-place”:** same level, but with a raise, change of rank or title, or change of duties. It creates specialties: workplace safety specialists, investigators, crime prevention coordinators, and FTOs. It recognizes achievements, gives a goal, and structures the career.
- **Bonuses:** one-time reward that controls salary costs and reinforces behaviors. Examples: for advanced first aid, CPO, certification to carry weapons, or another relevant certification. It creates another level of expertise in the force.

#### 9.4 Integrated Training (2.8.4)

- Include officers in the training of the parent company or client: customer service, communication, writing, and other topics. It is cost-effective and takes advantage of the computer-assisted instruction of companies and governments.
- It helps socialization: it broadens perspectives and focuses the department on what services to provide and how to provide them.
- It builds trust, gives the department visibility, and increases employees' security awareness.
- Someone who sees something unusual trusts an officer he or she knows more. It is an example of community policing that helps in investigation and intelligence.

#### 9.5 Video Collaborations (2.8.5)

- In-house videos, if production time can be included in the calendar. Possible help: a university's theater department or a community theater group.
- Collaborating with police, fire, and other crisis management partners allows sharing costs and resources.
- Printing, computing resources, and offices of the parent company or client are also shared. Sharing creatively reduces costs.

#### 9.6 Selling Security Services (2.8.6)

- Some departments sell their training to other entities and fund their own with the proceeds.
- York College of Pennsylvania offered short seminars (for example, report writing) and reserved seats for its own protection staff.
- Some contract security companies created institutes that train clients and their staff.
- Cautions: attend to legal liability on certain topics; contractors must not end up training their competition.

#### 9.7 Supervisory Training (2.8.7)

- It reinforces the officer's role as a management representative.
- They are not supervisors, but they lead in routine tasks and emergencies. It is advisable to train them as adjunct members of the management team.

#### 9.8 Internships (2.8.8)

- They expose the student to line and management functions. Interns can recruit among their peers and research to create instruction.
- They are a recruiting tool: they make it possible to hire an officer with a college education, trained and familiar with the environment. They will grow, because universities emphasize them.
- They also attract older workers changing careers. Postsecondary-level ones should have a management focus.
- To recruit entry-level officers: externships from high school protective services programs. They are shorter: the student accompanies a professional for a day or so.
- They should be seen as mentoring and educating the newcomer in the work environment.

#### 9.9 Formal and Informal Efforts (2.8.9)

- Professional alliances of officers with a common employer or area: from informal meetings to international organizations such as IFPO, which brings together line officers.
- Guest-expert lectures, for example at monthly training meetings: police (suicide bombers, hostages, how to testify), lawyers (legal topics), and professors (interpersonal communication and group behavior).
- **Wackenhut case:** founded in **1954**; it created the Wackenhut Institute, which became the G4S North American Training Institute. It trains in-house staff and the proprietary forces of other organizations; some courses give college credit. It teaches treating training as a core corporate function, not an expense: it improved efficiency, generated revenue, and commercialized services.

### 10. Roles of the Security Officer (2.9)

- How the company views the protection of people, property, and information (fundamental security responsibility) defines how the department is trained. Examples: hospital versus manufacturing plant; nuclear plant versus shopping mall.
- The officer's functions are in chapter 1. Two officers can have the same functions and a different role.
- Defining the role at each site allows the training schedule to cover all phases of the job.

Five roles:

| Role | What it involves | Training needed |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Management representative (2.9.1) | Management's agent in public relations with visitors, customers, tenants, and vendors. | Know the expectations and corporate culture; that way they become adjunct members of the management team. |
| Intelligence agent (2.9.2) | Gathers information for management about criminal or adversary activities. Discovers and reports rule violations by employees or tenants. Patrols look for conditions that cause losses. Assesses fire and safety risks. Grows in the face of organized crime (narcotics, shoplifting rings, prostitution, gambling, gangs) and terrorism (prior surveillance by attackers). | Investigation fundamentals, interview techniques, and investigative report writing. Recording and reporting observations. Evaluating conditions to decide whether to correct immediately or report. |
| Enforcement/compliance agent (2.9.3) | Ensures that management's rules are followed. Resembles the police, but within the legal and cultural framework of the environment. Sometimes enforces laws that are also internal rules, especially if a commissioned police officer. | Know the rules and their procedure; human interaction to achieve efficient compliance; reduce and resolve conflicts. Investigative aspect: document and testify about violations (combines intelligence and compliance). |
| Legal consultant (2.9.4) | Understand legal and quasi-legal (disciplinary) matters of the environment. | Torts, negligence, and contracts. Notions of criminal procedure: it may not bind the officer, but it does bind the police, and improves the police-security relationship. Special rules for minors. Administrative or regulatory laws. Industry standards (different from law, same compliance principle). Audits by government, insurers, and professional organizations. Labor laws: privacy, disciplinary processes, and union contracts. |
| Physical security or crime prevention specialist (2.9.5) | Physical security is the heart of the discipline. The role grows because of technological complexity. | Technical training on new equipment, which can last a lifetime. The ASIS PSP® certification helps define physical security and marks out a possible career. Central function: improve policies, procedures, and functions, not just follow them; bring perspective to management. |

### 11. Future Trends in Training (2.10)

- Attacks in Oklahoma City, New York, Madrid, Beslan, Mumbai, London, Paris, and other places reoriented the agenda: counterterrorism training is the rule, not the exception.
- New topics: response to protests, active assailant incidents, red flags of potential attackers, and social media monitoring.
- Training must evolve with the job: if the officer uses an access control system or operates a robot, he or she must understand how it works.
- Problem: training is usually reactive, although the officer must be proactive. Three factors will shape the future: government oversight, self-regulation, and new ways of delivery.

#### 11.1 Changing Roles (2.10.1)

- The greater the social complexity, the greater the officer's importance and the more training. Human service skills will gain weight.
- Examples: community protection in high-crime housing; traditional police functions because of the police's budget limits.
- Public-private partnerships: crime prevention, supply chain, critical infrastructure, and borders.
- Demographic changes and suspicions about certain groups create tension: interpersonal and multicultural skills are needed. This is aggravated by long, complex supply chains and the search for oil and natural gas.
- Better communication, legal knowledge, and mastery of technological devices and weapons will be demanded. For this, the traditional classroom and role-playing will be needed.
- A professional career improves recruitment, motivation, and retention. It is advisable to train in supervision, management, and business principles. The ASIS APP certification is another route.

#### 11.2 Government Oversight (2.10.2)

- Few governments legislate officer training. They often regulate only contracted providers, and a large part of the industry remains unregulated.
- Government standards often cover only licensing and screening, not training. Even so, they are hard to enforce for operational, economic, and political reasons.

#### 11.3 Self-Regulation (2.10.3)

- Standards and guidelines of professional organizations will gain weight.
- Europe: the Confederation of European Security Suppliers and the BSIA push for better training.
- North America: the International Association of Security and Investigative Regulators (IASIR) facilitates the logistics of regulation. It collaborates with ASIS and with NASCO (National Association of Security Companies) on officer training.
- The ASIS guideline *Private Security Officer Selection and Training* will probably be adopted by companies, providers, and professional organizations.
- Outside the sector: the Joint Commission on the Accreditation of Healthcare Organizations (JCAHO) expects security staff to participate in continuing education and development. The IAHSS supports this with training briefs for short sessions.
- Second problem: quality control. Service companies must lead; some already exceed government standards.
- Unions: sometimes they ask for more training. The Service Employees International Union (SEIU) seeks to improve training, raise standards, and create career opportunities.

**Exam note:** the correct name is Confederation of European Security **Services** (CoESS), not “Suppliers.” And the JCAHO has been called The Joint Commission since 2007; it recognizes both names.

#### 11.4 Delivery of Training (2.10.4)

- Training is more complex and specialized as security becomes international. More automated external defibrillators, computers, detection equipment, robots, surveillance systems, and integrated access controls are used. Evacuation and terrorism complicate the guidelines.
- Common problem: only one or two people need a topic. In-house classes are not cost-effective, except in large organizations with their own instructors; sending them outside is best.
- Organizations that train and certify individual officers will gain weight: handcuffs, pepper spray, aggression management, or interviews. The officer is sent outside or a trainer comes in.
- A credible outside certification gives high quality assurance and avoids developing courses. Outside experts will always be used in some way.
- Contractors with their own programs will open their classes to outsiders: they make money, train their staff, and show their capabilities (marketing).
- Collaborations: active threat training with tactical intervention brings together all the entities that would interact. It controls costs, improves relationships, tests the response, and gives tactical training to security staff, who are on site and in command of the environment.
- Security academies with highly interactive learning, also at a distance. In their favor: small security staffs (a small organization, or a large one with few officers or few per site). There will be a niche for whoever enrolls one or two people quickly.
- E-learning is growing: it is offered by high schools, universities, institutes, and the public sector. The learner, his or her manager, or his or her mentor choose courses (asset protection in retail, executive protection, physical security).
- Conclusion: quality training, internal and external, will remain at the center of all effective security management.

### Key Terms

pre-assignment training · on-the-job training (OJT) · apprenticeship · field training officer (FTO) · learning · cognitive learning · affective learning · psychomotor learning · retention · transfer · socialization · education · training · development · guided experience · cross-training · improved job performance · ease of supervision · procedure review · staff motivation · reduced turnover · legal protection · after-action report · job task analysis · principles of andragogy (Malcolm Knowles) · behavioral objectives · off-the-shelf program · Certified Trainer Syndrome · learning contact · participant evaluations · rating forms · drills and scenarios · incident review · lecture · case study · job aids (takeaways) · distance learning · mentoring · lifelong learning · introduction, body and closure · pass-along media · pass-along logs · synchronous delivery · asynchronous delivery · three-pronged effort · stereotypes · frog syndrome · off-duty training · tuition reimbursement · recognition programs · promotion-in-place · bonuses · integrated training · internship · externship · management representative · intelligence agent · enforcement/compliance agent · legal consultant · physical security or crime prevention specialist · active assailant · red flags · Certified Protection Officer (CPO) · Certified Protection Officer Instructor (CPOI) · Associate Protection Professional (APP) · Physical Security Professional (PSP) · Certified Institutional Protection Specialist (CIPS) · TIPS

## Capítulo 3 — The Security Services Contract (págs. 101–120)

**Central idea:** To hire outside guards, the client defines in writing what it needs (request for proposals, scope, orders) and the agency responds with a detailed operating agreement. Bids are compared using a standardized point method. Afterward, the contract is negotiated, awarded, and administered with ongoing controls.

### 1. Context: Types of Force and Service Environments (introduction)

- Three force models: proprietary staff only, mixed, or contract officers only.
- Besides protecting facilities, contract companies can help with recovery after crises, personnel recruitment, and transport of patients or employees. The book says they offer many more, without listing them.
- Officers are also deployed in areas with civil unrest and terrorism.
- Typical clients: governments, corporations, small businesses, residential communities, and individuals.

| Environment | Feature | Examples from the book |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Public areas | Unrestricted access | Residential areas; parades and street festivals |
| Semi-public areas | Some access restriction or admission charge | Stores, banks, hospitals, sporting and entertainment events, museums |
| Private facilities and property | Entry limited to certain people | Manufacturing plants, warehouses, offices |

- The client must specify the environment and requirements in an express contract with defined terms and conditions.
- An implied contract, inferred from the conduct of the parties, is not advisable.
- Contract versus warranty: the contract is between specific parties.
- The contractor's experience and the skills of its staff are not interchangeable from one application to another.
- Choosing the right contractor takes a lot of time; it should not be repeated unnecessarily.

### 2. Client Responsibilities (3.1)

**Definition — security services contract:** a legally binding agreement between at least two organizations. It details the expectations, responsibilities, and obligations of both parties.

- Small, informal, simple organizations have small, informal, simple processes. Large, formal, complex ones have large, formal, complex processes. That is why drafting must be done carefully.
- First step for the security manager: learn the contracting rules of his or her own organization.
- Purpura (2018) guidelines for hiring officers:
  - The buyer must beware.
  - Assess well the needs of the organization to be protected.
  - Get informed and know the state of the art.
  - Analyze advantages and disadvantages of each service.
  - Avoid decisions made out of panic.

#### 2.1 Request for Proposal (RFP) (3.1.1)

- Personnel costs are usually the largest item in a security department's operating budget.
- Before hiring, needs must be analyzed, especially the relationship between staff and electronic security solutions.
- That analysis is key to preparing the RFP, negotiating the contract, and administering it afterward.
- The needs must clearly justify the use of security officers.
- Three broad criteria for choosing a contractor:
  - Consistent performance.
  - Quick, efficient, and positive response to the client's concerns.
  - Competitive pricing.
- Then the scope of work is defined based on: site examination, facility layout, building design, and internal business processes.
- Factors that are also considered:
  - Corporate culture: how much it tolerates reinforced security measures and to what degree.
  - Security objective: maintain an image of security or create an effective asset protection force? They are different things.
  - Contract duration.
- From this analysis come the scope of work and a personnel schedule. The schedule translates into the total hours of the contract.

**Solicitation summary** (McCrie 2015). It is the next useful step. It gives providers the maximum information so they can put together complete proposals. It contains:

- Statement of purpose with the tasks the security staff will perform.
- Client contact person.
- Details of the bidders' conference.
- Letter of intent for providers.
- Proposal submission requirements.
- Modification or withdrawal of proposals.
- Post-opening withdrawals.
- Late proposals, withdrawals, or modifications.
- Right of appeal of bidders.
- Payment policies.
- Amendments to the RFP.
- Details of any planned discussion between provider and client.
- Procurement rules.
- Challenges based on unfairness.

**The RFP** is distributed after the summary and details the requested services in depth. Suggested headings:

- Nature of the security services required.
- Contract duration.
- Client background.
- Specific facilities requiring protection.
- Security problems.
- Services needed at the facility.
- Scope of services.
- Specific tasks and minimum requirements.
- Guidelines for officers.
- Incident report requirements.
- Provider responsibilities:
  - Continuity of service.
  - Possible provision of male and female guards.
  - Uniforms and equipment.
- Proposal content and how certain tasks will be handled.
- General information:
  - Conflicts.
  - Inspectors and tests.
  - Supervision by the contractor.
  - Adequate and competent supervision.
- Additional requirements:
  - Policies.
  - Tour limitation rules.
  - Overtime payment policies.
  - Training agreement.
  - Right to audit.
- Staff standards:
  - Education and background requirements.
  - Hiring and replacement of personnel.
  - Information about the security service provider.
- General guidelines for submitting proposals:
  - Letter of intent.
  - Deadline for requests.
  - Questions.
  - Proposals or declinations.
  - Restriction on contact with client employees.
  - Press releases.
  - Proprietary information.
  - Contract award.
  - Reservation.
  - Site visit.
  - Oral presentations.
  - Incurring costs.
- Provider selection:
  - Selection method:
    - Organizational capability and quality.
    - Understanding of the client's need.
    - Recruitment strategy and planning.
    - Qualifications and experience of the proposer's personnel.
    - Training: plans, curriculum, and capabilities.
    - Supervisory control.
  - Rating system.
  - Payment.
  - Liquidated damages/adjustment of compensation.
  - General provisions.
  - Incomplete shift penalty.
  - Improper assignment penalty.
  - Return of a previously dismissed worker.
  - On-the-job negligence.
  - Notification requirements.
  - General provisions for the contractor.
  - Delivery of the federal or other government employer identification number.
- Insurance.
- Reports.
- Responsibilities of the prime contractor.
- Subcontracting.
- Reservations.
- Contract duration.
- Contract termination.
- Equal employment opportunity.

Making an RFP takes a lot of work, but it is necessary. If the manager does not detail what he or she needs, the agency chosen will probably not meet his or her expectations.

#### 2.2 Scope of Work (3.1.2)

- The client defines the scope and performance standards completely and precisely. Without that precision, disputes soon arise over what performance is acceptable.
- Calling contractors at random can produce enthusiastic answers that do not reflect the actual quality of service.
- The client must be realistic about the risks, based on a recent risk assessment.
- The more specific the information, the more likely a quality service at a competitive price.
- The scope states: security tasks, days and hours of service, and total number of hours. It is given to all potential contractors. (Appendix 3A: example.)
- With the scope, the general and special orders, and the proposed operating agreement, agencies should be able to deduce all typical and routine requirements and foresee the administrative burden.
- Much more information is conveyed in formal and informal conferences. Contractors should be encouraged to ask for all the detail they need.

#### 2.3 General and Special Orders (3.1.3)

- It is crucial to prepare them in advance and in detail. Special instructions are also needed for each assignment or post.
- Providing them is the client's responsibility; in practice, the agency often writes them.
- Whoever prepares them, the client always reviews and approves them.
- Risk of the agency's orders: they may be designed for many different clients. They may not reflect specific needs, organizational culture, or local factors.
- Topics they must cover:
  - Personal conduct.
  - Duties of each post.
  - Disciplinary actions.
  - Reporting procedures.
  - Company rules and regulations.
  - The officer's authority on behalf of the client company.
- Appendix 3B: examples of orders.

#### 2.4 Bidders' List (3.1.4)

- The quality of service depends directly on the quality of local supervision and local training.
- Depending on the size of the agency:
  - Large national or regional firms operating at the edge of their usual area: they find it hard to customize service as much as a local provider.
  - Small firms: they may lack the infrastructure, resources, and capital of a large one.
  - Firms of sufficient size: more flexibility in scheduling and staffing.
- Pay to officers:
  - The client must set the hourly wage rates, not leave it to the agency.
  - It is especially important in hybrid organizations (contract plus proprietary). Large differences lower morale and satisfaction, raise turnover, and erode service.
  - General rule: the contract officer's lowest wage must be at least equal to the entry-level wage of the proprietary posts.
  - If the purchasing department is bureaucratic, the contract's compensation policy is set by the security manager, not purchasing.

### 3. Agency Responsibilities (3.2)

#### 3.1 Operating Agreement (3.2.1)

- Based on the RFP, the agency delivers a detailed operating agreement with each party's responsibilities.
- Agencies prefer a standard agreement, which can be vague and written to limit their liability.
- The agreement must reflect the client's unique requirements according to a risk assessment. Moving away from the generic takes effort, but yields a tailored service, of higher quality and satisfaction.
- Alternative: a detailed specification document that describes the performance standards required of both parties.
- The agency's willingness to adapt its agreement to the client's specifications should be a key selection criterion. It indicates its service philosophy and even its future performance. (Appendix 3C: example.)
- Topics the agreement may cover:
  - Liability.
  - Insurance.
  - Physical standards for officers.
  - Pre-placement and in-service training.
  - Continuity of service.
  - Type and scope of supervision.
  - Legal limitations.
  - Scheduling.
  - Equipment.
  - Compensation.
  - Adjustments to compensation.
  - Short-term supplementary service needs.
  - Workplace.
  - Fees to be charged.
  - Hours of coverage.
  - Staffing levels.
  - Services provided.
  - Audit and payment.
  - Period of performance.
- A general statement of the client's philosophy about its security program conveys intentions and nuances that formal language does not capture.
- The detailed agreement makes client and contractor decide together the policy and procedures on contract performance and officer performance.
- The client receives only what is detailed in the agreement.
- If the client sets unrealistic requirements, agencies eager for the work accept them and failure is set up.

**Controls over the agency:**

- Look for agencies that demonstrate below-average turnover.
- Require itemized billing: officer's hourly wage, overhead, and profit, separately. If needed, also break down the overhead.
- Periodically auditing invoices at that level of detail is revealing.
- The agency must guarantee that its advertising about officer qualification, screening, induction, and training is true.
- It must teach new officers about the client company and its line of business, and give them additional site-specific training.
- Good practice: a fixed number of pre-assignment training hours paid by the agency. It can reduce turnover, because the agency bears the cost of training replacements.
- Financial penalties for nonperformance:
  - The areas of nonperformance are clearly defined.
  - The fine must be enough to stimulate performance without making the agency abandon the account.
  - The agency takes part in negotiating them, to ensure that it understands them and is willing to comply.
- Other factors that affect the agency's performance:
  - Poor scheduling (highly variable and inconsistent hours for officers).
  - Insufficient supervision.
  - Few opportunities for advancement.
  - Little or no recognition.
- For adequate supervision: a single point of contact between agency and client.
- To verify that both parties comply: regular meetings, perhaps weekly or monthly. Any disagreement, large or small, requires immediate attention.
- Most important: a real commitment to partnership.

#### 3.2 Bidders' Conference (3.2.2)

- Carefully assemble the list of qualified agencies. Include several to ensure healthy competition.
- Invite them to explain their capabilities and to provide client lists as references.
- Verify the references thoroughly. Useful technique: ask the references in the proposal for names of other clients of the contractor. These give different observations than the references chosen by the contractor.
- Compare experiences (benchmarking) with nearby companies about different agencies.
- Visit one or more sites to see the agency's officers at work.
- At the conference, qualified bidders discuss the statement of work, the general and special orders, and the proposed operating agreement. Sequence:
  1. Walking tour of the facility with the agencies' representatives.
  2. Detailed explanation of the officer's main duties.
  3. Final question-and-answer meeting.
- That way everyone prepares their proposal with the same information.

### 4. Bid Evaluation (3.3)

An adequate, standardized methodology is needed. Its criteria must allow competing agencies to be compared fairly.

#### 4.1 Criteria (3.3.1)

- First criterion: the strict deadline for submitting the proposal.
- Criteria on the content of the proposal:
  - Spelling, grammar, and accuracy in responding to each element of the RFP.
  - Whether it is customized or comes from a template.
  - Whether it includes all the details requested.
  - Whether the quality of the package suggests that the agency has real interest in the job.
- Additional criteria: speed in obtaining officers' licenses, training offered, and staff experience in the client's sector.

**Supervision of contract personnel:**

- Agencies usually provide their own supervisors. They may be assigned to the site or make periodic unscheduled visits.
- The supervisor assigned to the site can cover a scheduled shift or take on a full-time supervisory role.
- The supervisory role generally depends on the number of people on site (contract hours required). That role should influence the pay rate.
- Unless the client hires someone with solid security supervision experience, it is better to leave supervision to the agency.
- It is recommended that the client give the agency's supervisors a broad induction.
- **Rule of thumb:** if contract personnel work more than **400 hours per week**, the client should hire its own security supervisor.
- Risk: if the client's supervisor watches contract personnel too closely, employment issues can arise.
- Solution: pass the client's instructions through the agency's supervisor or the lead officer.

**Figure 3.1 — Measures of Contract Security Agency Suitability** (Perry 2000). Agencies are judged on at least seven measures:

| Measure | What to evaluate |
| --- | --- |
| Personnel | Complete verification of employment and references; psychological tests where the law permits; polygraph where the law permits; minimum qualifications that meet the state or provincial standard; review and verification of the qualifications of upper and middle management; selection process; average length of employment |
| Training | Pre-hire testing and training in accordance with the state or provincial standard; review of training manuals, films, and tests; review of the contractor's facilities; annual training; state, provincial, or university certification |
| Supervision | Supervisor selection process; training and evaluation procedures; on-site supervision; field supervision (quality and quantity of visits); visit schedule and support from the provider's management; employee evaluation reports; agency contact who handles problems and concerns |
| Wages and benefits | Competitive wage scale to attract qualified candidates; merit pay to motivate; life and health insurance for contract personnel |
| Insurance | General and excess liability insurance (and its amount); whether any part is self-insured; automobile liability (if applicable); policy exclusions; policy availability; notice of cancellation; workers' compensation insurance |
| Cost of service | Charge-out rate and how much the officer receives; overtime rates and how much the officer receives; holiday rates and how much the employee receives; effective rate (overhead plus administrative costs); equipment charges; billing periods and procedures; cost determination |
| Operational issues | Availability of the agency's management 24 hours a day; emergency response capability; standard operating procedures (examine them); service agreement expectations; who provides and pays for uniforms and equipment; officer performance standards |

#### 4.2 Sample Analysis (3.3.2)

**Case data:**

- The client needs about 16,000 hours a year of contract service (308 hours per week).
- It expects about 850 overtime hours.
- It wants a five-step wage scale that includes a supervisor category.
- Special rates are noted for short-term supplemental service and for in-service training requested by the client.
- The contractor provides, on a 24-month lease: an electronic security tour management system, two mobile radios, and a bicycle for extended patrol.
- Seven agencies responded to the RFP with detailed proposals.
- The rates are examples only; they do not reflect current wages in any area.

**Step 1 — Base pay and billing rates (Figure 3.2):**

- Column A: the competing agencies, in no particular order.
- Column B: rate each agency bills at step 1. The officer's hourly wage is the base pay; each agency adds administrative expenses, overhead, and profit to it.
- Billing percentage = billed rate ÷ step base wage. Example: Company 1, $12.72 ÷ $7.35 = 173%.
- Columns C through F: the same calculation for steps 2 through 5. Values are rounded to the whole number.
- Column G: sum of the percentages in B through F. It is a weighted total (score) that indicates cost better than the rates alone. It then serves as an objective comparison criterion.

```latex
\text{Percentage of billing} = \frac{\text{billed rate}}{\text{step base salary}} \times 100
```

**Figure 3.2 — Straight-time and billing rates by wage step** (billed rate and, in parentheses, billing %):

| Company | Step 1 (base $7.35) | Step 2 (base $7.80) | Step 3 (base $8.40) | Step 4 (base $9.00) | Step 5 (base $9.00 per the figure) | Points (G) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | $12.72 (173) | $13.20 (169) | $13.89 (165) | $14.58 (162) | $15.30 (159) | 828 |
| 2 | $11.16 (152) | $11.85 (152) | $12.57 (150) | $13.29 (148) | $14.01 (146) | 748 |
| 3 | $11.76 (160) | $12.09 (155) | $13.02 (155) | $13.96 (155) | $14.40 (150) | 775 |
| 4 | $11.55 (157) | $12.09 (155) | $12.81 (153) | $13.50 (150) | $14.22 (148) | 763 |
| 5 | $11.31 (154) | $11.94 (153) | $12.66 (151) | $13.38 (149) | $14.16 (147) | 755 |
| 6 | $13.29 (181) | $13.47 (173) | $14.01 (167) | $14.79 (163) | $14.79 (154) | 838 |
| 7 | $11.67 (159) | $12.33 (158) | $13.23 (157) | $15.00 (157) | $15.00 (156) | 788 |

**Exam note:** Figure 3.2 gives $9.00 as the step 5 base wage, the same as step 4. But the step 5 percentages only work out with $9.60 (for example, $15.30 ÷ $9.60 = 159%). The overtime rates in Figure 3.3 ($12.65 and $13.50) also work out with $9.60.

**Exam note:** other figures in Figure 3.2 do not add up. Company 7 at step 4 ($15.00) does not give 157%; Figure 3.6 implies $14.13. Company 6 at step 4 ($14.79) would give 164%, not 163%. The sums for Companies 5 and 7 give 754 and 787, not 755 and 788.

**Step 2 — Supplemental service costs (Figure 3.3):**

- Evaluates the rates for overtime, training, and short-term supplemental personnel. Controlling them is critical to staying within budget.
- Two base overtime rates:
  - B1 = $12.65: 150% of the average of all the wage steps in Figure 3.2.
  - B2 = $13.50: 150% of the average of only the three highest steps.
- Both are used because, when analyzing the proposals, it is not known how the officers' wages will be distributed.
- The weighted total gives a clearer picture of the actual supplemental rates.

**Figure 3.3 — Overtime, training, and short-term supplemental service rates** (billed rate and, in parentheses, %):

| Company | Overtime B1 (base $12.65) | Overtime B2 (base $13.50) | Training C (base $7.35) | Short-term supplemental D (base $13.50) | Points (E) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | $18.82 (148) | $19.80 (147) | $10.98 (149) | $24.00 (178) | 622 |
| 2 | $17.55 (139) | $18.51 (137) | $11.16 (152) | $18.51 (137) | 565 |
| 3 | $19.56 (155) | $20.94 (155) | $11.76 (160) | $21.60 (160) | 630 |
| 4 | $17.55 (139) | $18.51 (137) | $11.55 (157) | $17.40 (129) | 562 |
| 5 | $17.55 (139) | $18.48 (137) | $11.31 (154) | $17.40 (129) | 559 |
| 6 | $18.66 (148) | $19.71 (146) | $13.20 (180) | $19.44 (144) | 618 |
| 7 | $17.55 (139) | $18.60 (138) | $10.95 (149) | $19.95 (148) | 574 |

**Step 3 — Equipment costs:**

- The annualized cost of the required equipment, if separate from the company's overhead, is added to the hourly labor rate. That way the bids are analyzed completely.
- In the example, the contractor provides the tour system, bicycle, and radios.
- The equipment points appear in Figure 3.5. The book does not explain how they were assigned: pending.

**Step 4 — Fringe benefit costs (Figure 3.4):**

- Benefits considered: paid holidays, vacation, sick leave, health insurance, and life insurance.
- An agency with few benefits has a price advantage over a more generous one. But benefits matter for officers' morale.
- Method to equalize: 5 points for each of the five benefits, 25 points possible. 5 points are subtracted for each benefit the agency does provide.
- The agency with the fewest benefits gets the most points. The low score wins. That way the cost advantage of providing few benefits disappears.

**Figure 3.4 — Fringe benefit costs of contract security:**

| Company | Paid holidays | Vacation days (1–3 years) | Sick leave | Group health insurance | Life insurance | Points |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | 10 | 5 | 15 | $300 ded. 85/15 | N/S | 5 |
| 2 | 10 | 5 | 15 | $300 ded. 80/20 | $2,000 | 0 |
| 3 | — | — | — | $300 ded. 80/20 | — | 20 |
| 4 | 10 | 5 | 15 | $300 ded. 80/20 | $2,000 | 0 |
| 5 | 10 | 5 | 0 | $300 ded. 80/20 | $1,000 | 5 |
| 6 | 10 | 5 | 15 | $300 ded. 80/20 | $2,000 | 0 |
| 7 | 10 | 5 | — | N/S | $2,000 | 10 |

- “ded.” = deductible. The book does not explain the abbreviation N/S or the 85/15 and 80/20 splits: pending. In the scoring, N/S counts as a benefit not provided.
- The position of the two N/S cells was reconstructed from the points, because the text of the figure comes out scrambled.

**Results — Figure 3.5, Contract Security Bid Analysis:**

| Area of competition | C1 | C2 | C3 | C4 | C5 | C6 | C7 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1. Straight-time rates (Fig. 3.2) | 828 | 748 | 775 | 763 | 755 | 838 | 788 |
| 2. Overtime, training, and short-term supplemental (Fig. 3.3) | 622 | 565 | 630 | 562 | 559 | 618 | 574 |
| 3. Equipment costs | 8 | 10 | 20 | 10 | 10 | 10 | 8 |
| 4. Fringe benefits (Fig. 3.4) | 5 | 0 | 20 | 0 | 5 | 0 | 10 |

| **Total** | 1,473 | 1,333 | 1,445 | 1,345 | 1,329 | 1,476 | 1,390 |
| **Rank** | 6 | 2 | 5 | 3 | 1 | 7 | 4 |

- Bidder 5 wins, with the lowest score: 1,329 points.

**Exam note:** row 5 of the figure asks about contracts “in the country.” The text, on the other hand, speaks of having accounts “in the same area.”

**Validation — Figure 3.6, Adjusted Base Rates (over 16,000 hours):**

- The estimated annual labor cost of each bid is calculated to validate the evaluation.
- Average straight-time (S/T) rate: average of the step 3 and step 4 rates in Figure 3.2 (base wage $8.40 and $9.00).
- Average overtime (O/T) rate: average of the two overtime rates in Figure 3.3.
- Base schedule for the calculation: 16,150 S/T hours and 850 O/T hours.

| Company | Average S/T rate | Average O/T rate | Estimated S/T $ | Estimated O/T $ | Estimated annual labor cost |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | $14.24 | $19.26 | $229,976 | $16,371 | $246,347 |
| 2 | $12.93 | $18.03 | $208,820 | $15,326 | $224,146 |
| 3 | $13.40 | $20.25 | $216,410 | $17,213 | $233,623 |
| 4 | $13.16 | $18.03 | $212,534 | $15,326 | $227,860 |
| 5 | $13.08 | $18.02 | $211,242 | $15,317 | $226,559 |
| 6 | $14.34 | $19.19 | $231,519 | $16,312 | $247,903 |
| 7 | $13.68 | $18.08 | $220,932 | $15,386 | $236,318 |

- The winner (5) cost $2,413 more than the cheapest (2): $226,559 versus $224,146.
- It still won: 5 already had other accounts in the same area and 2 did not. Local presence weighed more than the small cost difference.

**Exam note:** Figure 3.6 has arithmetic errors. Company 6: 16,150 × $14.34 = $231,591, not $231,519 (its total does use $231,591). Company 7: 850 × $18.08 = $15,368, not $15,386. Several average rates (Companies 1, 3, 5, 6, and 7) do not match Figures 3.2 and 3.3 exactly.

#### 4.3 Incentive Pricing (3.3.3)

- Growing trend: negotiate wages directly and base the agency's profit on the base payroll plus some incentive.
- The client must ensure that the wage actually paid is sufficient to attract qualified officers.

| Approach | How it works | Effect |
| --- | --- | --- |
| High direct wage + recruiting paid by the client | A direct wage higher than normal is agreed; the client pays the direct recruiting expenses | The contractor attracts and retains staff without charging extra compensation; the client can demand tougher specifications and better performance |
| Maximum officer turnover level | Cap based on local turnover in jobs with equivalent pay; measured each month | Turnover at the agreed level → bills at the agreed rates. Lower → bonus, as a % of the normal bill. Higher → penalty, as a % discount on the bill |

- The first approach is different from just raising the hourly rate: of that increase, only a part reaches the officer.
- Even if the client pays a low-turnover bonus every month, it costs less than the expense of training constant replacements.

### 5. Contract Award (3.4)

- After deciding the award, formal negotiations between the parties begin.
- Objective: that the general and special orders and the terms and conditions of the operating agreement are fully understood.
- Both parties review and agree on the risks and responsibilities relating to the officers.
- Silently accepting terms that are unsatisfactory to either party is a bad start.
- From the beginning it must be clear that the terms of the agreement apply strictly to both parties.
- Professional, serious contractors welcome this kind of agreement. It eliminates many frustrations they experience with distant, unprepared clients.
- A professional approach generates a professional response.

#### 5.1 Legal Considerations (3.4.1)

- Premises liability is a broad, complex, and changing area of law.
- The client must determine how much liability it can transfer to the contractor through the contract.
- Example: the notion of agency, that is, whether someone acts on behalf of another person, is key when administering the contract.

### 6. Administering the Operating Agreement (3.5)

- Day-to-day administration is not simple. A solid, satisfactory relationship requires continuous communication.
- Checks and balances needed:
  - Regular scheduled meetings to review operations.
  - Surprise site inspections by representatives of the client and the contractor, together and separately.
  - Regular operational and procedural audits.
  - Invoice review.
- If inadequate performance, by the officers or the agency, is tolerated, it soon becomes the new norm.

### Key Terms

proprietary security force · contract security officers · hybrid (contract and proprietary) security organization · public areas · semi-public areas · private facilities and property · express contract · implied contract · warranty · security services contract · buyer beware · request for proposal (RFP) · solicitation summary · bidders' conference · letter of intent · scope of work · statement of work · personnel schedule · general orders · special orders · post · bidders' list · operating agreement · specification document · right to audit · tour limitation rules · liquidated damages · incomplete shift penalty · improper assignment penalty · prime contractor · subcontracting · turnover · overhead · financial penalties for nonperformance · single point of contact · benchmarking · bid evaluation · lead officer · charge-out rate · effective rate · workers' compensation · five-step wage scale · billing rate percentage · weighted total · straight time (S/T) · overtime (O/T) · short-term supplemental service · fringe benefits · incentive pricing · maximum officer turnover level · contract award · premises liability · agency · checks and balances

## Capítulo 4 — Consultants as a Protection Resource (págs. 143–162)

**Central idea:** Security consultants provide outside expertise, objectivity, and fresh ideas when the company lacks time or internal knowledge. The chapter covers their types, how to find, select, and pay them, and how to manage the project through to the final report.

### 1. Why Consultants Are Used (introduction)

Reasons for seeking outside help:

- lack of time;
- lack of in-house specialized knowledge;
- desire for an independent, objective assessment;
- fresh ideas;
- flexibility to hire personnel only when needed.

Professional security consultants are the main resource. Sometimes expert people from within the company itself are used.

### 2. The Value of Consultants (4.1)

Advantages of the independent consultant:

- Does not promote or sell products: assesses real needs and recommends a mix of solutions to reduce threats.
- Facing liability risks, an objective study by a third party is preferred to an internal analysis. It is an advantage in liability and due diligence.
- Brings creative, “out-of-the-box” thinking.
- Is less susceptible to internal politics and bureaucracy.
- Usually costs less than hiring staff: no capital outlay or payroll burden. Especially if the work is periodic and does not justify a full-time position.

Resistance: middle managers and line employees may fear for their jobs. The fear is mostly unfounded, but consultant and management must address it. Typical concerns:

1. Asking for outside help suggests that the security staff is incompetent.
2. A negative report from an outsider makes the security program and the organization look bad.
3. An outsider who knows the company in depth could compromise the organization and its policies and procedures.

Even so, using consultants allows the security executive to add temporary talent, reduce costs, and improve the status of the department and its staff.

### 3. Types of Security Consultants (4.2)

Three broad external categories. In addition, an internal security consultant or an advisory committee can serve as an internal resource to detect problems before calling an outsider.

| Type | Function | Key features |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Security management consultant | Helps the client manage its protection strategies | Largest group; specialized by sector; many are generalists; does not get into detailed technical design |
| Technical security consultant | Converts concepts and functionality into detailed plans and equipment specifications | Requires years of technical training and experience |
| Forensic security consultant | Investigation, evidence, vulnerabilities, mitigation, and litigation | Works only on security issues |
| Security advisory committee | Internal resource that monitors whether current measures are adequate | Decides whether a problem is solved in-house or referred to an outside consultant |

#### 3.1 Security Management Consultants (4.2.1)

- They are the largest group in this niche profession.
- Their specialty is the basis of their expertise and reputation. The most frequent: healthcare, manufacturing, transportation, banking, finance, and retail.
- Knowing the consultant's specialty is a key selection criterion.
- Example 1: a retailer assessing its distribution system should look for expertise in retail security, loss prevention, or supply chain.
- Example 2: a theme park rewriting its policy and procedure manual should look for experience in theme parks and in policy writing.
- Both examples show the importance of the scope of work.
- Other specialties could also serve. For the retail case: experts in warehouses, trucking, delivery services, or shipping and receiving. For the park: a retail or loss prevention consultant.
- Many are generalists. Example: a banking and finance expert generally knows investigations, physical and electronic security, and preemployment screening.
- Limit: they can give the functional concepts of a system, but are not specialists in its detailed design.

#### 3.2 Technical Security Consultants (4.2.2)

They have specific technical or subject-matter expertise. Typical applications:

- physical security and systems integration;
