- information technology security (IT security);
- personnel security;
- convergence (combining groups, procedures, or technologies);
- legal matters and other regulations;
- engineering;
- personal or proprietary information;
- liability and due diligence;
- management of security personnel and the protection force.

Function and use:

- They translate the management consultant's concepts into plans and equipment specifications.
- Some also write policies and procedures, or subcontract that part to a management consultant.
- Typical use: new construction or renovation. They work with architects and design engineers.
- They integrate access control, video surveillance, and alarm monitoring into the initial design.
- They detect security problems in the plans before they are finalized.
- They recommend hardware and software compatible with the building's other systems.
- Advantage: they keep security at the forefront of planning and save money, because they avoid retrofitting security after construction.

#### 3.3 Forensic Security Consultants (4.2.3)

- Forensic consulting covers: investigation; identification and collection of evidence; identification of vulnerabilities; mitigation strategies; and litigation.
- They may be called an expert or expert witness. That term is broad and implies expertise in any subject; the forensic security expert works only on security issues.
- Management and technical consultants may also accept forensic assignments in civil security lawsuits.
- Use-of-force case: a nightclub security employee ejects a drunk customer, who suffers serious injuries. The expert gives an opinion on whether the force was reasonable or excessive.
- False arrest case: a customer is detained for theft and it is proven that there was no crime. The expert gives an opinion on whether there was probable cause for the detention.
- Risk managers use them to assess whether a negligence claim has merit or whether a claim against the insurance has merit.
- Some security departments, with their insurer, require certain forensic consultants in their defense because of prior successes in litigation.

#### 3.4 Security Advisory Committee (4.2.4)

- Internal resource that helps executives and the chief security officer (CSO) verify that current measures are adequate.
- If changes are needed, it helps decide whether they are corrected with internal resources or referred to an outside consultant.
- Purpose, according to the book's sample policy statement: critically examine the program to protect all assets; maintain overall oversight of the program; and help meet corporate and government requirements.
- It is chaired by a project coordinator. It reviews the program **at least quarterly**.
- Functions: see whether additional measures are needed; advise on changes to policies or procedures; evaluate proposals according to their effect on the company, on specific units, and on employees; receive suggestions from supervisors and employees; and consider corrective actions.
- Members: represent key functions, have internal stature and credibility, and know the operation well.

### 4. How to Use a Consultant (4.3)

Hiring arises from a specific problem, need, challenge, or goal.

Case: a supermarket chain suffers many violent crimes at one store. If the scope is limited to that store, the consultant is asked to measure the crime level and recommend changes. In practice, the consultant will do a complete crime analysis.

**Crime analysis** (Vellani 2001): a logical examination of the crimes that have overcome preventive measures. It includes the frequency of specific crimes, the timing details of each incident (hour and day), and the risk to occupants. It also includes applying revised standards and preventive measures that, if met and monitored, can solve the problem.

Consultant's steps in the case:

1. Determine what crimes occurred at the store and in its parking lot.
2. Assess the existing security measures where they occurred.
3. Note the additional measures needed to block future opportunities.

Effective combination of solutions that may be recommended:

- crime prevention through environmental design (CPTED): architectural design plus prevention methods;
- changes in environmental design;
- updating of policies and procedures;
- more security personnel;
- improvement of physical security requirements.

Other points:

- Warning sign: a non-independent consultant who promotes a single product or a limited range of measures. A good program uses a varied, well-considered set of measures.
- Security-in-depth: series of barriers in a protection plan. If management rejects the proposal because of competition for resources, the independent consultant rules on whether the barriers are efficient and cost-effective.
- The consultant must also detect whether the barriers create new hazards or problems and how to solve them.
- “Fresh eyes”: the outsider sees what the store staff do not see every day.
- Management usually accepts the consultant's ideas because he or she brings experience from other companies and knows industry standards.
- Sometimes the consultant organizes meetings among clients from several companies to discuss best practices.
- The consultant must stay above internal politics: getting into it distracts and costs time and money.

### 5. How to Find a Consultant (4.4)

- Logical starting point: the consultants the executive already knows.
- The more specialized, the more selective the consultant is in accepting assignments.
- Distrust the independent consultant who claims to master all aspects of security.
- Consulting firms bring together varied experts to broaden the assignments they accept.
- Best source: a colleague's recommendation, ideally from a similar business.

Associations to search (companies without security contacts):

| Type of source | Organizations cited by the book |
| --- | --- |
| Security associations | International Association of Professional Security Consultants (IAPSC); ASIS International |
| Cross-sector consulting | Institute of Management Consultants |
| Sector associations | Building Owners and Managers Association; Institute for Real Estate Management; International Association of Chiefs of Police; or local equivalents |
| Others found on the Internet | International Professional Security Association; Professional Information Security Association (lists consultants by service and location); Information Systems Security Association |

**Exam note:** the book writes “Institute for Real Estate Management”; the real name is Institute **of** Real Estate Management.

### 6. Selecting the Consultant (4.5)

It requires reviewing credentials and interviewing. Depending on the size of the project, a request for proposal (RFP) can be used with the steps from chapter 3. Alternative method, **after defining the scope of work**, in five steps:

| Step | What is done | Details |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1. Identify candidates | Ask peers and colleagues for names | Industry associations (the IAPSC website lists consultants by location, services, and industry); ads on industry websites |
| 2. Invite to submit an application | Tailored application with comparable data (model in Appendix 4A) | Alternative: letter of services, using 4A as a checklist; attach résumé; proof of license or registration where required |
| 3. Evaluate the applications | Compare quality of documents and credentials | Contact several previous clients; with the best, background investigation through references and structured interview |
| 4. Interview the top two to three | In person, with at least two company representatives | Redacted work samples; same questions for everyone; probe their security philosophy; prior walk-through if there is physical security; ask them to explain how they would approach a problem |
| 5. Negotiate an agreement and finalize | Negotiate with the first candidate | Scope, deliverable, methodology, deadlines, and expenses; if it fails, move quickly to the next; then present the contract |

Details of step 2:

- The sample résumé (Appendix 4B) includes a section on knowledge, skills, and abilities (KSAs). There the consultant adapts it to the project.
- Application plus résumé allow a uniform comparison.
- Asking for both discourages weak candidates: their own documents rule them out.

Evaluation criteria according to the ASIS Risk Assessment guideline (2015):

- verify background, education, and experience;
- test knowledge and skills;
- review written work samples;
- interview to evaluate knowledge, communication, and personal behavior;
- confirm relevant credentials and certifications.

Other points:

- Non-local candidate: negotiate the cost of bringing him or her in, or have an employee travel to see him or her. Teleconferencing is an increasingly useful option.
- Contract: sample professional services agreement in Appendix 4C.
- 4C includes supplementary agreements on handling proprietary or government-classified information and on conflicts of interest.
- All are discussed and signed **before** starting. Always consult legal counsel when drafting the contract.

### 7. Fees and Expenses (4.6)

Factors that set fees: the subject matter, the level of expertise, and the geographic region.

- Consider time and quality: a low fee can turn out more expensive, because a less skilled consultant takes longer.
- The industry has a long history of awarding to the lowest bidder.
- Some product or service companies offer very cheap consulting. Their objectivity is doubtful if the solution leads to buying their equipment or services.
- Faced with an apparent bargain: *caveat emptor* (“let the buyer beware”).
- Medium or large firms charge more than the individual consultant or small firm. It may be due to higher overhead, but also to analysts, editors, and specialists who work as a team.
- All bill direct charges (time and travel) and overhead: office rent, administrative help, proposal expenses, publications, and professional taxes and licenses.
- That is why the daily rate does not equal an annual income: they do not work every day, but their expenses continue.
- Time tracking: time and expense software, project diary, spreadsheets, or billing and scheduling software.
- The client chooses the form of payment that fits its accounting and the type of assignment.
- There are **five options**, not mutually exclusive (e.g., a retainer plus additional hours). Also set how expenses are billed and approved.

| Arrangement | When it applies | How it works, advantages, and risks |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Hourly fees | Assignment of less than a day with uncertain duration; forensic consulting | “Let the meter run.” E.g.: interviewing an employee who is being reassigned, about two hours. In forensic work it is charged by the hour and fraction (avoiding litigation, assessing a case, negotiating a settlement) |
| Daily fees | Assignments of one or several days | Hourly rate × 8. It usually benefits the client, because the workday can run long, unless the contract fixes the hours. A number of days at a fixed rate and a somewhat lower rate afterward can be agreed |
| Fixed fees or flat fee | Days that can be estimated precisely and are controlled by the consultant; non-complex study | Total amount, often with expenses included. Calculated with the consultant's hourly rate plus that of office staff. Advantage: compare offers and budget exactly. Not suitable for implementing a program: the consultant depends on client employees and absorbs the extra time; in that case the scope must be defined very carefully to protect both |
| Not-to-exceed fees | When a guaranteed limit is wanted | Costs of contingencies or delays are not passed on to the client, unless the client agrees. Difference from fixed: it allows paying less than estimated |
| Retainers | Regular use of the consultant | The consultant commits to a certain number of days a year and the client has guaranteed access. Substantially discounted rate |

Not-to-exceed example: 5 days are estimated with a cap of 7. If it is finished in 5 or 6, 5 or 6 are paid. If 8 are needed, 7 are paid.

Retainer details:

- Example: 2 days a month at a fixed rate per day, or 24 days a year at a fixed annual price.
- The client secures the minimum days; the consultant secures an income.
- It is negotiable: the client can use all the days in the first half of the year or less than half.
- The consultant keeps the payment even if the minimum days are not used.
- Disadvantage for the consultant: scheduling problems if an emergency arises from a retainer client while he or she is working on another project.
- Recommended solution: set specific days in specific months. If not possible, start on the first available day after notice.
- Extra days: paid at the discounted rate or the normal rate, as agreed.

Miscellaneous arrangements (4.6.6):

- agencies that bill for individual consultants, especially experts and specialized services;
- full or partial payment with equity in the client;
- percentage of the savings achieved (uncommon in security management consulting).

With innovative formulas, both parties seek legal advice. A clear, binding agreement avoids litigation or interruptions.

Expenses (4.6.7):

- They cover transportation, subsistence, administrative services, and other project costs.
- They are reduced if the consultant uses the client's services: administrative assistance, office, and copies.
- Reimbursement at actual cost, supported with expense reports. Alternative: daily per diem for subsistence plus actual cost of transportation and others.
- Define what is reimbursable and the limits (e.g., daily cap on hotel and meals), and communicate it during selection.
- Common practice: give the consultant the same travel allowances as the client's senior management.
- Airfare in coach class, unless first or business class is expressly approved. Do not assume everyone knows that policy.
- International travel: almost always at least 2 days of travel (round trip). It requires special compensation.
- Goal: agree on the expenses at the start. Appendix 4C has expense forms and guidelines.

### 8. Working with Consultants (4.7)

- After signing and before starting, inform affected managers and employees. The announcement, ideally from the chief executive officer (CEO), asks for cooperation.
- Assign a consulting project coordinator who informs, helps, and monitors progress.
- The scope of work must include a work plan, progress reports, and a final report.

#### 8.1 Coordinating the Project (4.7.1)

- Option A: a single representative as coordinator, normally the CSO or the vice president (VP) of security.
- Option B: a temporary project committee, often drawn from the security advisory committee.
- There must be a designated alternate who responds in time if the coordinator is absent.
- An internal project sponsor is advisable. Good candidate: whoever proposed the original idea. He or she is an ally of the consultant and the project manager.
- Mission of the coordinator and the committee: to be the liaison between consultant and company. It is a critical task.
- Members: represent the areas involved, know the organization well, and have the credibility and influence to obtain data and schedule interviews.

#### 8.2 Organizational Orientation (4.7.2)

The coordinator organizes an orientation that includes:

- background and responsibilities of key personnel, before meeting them, and their role in the assignment;
- organization chart;
- operating environment;
- key assets and functions;
- relevant internal and external relationships;
- specific legislative or regulatory controls;
- company history;
- senior management's philosophy;
- competitive position;
- if publicly traded: the latest annual shareholder report and mandatory regulatory reports.

In addition:

- discuss results of similar previous projects;
- reveal unique or unusual situations;
- warn of cultural idiosyncrasies: a minor action by the consultant could cause an incident.
- Even so, the consultant is an objective, independent observer, with the freedom and the obligation to state the facts.
- Work in another country: he or she must know the culture and customs, working conditions, local legislation, and visa requirements.

#### 8.3 Levels of Assistance (4.7.3)

- The consultant's time costs money: the coordinator ensures timely support.
- Support departments: legal, labor relations, public relations, and finance; qualified internal technical help.
- Internal staff can prepare letters, memoranda, and reports; the contract's reports are prepared by the consultant.
- Nondisclosure agreements: necessary, because the consultant has access to sensitive information.
- Method for handling sensitive information, including conclusions and recommendations: few people, and trusted ones.
- Key: written reports are subject to discovery by the opposing party in a trial. Define the custodian and location in case of a subpoena.
- Visits to other companies or sites: the coordinator handles security clearances, flights or corporate aircraft, rental cars, and hotels.
- The methodology must be sound and accepted. The most used: the ASIS Risk Assessment guideline; IAPSC's *Forensic Methodology Best Practice*; and ISO 31000 *Risk Management—Guidelines* (International Organization for Standardization).

#### 8.4 Scope of Work (4.7.4)

- It must be in the request for quote and in the written contract. Those few paragraphs are not enough to start.
- Before starting, client and consultant review objectives, goals, scope, and deliverables, together with the coordinator, committee, and those affected.
- **Definition:** the scope of work is the central objective or clear focus of the assignment.
- Example: “reassess the distribution system to detect procedural deficiencies that cause cargo losses.” Every inspection, interview, and document is guided and limited by that objective.
- The initial review defines strategies, brings up to date anyone who did not take part in the selection, and allows questions. The consultant is already part of the team.
- **Scope creep:** the scope grows after signing and starting. Either party can raise it, often out of necessity.
- It is of most concern if payment is hourly and the budget can be exceeded.
- It is expanded only with the agreement of both parties, in writing, as an addendum to the contract.

#### 8.5 Work Plans (4.7.5)

- They are prepared with the coordinator after agreeing on the scope: tasks, priorities, assignments, and schedules.
- Deadlines are turned into milestone and goal charts to review progress.
- The frequency of reviews and the information the client will deliver are set, with dates.
- In long projects: frequent progress meetings.
- The consultant is ultimately responsible for staying on course despite internal politics or culture.
- Measuring progress: earned value analysis, done by consultant and coordinator at those meetings.
- If support deadlines are not met, the work slows or stops. The coordinator is responsible for seeing that they are met.

#### 8.6 Progress Reports (4.7.6)

- Periodic meetings and written reports, with the qualifier “if deemed necessary” in the work plan.
- All project staff and interested managers attend.
- The coordinator, or an assigned member, takes the minutes: decisions, progress, tasks, and deadlines.
- Frequency depends on size and complexity. Too many meetings hinder the work; too few put control at risk.
- If the meetings are enough, interim reports can be omitted.

#### 8.7 Final Report (4.7.7)

- Every project requires a written final report. In management consulting, with recommendations for the future, it must be detailed. Even a technical assignment with a system up and running requires one.
- Structure: 1) executive summary; 2) results achieved; 3) recommendations. It is advisable to follow the order of the scope.
- Results: whether the goals were met, which parts of the plan were not achieved and why.
- Recommendations: additional work needed and how to do it.
- Final briefing to senior management: given by the consultant, with the coordinator's support.
- Number each recommendation. Example from the book: “Recommendation #23”: stop using part-time police officers in the cash offices. Replace them with modern procedures and physical barriers, with a two-door man trap and remote electronic access control. Its implementation would be “Project #23.”
- The report must have enough detail for the client's staff to carry out the tasks.
- If the client needs the consultant to implement: additional contract or amendment, with a new scope.
- Example: after a vulnerability study, the consultant searches for and prequalifies a security executive. Usual fee: **25% to 30% of the first-year salary**, plus search expenses.

### 9. The Future of Consulting (4.8)

- The use of consultants will tend to grow.
- When downsizing, companies lose internal specialists and then hire consultants. That is why many consultants focus on a niche they offer to many clients.
- Niche example: crime analysis. The employees who did it were promoted or moved to general functions.
- Consultant alliances, increasingly common:
  - management consultant who recommends improving access control + technical consultant who specifies, bids, and supervises the installation;
  - forensic consultant who testifies about a security deficiency + management consultant who recommends how to correct it.
- Some organizations have specialized consultants on staff and turn to vendors and other consultants when needed.
- The key to success for both parties is knowing how to work effectively with the consultant.

### Key Terms

security management consultant · technical security consultant · forensic security consultant · expert witness · security advisory committee · scope of work · scope creep · addendum · crime analysis · crime prevention through environmental design (CPTED) · security-in-depth · request for proposal (RFP) · request for quote · knowledge, skills, and abilities (KSAs) · hourly fees · daily fees · fixed fees · not-to-exceed fees · retainer · per diem · caveat emptor · overhead · project coordinator · project sponsor · liaison · nondisclosure agreement · discovery · subpoena · earned value analysis · work plan · progress reports · final report · executive summary · final briefing · man trap · liability · due diligence · probable cause · false arrest · IAPSC · ISO 31000

## Capítulo 5 — Executive Protection (págs. 181–218)

**Central idea:** Executive protection seeks to keep the principal out of danger, above all through advance planning, not by confronting the aggressor. A good program starts from a recurring risk assessment and is managed like a business (metrics, indicators, vendors). It combines hard skills with soft skills, and covers residence, close protection, transportation, and special teams.

### 1. Purpose and Collateral Value of Executive Protection (5.1)

**Definition.** Executive protection (EP), also called close protection: security and risk mitigation measures for people with elevated personal risk. That risk can come from their employment, high public profile, great wealth (high net worth), or affiliations.

- Many programs are loosely based on the standards and procedures of highly visible government programs.
- The main references are the U.S. Secret Service and the Diplomatic Security Service.
- The field evolved greatly in the early 21st century. Today a dynamic professional with multiple skills is demanded, one who integrates into changing environments.

**Key distinction from a traditional police officer or guard:** the EP agent's main objective is to keep the principal out of harm's way. The agent does not confront the conflict head-on. The agent quickly gets the client out of the situation and, above all, avoids it through advance planning.

**Purpose of the program.** It varies, and the professional must know exactly which risks his or her program mitigates.

- Specific, credible threat: requires a particular style of close protection for a period.
- Most common case: a sustainable, programmatic, and flexible approach.

**People who may require protection:**

1. People recognizable by position or prominence, sought out by the public and with public personal data. E.g.: A-List celebrity or CEO (chief executive officer) of a large company.
2. People with a highly controversial job or position. E.g.: leader of a political party or group.
3. People of great wealth, with higher risk of extortion or kidnapping.
4. People who have received threats against their safety.

Also business leaders. The board of directors often imposes protection on the CEO and/or the COO (chief operating officer). Reason: the harm the company would suffer if something happened to them.

**First step to build a program:** assess the risk to the individual, his or her family, and the organization. The current risk portfolio must be understood and the future one anticipated.

- Nothing detracts more from credibility than a plan that does not respond to the client's real risk.
- Example: demanding four agents for a CEO at a safe event, when his or her peers have one. The client will not forget it and it will be hard to persuade him or her later.

**Hard vs. soft skills:**

| Type | Examples | What the book says |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Hard skills | Shooting, martial arts, driving | It is the image of the bodyguard in popular culture. They are important, but the client almost never sees them |
| Soft skills | Clear and efficient communication, logistical planning, critical thinking and problem solving, verbal conflict resolution | They are what make the agent successful and give the client the most benefit |

What the client sees, remembers, and values:

- how the agent represents him or her and his or her organization;
- how the agent integrates into different professional environments;
- how the agent supports his or her family and staff;
- how the agent effectively solves all kinds of problems.

**Collateral benefits that every agent must know how to quantify and deliver:**

| Benefit | What it consists of |
| --- | --- |
| Security drivers | They reduce risks. They plan primary and alternate motorcade routes and study traffic. They receive alerts and know the best drop-off and pickup points. They save the client time |
| Time management | Plan schedule and trips at events, conferences, and engagements. Reduces unnecessary time among crowds and speeds the move from one event to another |
| Travel logistics | Advance planning is the most critical thing the agent does to mitigate risk, and the most useful for the schedule. It is coordinated with the client's staff and contingencies are prepared |
| Normalcy | For ultra-high-profile clients, routine tasks (taking a child to the zoo, getting a coffee) are complicated by the public. That creates risk and annoyance. The agent, discreetly, manages those approaches |

**Return on investment (ROI).** EP is costly, so its ROI must be quantified. It is usually tied to the collateral benefits; each program can contribute others of its own. Clients and civilian staff do not see the hours of training. They judge success by how contingencies are resolved, how the agent communicates, and how the agent represents them.

**Intelligence.** Successful protection is tied to a robust, continuous intelligence program. It gives the agent a complete picture of the risk in order to adapt his or her practices.

### 2. Domains of Protection (5.2)

#### 2.1 Corporate Executive Protection (5.2.1)

If the CEO or another prominent figure is kidnapped or incapacitated, the company can lose focus in the short term. In the long term, it can suffer reputational damage. Business continuity is disrupted: operations, culture, stock price, revenue, and position.

- EP teams have been an integral part of mature security organizations in large corporations for about 20 years.
- Risks are increasingly global and compound, from cyber to privacy.

**Corporate program structure models:**

| Model | Characteristics | When it is used |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Full-time employees | EP staff are in-house personnel | When the board imposes the program and/or several executives use it |
| Outsourced | Vendors provide the protection | The book does not specify |
| Hybrid | In-house staff set strategy, policies, and procedures; vendors supplement | It is common to outsource specialties: technical security countermeasures or travel support in a specific region |

**Why a corporate program is started:** (1) risk to the executive; (2) business need; (3) need for structure and efficiency to support senior management. The corporate agent must understand the business of EP as much as the tactical and operational.

**Questions that EP agents and managers must know how to answer and explain:**

1. Why did the corporation start the program?
2. Is there a board mandate to protect certain people or positions?
3. Is there a specific or ongoing risk, and how is it measured?
4. Are there key performance indicators (KPIs) for the team?
5. Is there a one-, three-, or five-year roadmap to expand the EP team and threat management?
6. Is the program corporate only, or does it also provide personal and residential coverage? If so, how are those tasks divided and recorded?
7. Does it include the executive's family, and how is that recorded?
8. Has the executive had protection before?
9. What access will EP managers have to discuss strategy, expectations, and mitigation with executives, staff, and families?
10. What other key stakeholders will the team work with? The book cites, without closing the list:
    - legal team;
    - chief financial officer (CFO) and tax and executive perk team;
    - human resources;
    - policy team;
    - communications and public relations;
    - events staff;
    - corporate support staff, such as executive assistants.

Knowing how to explain the need for protection to the board, the CFO, and others makes the program look like a valued professional unit.

**Case: “car brand A”:** if the executive visits a client who manufactures brand A, he or she must arrive in a car of that brand. It teaches that an eye for detail avoids embarrassment and business problems, and demonstrates value.

**U.S. tax regulation.** Corporate EP is usually covered by Internal Revenue Service (IRS) rules on executive perks. Notable requirements:

- genuine (bona fide) 24/7 security program, provided by trained professionals;
- a verifiable risk;
- an annual audit demonstrating that a genuine risk exists.

#### 2.2 Celebrity Protection (5.2.2)

- Historically associated with the term bodyguards. In the 21st century it became professionalized.
- It can overlap with other domains: many corporate leaders and some religious leaders are iconic.
- Fame is achieved through media attention. It includes entertainers, athletes, media personalities, actors, and artists. They depend heavily on their public brand.
- Typical challenges: meet-and-greet encounters with fans, concerts or performances, and press events.
- Those events raise the risk to privacy and the risk of being targeted by persons of concern or stalkers.
- According to studies (Meloy 2016), athletes have greater situational vulnerability. They appear in nightclub altercations, robberies, drive-by shootings, and other violent encounters.

#### 2.3 Government Protection (5.2.3)

All levels of government have units that protect prominent figures.

- **U.S. Secret Service**, a division of the Department of Homeland Security. By federal mandate it protects:
  - the president and the vice president;
  - their respective families;
  - many visiting heads of state;
  - presidential candidates;
  - national special security events (NSSE).
- **United Kingdom:** the prime minister is protected by SO1, a special unit of the Metropolitan Police.
- **FBI** (Federal Bureau of Investigation): cross-trained agents protect its director. The model is repeated in other specialized agencies.
- **State governors:** the state police provide the “dignitary protection details.”
- **Mayors:** specialized units of their municipal police.

**Protection agent vs. law enforcement agent.** Although many are sworn police officers, the protection agent's duty is to get the principal out of danger if there is an incident. And, above all, to avoid exposing the principal through planning. The agent does not run toward the problem: the agent flees the danger with the principal. This attitude is hard to retrain in police officers.

**Differences between government and private protection:**

| Aspect | Government | Private |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Authority | Derives from specific laws and regulations | Often no formal authority to act |
| Control of the environment | The authority may allow control of the principal's area. E.g.: the Secret Service's federal law is 18 U.S.C. Section 3056 (Title 18 of the U.S. Code) | No such power |
| Status | Sworn officials or law enforcement agents: they have authority and go through demanding training and vetting | Not necessarily |
| Weapons | Often legally armed | Not always permitted |
| Training | Mandatory regular cycles of training and tactical and physical evaluation | Not always required |
| Plans | Strict protocols: advance report with primary and contingency plans, reviewable after an incident | The book does not require it |
| Incidents | There may be a formal investigation by bodies such as the U.S. Congress | The book does not mention it |

**Essential characteristics of the effective agent, common to all levels:**

1. Excellent critical thinking.
2. Effective communication.
3. Ability to de-escalate situations.
4. Ability to plan and advance all of the principal's movements.
5. Anticipate challenges and have primary and backup plans for every scenario.
6. Access to information and intelligence.
7. Assess risk constantly and apply it to the protection plan.
8. Focus on keeping the principal out of danger, not on getting into an altercation.

Many move from government to the private sector and their training is valuable. But they must understand the weight of soft skills. Also the limits of their authority and the licenses required in the private sector.

#### 2.4 Faith-Based Protection (5.2.4)

Aimed at leaders of faith-based entities. Two extremes of risk:

- people fixated on their preaching, who believe they are “one step away” from a higher power and cling to them in a dangerous way;
- people who vehemently oppose their preaching and want to harm them.

Because they are in the public eye and in contact with their community, their risks resemble those of celebrities.

#### 2.5 Hybrid Protection Models (5.2.5)

- They connect several domains. E.g.: iconic corporate figures with celebrity status, or politicians with a celebrity presence.
- Global trust barometers indicate that people increasingly depend on corporate leaders and celebrities for opinions on social issues.
- Deloitte, *Global Human Capital Trends* 2018 (Agarwal 2018): organizations are no longer judged only by financial results or product quality. They are judged by their relationship with workers, customers, and communities, and by their social impact.

**Do not confuse:** “hybrid” has two senses in the chapter. In 5.2.1 it is a structure model (in-house staff plus vendors). In 5.2.5 it is the mixing of domains (corporate, celebrity, government).

### 3. Managing the Executive Protection Program (5.3)

#### 3.1 Third-Party Operations (5.3.1)

A corporation can outsource part or all of its protection operation.

- **Supplementary services:** local security drivers; advance staff who know the destination city; additional protection staff authorized to carry weapons in that city.
- **Auxiliary elements:** protective intelligence analysts and technical surveillance countermeasures (TSCM) technicians.
- **Full outsourcing:** including management and strategic direction of the program.

The approach depends on many factors. A key one: whether the corporate security program has the depth to run it. That includes recruiting and retaining staff in this specialized career and handling their logistics.

The process of hiring consultants is in Chapter 4. **Considerations specific to hiring EP consulting or services:**

1. Types of services the provider offers.
2. Whether the staff are the provider's own or subcontracted.
3. Where it has offices and staff.
4. Insurance.
5. Licenses.
6. Reputation. The team is the principal's immediate circle. If the provider carries ethical problems, litigation, or criminal matters, it can seriously damage the executive's brand.

**Other considerations when evaluating providers (West 2016):**

- strategic planning support;
- residential security services;
- protective surveillance or countersurveillance;
- ability to add intelligence services;
- event security support;
- possible investigative support.

#### 3.2 Threat, Vulnerability, and Risk Assessments (5.3.2)

They are similar in concept to traditional risk assessments. They are aligned with the strategic approach of enterprise security risk management (ESRM).

Unlike static facilities, they measure the principal's own risk according to:

- career;
- public image;
- lifestyle;
- current position and status;
- family history.

The scope can include the impact of the entity he or she represents (religious group, corporation) on his or her risk. Also his or her social environment: family members as soft targets to reach him or her. E.g.: the kidnapping of a child can prevent him or her from functioning.

#### Threat Assessment

In EP it has two meanings:

| Meaning | What it assesses |
| --- | --- |
| 1. Proximity to criminal elements | Terrorism and random crimes. The risk of random crime comes from the proximity of residence, office, or frequented places to areas with more crime |
| 2. Behavior-based | Persons or groups of interest detected by the protective intelligence function. The risk of targeted acts depends on public presence: conference, religious event, concert |

The company must consider both. In addition, violence is not enough: threats to the personal brand and to personal or corporate assets must be included.

- Frauds against high-net-worth people: fraudulent unemployment claims, fraudulent bank or credit accounts, threats to their online presence.
- Cybercrimes: social media hacking, smishing (text message fraud), SIM swapping, and others.
- They can attack accounts of family members and staff to reach the principal's.

**Threat vectors of a comprehensive assessment:**

1. technical (mobile and cyber systems);
2. reputational;
3. physical;
4. health and well-being (lifestyle, medical, natural).

#### Vulnerability Assessment

It considers the environments the principal passes through:

- static locations: home and office;
- vessels and aircraft;
- fixed base operations (FBO), that is, private aviation terminals;
- other frequent destinations.

Vulnerabilities are not limited to security systems. They can be psychological, administrative, technical, and physical. There are also external factors: the capacity of local government and police to respond to crime.

- Understaffed police, fire, or emergency medical services create a vulnerability that may require reinforced mitigation.
- If the average police response time exceeds a tolerable window, shelter-in-place or evacuation strategies are reinforced.

#### Risk Assessment

It is appropriate to use ASIS risk models. But in EP there are two aspects for setting the executive's risk profile:

1. **Attractiveness of the target.** It influences the probability of attack. It looks at whether the principal is iconic, highly recognizable, or associated with very attractive assets or people. Also whether he or she is an important social, political, or economic figure.
2. **Probability that a threat will succeed.**

Threats are analyzed according to: position in the company; access and exposure to potential adversaries; access to wealth or other lifestyle traits; publicity; travel habits.

**Questions an EP risk analysis answers:**

1. Who would want to harm the executive?
2. How do adversaries obtain information about him or her?
3. What is the current probability of each identified threat?
4. Does the executive want, need, and accept protection during the workday? Only on trips? 24 hours?

The specialist must assess the probability that threats will be carried out successfully. The most worrying are those known to occur but that arrive by surprise. **Sample of real threats to executives (11):**

1. Assassination.
2. Kidnapping.
3. Extortion.
4. Street violence.
5. Targeted attacks.
6. Workplace violence.
7. Online account hacking.
8. Danger to family members.
9. Public embarrassment (deliberate or accidental).
10. Injury.
11. Illness or medical emergency.

**Graduated levels of protection.** Not all of a company's executives have the same risk. Some are public and represent controversial aspects; others work behind the scenes. That is why a holistic approach is advisable.

- E.g.: the full weight of the program for the CEO; transportation and protective intelligence for the entire C-suite.
- The gradation is based on the individual's risk and on his or her value to the corporation.

**How to set an executive's threat level:**

1. Identify all threat elements: protesters, criminals, extremists, terrorists, workplace violence, and hazards from travel or other activities.
2. Analyze whether each element threatens the executive.
3. Determine how an event could develop.
4. Identify anyone with the capability and intent to harm, a history of threatening the executive or others, or who has already targeted him or her.
5. Assign the principal a risk category and give him or her the appropriate protection.

Risk assessments do not last: risk changes often. That is why they are repeated periodically.

#### 3.3 Operations (5.3.3)

Once threats and vulnerabilities are defined, in-house employees or third parties operate. **Functions of the EP team:**

1. Contribute to security policies, including the team's use-of-force protocols.
2. Develop training plans in personal protection, travel, and security awareness for executives and employees.
3. Manage event security and implement protection protocols for staff and VIPs (very important persons).
4. Communicate and liaise with local police and federal agencies where appropriate, especially on trips abroad.
5. Develop, implement, and supervise security measures at the executives' residences.
6. Develop and implement processes, procedures, and best practices in: fire safety and life protection; executive data privacy; crisis communication; event security.
7. Manage travel security:
   - advance destinations: best routes, locate venues, detect risks, assess hotels, and refine plans;
   - brief senior management on the logistics and risks of trips;
   - communicate risks to the executive's support team before the trip;
   - be ready to inform the executive of new risks during the trip.

#### 3.4 Metrics and Return on Investment (5.3.4)

**Possible metrics (Oatman 2015):**

1. Number of threats or other communications.
2. Number of public appearances by the principal with protesters present.
3. Executive time saved.
4. Number of events with in-person EP service.
5. Number of home drive-bys when the executive is away.
6. Number of risk assessments and advances performed.
7. Number of trips a year to medium- or high-risk destinations.

**Key performance indicators (KPIs).** They keep the program focused on what matters. Requirements of a good EP KPI:

- align with the program's goals and their importance;
- be understandable to all parties;
- be measurable and consistent;
- be timely.

**Possible KPI areas (West 2016):**

| Area | What it includes |
| --- | --- |
| Continuous risk assessments | Principal's prominence, destination risk, intelligence (with social media monitoring), persons of concern |
| Stakeholder satisfaction | Opinion of the principal or traveler, his or her productivity, opinion of key partners, of program management and vendors, and of event managers |
| Responsiveness | Availability and adequacy of EP resources, issues addressed, performance on urgent requests |
| Quality of services | Program management, deliverables, field support, administrative tasks |
| Team workload | Travel days, domestic trips, international trips, compensatory time |
| Readiness | Training, drills, physical assessments |
| Quality of communications | Appropriate, accurate, clear, and concise |
| Operational transparency | Execution as expected; stakeholders understand the program well |
| Financial performance | Budget set and met; response to problems detected |

#### 3.5 Essential Competencies of Protection Personnel (5.3.5)

Success depends on choosing service-minded candidates. **Behavioral attributes that are not acquired through training:** integrity, professional behavior, good judgment, team mindset, and self-awareness.

**History of the competency framework:**

- **Before 2019:** the industry relied on publications without peer review and curricula written by schools in the 1980s. Without defined competencies, recruitment, selection, and training in knowledge, skills, and abilities (KSA) suffered.
- **2019:** the ASIS Executive Protection Council, Committee for Standards & Guidelines, began drafting competencies with the international community.
- **2020:** an international team of experts drafted the competencies with support from the University of Nebraska Public Policy Center. Working groups broke them down into KSAs. The university validated them with an international survey (University of Nebraska 2020).

**Essential competencies (6 categories):**

| # | Category | Components |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Personal characteristics | Physical fitness; mental fitness; traits and attributes (communication, integrity, resilience, diligence, professionalism, judgment, critical thinking, problem solving) |
| 2 | Legal and ethical considerations | Confidentiality; rules and laws of the jurisdiction (duty of care, certification and licensing, legal liability; open list in the book); use-of-force guidelines (organization policies, force continuum, reports; open list); industry ethical standards |
| 3 | Information security and management | Basic technology risks; familiarity with technology security |
| 4 | Communication | Effective communication; listening practices; elicitation of information |
| 5 | Protective operations: planning | Advance work; management of oneself and small teams; threat, risk, and vulnerability assessment |
| 6 | Protective operations: tactical | Principles of practice (defensive tactics and de-escalation, weapons, surveillance and countersurveillance, equipment, alertness; open list); transportation; medical first response (first aid, patient assessment; open list); movement to maintain safety; incident management (situational assessment, evacuation plan, crisis response) |

#### 3.6 Training and Education of Protection Personnel (5.3.6)

**Three factors when designing the training of a private program:**

1. A standardized training and education program, so that agents know how to mitigate their clients' risks.
2. Governance over those programs: quality standards that withstand critical scrutiny after an incident.
3. An audit function that confirms up-to-date regulatory compliance. E.g.: private licenses to operate as an agent in the jurisdiction.

It is advisable to define what training they receive, how often, and how it is audited. That way the training strategy is aligned with the long-term protection strategy and roadmap.

**Hard skills training.** Two reference federal programs in the U.S.:

- the Protective Service Operations Training Program of the Federal Law Enforcement Training Center (FLETC);
- the Secret Service's James J. Rowley Training Center, with its *Protective Operations and Protective Detail Training*.

They can go beyond the basics, but serve as a guide. Courses must be certified or recognized by local bodies or regulators. List (not exhaustive, according to the book):

1. Basic protective service operations (PSO):
   - protection formations;
   - PSO advances;
   - case studies and attacks on principals;
   - cover and evacuation of principals.
2. Defensive tactics and appropriate use of force.
3. Motorcades:
   - route surveys;
   - motorcade formations and the importance of arrivals and departures;
   - motorcade security.
4. Advanced vehicle handling:
   - offensive and defensive driving techniques;
   - specialized or technical driving, such as winter driving;
   - recognition of vehicle ambushes and countermeasures.
5. Medical training according to the program's needs:
   - basic first responder;
   - ongoing field medical training (scenarios);
   - emergency medical technician (EMT) or advanced medical training if needed.
6. Detection of protective surveillance and countermeasures.
7. Protective intelligence and its use in advance planning and operations.
8. Firearms:
   - weapons safety rules and standards;
   - armed confrontation in a protective services environment.
9. Physical fitness standards:
   - set them together with the human resources (HR) department;
   - set regular training and evaluation dates.

Then a schedule of ongoing training is set. It must consider the renewal of certifications, the minimums of local regulations, and how to exceed them. Ongoing scenarios, tabletop exercises, and problem solving improve the agent and the team.

**Continuing education and soft skills.** Few agents will experience a large-scale attack like in the movies. Almost always they deal with diverse people to give the principal a smooth, safe experience. They also solve problems, plan, and de-escalate. What makes an agent successful: communicating well, treating people with respect, negotiating, and mediating.

There are not always courses for this; it is worked on through continuing education. **Opportunities and competencies to consider:**

1. Education in de-escalation. It can be delivered by psychologists specializing in dangerous behaviors, or done together with government agencies:
   - managing words and body language with an enraged citizen;
   - calming someone under the influence of substances and/or with altered mental capacity;
   - training with role-playing to practice the techniques.
2. Written and verbal skills:
   - effective report writing;
   - how to write an incident report;
   - how to write a PSO advance report;
   - how to communicate with the principal and his or her staff.
3. Case studies and trends in protective operations:
   - follow industry magazines, articles, and cases;
   - join peer and benchmarking groups;
   - communicate what is learned from cases and apply it to proposals for greater protection.
4. Self-improvement:
   - resourcefulness and resilience;
   - self-awareness and emotional intelligence.
5. Continuous study of the principal's business, clients, and organization. It serves to understand risk and communicate with stakeholders.

The agent must always balance hard and soft skills for long-term success.

### 4. Executive Protection Operations (5.4)

#### 4.1 Residential Security Services (5.4.1)

Protecting the principal at the main residence and the others has become an important niche. It combines technology (cameras, motion detectors) with close protection agents. It usually operates 24/7, especially at the main residence.

**Why it is necessary:** some corporate programs require it by board mandate. In addition, the data and addresses of wealthy and prominent people are easy to find. That attracts unwanted attention and crowds to their homes.

**Multiple layers with redundancy**, as at large events or monuments:

- crime prevention through environmental design (CPTED);
- redundant layers of technology with back-up and rollover if one part fails;
- several layers of staff posts to maximize response;
- engagement and participation of principals, staff, and residents.

Principals and team must agree on what to do and how to react in an incident. Staff are trained and evaluated on it. Ideally: residents review it with the team at least once a year.

**Functions:** physical presence, almost always continuous; access control to the house; readiness to respond to incidents; managing unwanted visitors; redirecting the press; patrols and monitoring of anti-intruder systems.

**Complexity.** Unlike the office or an event, the agent is always present in the home. That can generate tension even with very security-conscious principals. Clear communication, clarified expectations, and maximum discretion with principal, family, and neighborhood are required. Trust takes years to build and is lost in moments if protocol is broken.

**Eminem case (Matthews 2020):** a young man broke a window and entered the rapper's house. According to reports, the property's guards were asleep. It is an example of residential security failure.

**Good practice when starting a program or adding agents:** property-specific training, with a period of shadowing experienced agents. **What the new agent must know:**

1. Security systems and technology: cameras, motion sensors, panic alarms, and access control.
2. Who updates, maintains, and accesses the data of those systems.
3. How the team monitors them: what training is needed and what privacy protocols apply.
4. How the principal notifies security if a problem arises.
5. How security contacts the principal if an incident is developing on the property.
6. The property's emergency response protocols.
7. Response time of fire, police, and emergency services, and how they are let in.
8. Access control management:
   - who can enter the house;
   - with what level of access;
   - how permanent and temporary staff, such as chefs, are verified;
   - who has authority to authorize access;
   - how the access control list is updated and managed;
   - escalation procedures when an access is questioned or denied.
9. Agent posts on the property.
10. Post rotation: fixed or roving?
11. When and how often they may train on the property.
12. Service expectations of the family and staff.
13. Training additional to basic EP:
    - intelligence picture of the area and the property;
    - metrics and cases of incidents that have occurred on the property;
    - all emergency protocols and responses;
    - face page of critical staff;
    - access control practices.
14. Privacy protection on the property:
    - when and how often to conduct technical countermeasures sweeps;
    - protocol for accepting gifts, furniture, and other objects.

The list is not exhaustive. The residential agent works at a fixed location, often known to the public. Paradoxically, that place is also the principal's safe haven. It is a crucial post and a great force multiplier for earning his or her trust.

#### 4.2 Close Protection (5.4.2)

**Security advances.** Planning the principal's movements in detail is key to the agent's success and the principal's safety. That logistical ability distinguishes the professional from someone who thinks EP is just escorting someone through a crowd. It also demonstrates collateral benefits and ROI, because it maximizes the principal's time and efficiency.

The agent controls risks proactively. Examples:

- choose hotels in safe areas with a good security record;
- choose primary and alternate motorcade routes that minimize danger;
- plan the walking paths for entering and leaving events, with hold rooms identified;
- plan evacuation routes at each venue;
- secure medical support and any special needs in advance.

#### International Travel Advances

Trips abroad can be riskier if the destination is unfamiliar or dangerous. The team must research from home and also travel to the destination beforehand. First: determine whether the trip is really necessary.

**“Know before you go” steps:**

1. Research current data on the destination online.
2. Obtain a professional country report: history, current affairs, and attitude toward the executive's country. There are government and commercial sources.
3. Learn the climate, health conditions, public services, time zone(s), and exchange rate.
4. Learn the essentials of social customs.
5. Clarify why he or she is traveling, how he or she wants to travel, and who will attend the meetings. If there are other prominent figures, see whether the principal will absorb part of their risk.

**Steps before departure:**

1. Conduct an advance mission to the destination.
2. Contact local security or police and the embassy or consulate of the executive's country.
3. Conduct a risk assessment of the destination.
4. Book strategically (safest lodging and transportation) and discreetly (without announcing the trip to potential adversaries).
5. Manage travel documents: visas, passports, and itineraries.
6. Rehearse, at least mentally, security on every possible means: commercial and private aircraft, cars, boats, ships, and trains.
7. Review personal safety tips with the executive.
8. Review health: bring health supplies and information; prepare or update the medical emergency plan; have hospitals, trauma centers, medical transport, and suitable doctors located.
9. Research local travel and vaccination rules, especially if there is a pandemic or other health emergency.
10. Assess network and cybersecurity vulnerabilities, and whether clean devices (laptops, tablets, smartphones) are needed.
11. Download travel security apps from the organization's provider and/or from reliable sources, such as the U.S. Department of State or the Red Cross.
12. Practice good cyber hygiene: avoid public Wi-Fi and consider a virtual private network (VPN) to access work systems.

**During the trip, three-part security concept:**

1. keep a low profile;
2. stay away from problem areas and situations;
3. know what to do if a problem arises.

In addition, the specialist must communicate often with the security base.

**High-risk regions** (designated by the U.S. Department of State or another authority). Places where Westerners gather must be avoided. Terrorists look for sites with a secure concentration of Americans or Westerners at a specific time.

- E.g.: religious services at churches frequented by Westerners. An international church is full every Sunday morning.
- The same goes for nightclubs and other places for Americans and Western Europeans.
- If the Department of State advises avoiding them, it is best to heed it.

**Key terms of a security advance:**

| Term | Definition |
| --- | --- |
| Advance team | Person or group that goes ahead of the principal and the protection team. Plans and verifies the advance plan according to the vulnerability and risk assessments |
| Vulnerability assessments | Also called intelligence reports or risk, threat, and vulnerability assessments (RTVA). Information on the risks and vulnerabilities the plan must mitigate. E.g.: without modern medicine on site, agents bring medical support |
| Site surveys | Complete site information: room layout, the principal's path, and emergency services (generators, emergency exits, response times) |
| Remain overnight/hotel security plan | Extra protocols if he or she stays overnight: overnight security plan; general assessment of the lodging; emergency evacuation or relocation plans; communication plan between principal and overnight team |
| Motorcade routes | Primary and alternate routes according to local traffic. They are rehearsed one or two days before, at the same time of the transfer. Includes a manifest of who is going or may go and the general seating assignment |
| Arrival/departure areas | Primary and alternate points at each venue. Contingency plans say what to do if the primary is not available and when to switch to the alternate |
| Path of travel | Walking route at each venue. E.g.: exit the vehicle, hallway to the hold room, left side, stage, exit left, press room, hallway back, motorcade |
| Access control list | People authorized to enter the site, the backstage area, or another area where the principal will be |
| Run of show | Schedule or itinerary of any event the principal attends or participates in |
| Trip itinerary | Complete itinerary: flight times, manifests, arrival and departure times for events, guests, or people in the motorcades |

**Exam note:** in this box the book equates “vulnerability assessments” with risk, threat, and vulnerability assessments (RTVA). In 5.3.2 it treats them as three distinct assessments.

Many large organizations use a standard format or checklist, with rules for when and how to present it. The formal advance plan becomes the playbook for the event, appearance, or trip. That way, large teams at several events and locations follow the same rules. The principal receives the same level of service everywhere, which gives consistency and confidence.

Site advances are essential on prominent trips or events, especially if the principal is a keynote speaker or the event is highly publicized. They are also useful on personal trips, such as a family vacation. At lower-risk private events they can be greatly reduced, but their basic principles are kept:

- know where the clients will be;
- how to communicate with them;
- what emergency capabilities there are at each location;
- how to evacuate and/or activate emergency protocols.

#### Close Protection Teams

They are the final layer of physical protection for the principal. They are the ones who walk in front of and around prominent figures in the news. In the U.S., the typical example is Secret Service agents next to the president or vice president, in suits, sunglasses, and earpieces.

- Several agents are not always needed. In corporate or non-government settings a low-profile team that goes unnoticed is preferred.
- The layers of security must be anticipated in advance planning.
- Facing a prominent event, the team leader: (1) reviews the vulnerability and risk assessments of the event, the location, and the principal; (2) reviews the advance plan; (3) decides how many agents are needed that day or event.
- That decision combines the assessments with the organization's close protection standards.

**Key terms of close protection:**

| Term | Definition |
| --- | --- |
| Team leader | Ultimately responsible for protection on a day or event; directs the agents' movements. A large team can have several, who take turns. Directs the choreography: moving closer, giving space, getting ahead to the next event |
| Detail leader | Responsible for the entire detail, including rules and procedures. Team leaders usually report to him or her; in small teams one person can fill both roles |
| Formations | They depend on the venue, the size of the event, and other factors that set the distance to the principal (see next table) |
| “Loose” protection | Discreet style for small private events or vacations where the principal does not want a visible team. Agents set apart, plenty of space, or nontraditional formations. The principal is clearly informed of the risk assumed and it is agreed when to return to closer protection |
| Protective surveillance | Also called covert protection; it has gained ground in recent years. It is an additional layer or a substitute for close protection, and distinct from it, even from “loose.” Teams held back or on the outer perimeter that warn of out-of-the-ordinary activity |
| Security driver vs. protection agent | Many agents are cross-trained, but one is designated as driver that day or assignment. He or she stays at the wheel while the principal is at the event, for a quick evacuation. If he or she does double duty inside the event, the team will not be ready for exits or evacuations |

**Formations:**

| Formation | Agents | Description and use |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Box formation | At least four | Traditional box around the principal. For moving through large crowds |
| Diamond formation | Generally four | Variant of the box, with agents in front of and behind the principal. For large crowds, narrow spaces, and when an agent is needed at the “point” in front |
| V formation | Small team | One agent in front of the principal and one or two at the sides |
| Multiple formations | Variable | They are combined according to the environment. The team or detail leader decides in advance or on the fly. Each agent must know the formations, how to take his or her position, and his or her function in each |

**Keys for a close protection team to work well:**

1. clear, constant communication;
2. a standardized level of training;
3. train together as much as possible;
4. clear, consistent security plans, reviewed before each day, event, or trip.

**Mental preparation and resources:**

- Specialist and executive must prepare mentally to anticipate threats.
- The program's strengths and resources must be cataloged so they can be used when needed.
- The program's vulnerabilities must be identified to better predict the adversary's likely approach.
- Strengths are combined. E.g.: if a brave, intelligent, strong agent does not master defensive driving, a professional driver is hired or he or she is trained.
- Successful EP is above all an intellectual labor. That is why anyone who is intelligent, trained, and in good physical shape can protect anyone.

**Practicing reactions.** A threat or attack breaks out suddenly, with no time for a considered reaction. That is why one must practice constantly:

- physically: protective movements, quick escapes, driving, or shooting;
- mentally: always ask “what if?” and think through the reaction.

That way the agent reacts immediately, because the reasoning is already done. Assaults and assassination attempts begin and end with enormous speed. Being mentally prepared is worth more than any other precaution.

**“Working the principal”:** the choreography with which the specialist moves physically alongside the principal.

- EP specialists are not fighters, bodyguards, or soldiers. Their job is not to take down, arrest, or kill the aggressors.
- Their main job is to avoid dangerous people or conditions: fire, street crime, or public embarrassment.
- Faced with a possible assassin: (1) move the principal away from the danger; (2) cover him or her with the body (shield); (3) get him or her out of the area as soon as possible.
- Do not stay to fight unless there is no alternative.
- To blend in, the specialist can use a title such as “CEO's assistant” and stay to one side doing countersurveillance.
- Rule of thumb: be close enough to protect, but not so close that he or she has to be introduced.

| Concept | Example from the book |
| --- | --- |
| Getting the client out of trouble | On seeing a disturbance that may turn violent, the agent puts the principal in the car and moves away quickly |
| Keeping an executive out of trouble | Agent and principal alert each other with a neutral phrase or a visual signal that it is time to leave |

**Security–convenience continuum.** Security and convenience are at opposite ends. Moving toward one end moves away from the other to the same degree. More security requires sacrificing convenience; more freedom implies less security.

- Neither extreme, total security or total convenience, is practical.
- Principal and agent decide where to position themselves on the continuum and what concessions to make.
- Past a certain point, more security hampers the executive and makes him or her less effective. He or she becomes a “victim of protection” instead of a victim of an attack.

**Exam note:** the book says that at the maximum security end there is also “the greatest degree of risk.” That contradicts its own explanation: at that end the risk is minimal. Remember: maximum security = minimum convenience.

**Protective equipment is not enough.** Firearms, alarms, armored cars, cameras, and two-way radios are useful. But neither one nor all together guarantee protection, for two reasons:

1. Relying too much on technology locks the principal in a “vault.” The executive needs to move; isolated, he or she is no longer an executive but a prisoner.
2. Adversaries usually know how to defeat the equipment: bypass alarms, disable armored cars, or listen to the radios.

Equipment can buy defensive time. But when the adversary attacks, success depends on the agent's conditioned responses to get the principal out of danger.

- Almost no shootout in the EP field has lasted more than a few seconds.
- In all presidential assassination attempts in the U.S., the Secret Service followed its “cover and evacuate” model. It did not choose to return fire.
- In a crisis, using your head matters more than weapons or equipment.

**Exam note:** that the Secret Service never returned fire is inaccurate. In the attack on Truman at Blair House (1950), White House agents and police officers did fire. For the exam, keep the book's idea: “cover and evacuate.”

#### Protective Details in High-Threat Environments

Protective security details (PSD) are also called personal security detachments, personal security details, or personal protection details. They are teams that protect high-risk personnel, normally in high-threat environments.

- The term has existed for decades. It originated with the protection of generals and dignitaries abroad.
- It spread in the early 21st century with private teams in conflict zones such as Iraq and Afghanistan.
- Those early deployments were barely regulated. There were public incidents, abuses, and other misconduct, later tried in foreign courts.
- Audits by the governments that hired PSDs gave rise to international guidelines.
- Among them, those cited in the ASIS/ANSI (American National Standards Institute) standard *Management System for Quality of Private Security Company Operations – Requirements with Guidance*, revised in 2017.

#### 4.3 Transportation (5.4.3)

**Flight operations.** High-profile people usually fly on private aircraft. Advantages over commercial flights:

1. manage their own flight itinerary;
2. control the passenger manifest;
3. express or expedited arrival and departure;
4. greater privacy;
5. ability to work effectively in transit.

The productive time gained alone usually justifies the investment. Some see it as frivolous. A good EP program defends it on grounds of more productivity and less risk, which translate into more profitability for shareholders.

Ways to operate: own the aircraft, lease them, share ownership with other organizations, or charter them.

**Fixed base operations (FBO).** Executive flight operations facility where aircraft are housed between flights.

- They are usually operated by third parties. Some companies with large flight departments run their own home-base FBO.
- Third-party FBOs support moving aircraft to other FBOs away from the base, called “foreign.”
- A full-service FBO may offer: on-site security, tarmac support, mechanics, repair, catering, and cleaning.
- Many are inside larger hubs, such as municipal airports. Others are independent of government facilities.
- Independent ones may have less scrutiny than a public airport on perimeter and access control.
- They make it easy to go from car to plane, sometimes by vehicle to the aircraft. They also speed up customs and border control.

**Driver services, two forms:**

| Type | Origin | Focus |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Chauffeur | In-house drivers or outside livery companies | Customer service and efficient, safe transfer. More attention to professional image and pride in the work than to security |
| Security driver | The book does not specify | Specific training in defensive driving and in protective operations, to integrate into the program |

**Perception of “unnecessary luxury.”** It worsened in the 2008 recession. Several executives even testified before the U.S. Congress after traveling by private plane and private transport. That view ignores the benefits of the driver:

- better security and better time management;
- the principal works in transit and arrives on time, without hunting for the address or parking;
- less risk for the organization, by taking the high-net-worth person out of the driver's seat;
- less personal risk: the principal is dropped off at the most efficient point, such as the main entrance, instead of parking and walking.

**Ideally: a security driver and, in addition, an EP agent.** If the agent also drives, he cannot watch the route. He may also have to drop off the principal and then park. That leaves the principal alone at arrivals and departures, the critical moments.

- **Denis Voronenkov case (March 2017):** his bodyguard dropped the former Russian lawmaker off in front of the Premier Palace hotel and went to park. Meanwhile, an assassin shot him dead.

**The executive's vehicle:**

- roomy interior, for the executive, the specialist, and the necessary equipment;
- large protective mass, for ramming;
- powerful engine, to escape the attackers.

The risk assessment decides whether armoring is needed. Almost any car can be armored after it is manufactured. Some makes offer factory-armored vehicles. Their advantage: they look like other vehicles and do not draw attention to the principal.

**Features of an armored vehicle:**

1. metal panels and bullet-resistant glass;
2. run-flat tires;
3. explosion-proof fuel tank;
4. front bumper reinforced with steel for ramming;
5. electric dead bolt locks;
6. dual battery system;
7. interior/exterior intercom;
8. remote start.

**Other features of an EP car, armored or not:**

- trunk release from the inside, useful for escaping (already standard in many new cars);
- global positioning system (GPS), to avoid getting lost;
- locking gas cap;
- mobile phone;
- protected exhaust pipe;
- electronic assistance system, such as On-Star;
- alarm.

**Key practices during transport:**

- Call the central security office at the start of the trip, with the route and time. E.g.: "We are leaving the plant for the office; it is 3:15 p.m.; we are taking I-67 toward U.S. 20."
- That call lets security or the police reconstruct the journey if the car disappears.
- Use discreet or coded language to say who is traveling and where, in case someone is listening.
- Thoroughly inspect the car whenever it has been out of sight and unattended.
- Because inspection takes time, keep it in a locked, alarmed garage whenever possible.
- An inspected car stays "sterile" only if it is kept locked or watched.

**The route (based on the advance):**

- fast, avoiding dangerous areas, with the minimum number of stops;
- several known alternate routes;
- safe havens for stops along the way;
- locations of hospitals, police stations, and other vital resources along the route;
- times to each leg, likely traffic, road conditions, construction or detours, drawbridge openings, and other temporary conditions;
- do the advance at the same time of day as the trip, to see actual traffic;
- additional precaution: drive the advance route in a different vehicle from the one that will carry the principal.

**Exam note:** in this section the book writes "*EPS specialist*" without defining the acronym. It refers to the same executive protection specialist (*EP specialist*) as in the rest of the chapter.

#### 4.4 Special Equipment and Technical EP (5.4.4)

Close protection teams can rely on highly specialized equipment and resources. The professional must know when to use them, how to vet providers, and how to integrate them into operations.

**Digital executive protection.** Just as there are layers of physical protection, the principal needs a team that looks after his digital security.

- Digital risk causes stress, financial loss, loss of private information, and reputational damage. It can also create physical risk.
- **Doxing:** posting personal information online with malicious intent. It can expose the principal to physical danger.
- Principals and protection leaders must hire experts. They must also have contingency plans if digital security is compromised, just like evacuation plans for a site.

Possible measures:

1. ongoing training of the principal, staff, and family in online security and privacy, including how to avoid *phishing* and *spear phishing* (targeted impersonation);
2. ongoing audits of home and company networks;
3. ongoing audits and reviews with family and staff on good social media practices: not revealing too much and keeping privacy high;
4. a protocol for taking devices to places with high cyber risk. E.g.: using a separate device, with no intellectual property, when traveling to those places.

**Technical security countermeasures (TSCM).** The practice of "sweeping" places for listening devices. It derives from government practices to protect classified information.

- More and more companies and individuals offer it, alone or as part of a service package.
- It can take several hours and be highly technical.
- The close protection leader decides whether it is needed together with other stakeholders.
- Good practices go beyond the technical: training principals, staff, and family in protecting private information.
- E.g.: not discussing sensitive topics in hotel lobbies, restaurants, or other public spaces where it is not known who is listening.

**When TSCM may be required:**

1. monitoring corporate spaces where sensitive intellectual property or other matters harmful to the company or the principal are recorded, overheard, or leaked;
2. monitoring private spaces to ensure the privacy of the principal and family;
3. ensuring that certain objects, such as gifts from strangers, do not carry audio or video devices;
4. ensuring the privacy of hotel rooms and event spaces where the principal is known to be;
5. ensuring the privacy of vehicles and/or aircraft where the principal handles sensitive information.

**Exam note:** the book develops TSCM in two ways. In 5.3.1 it writes *technical surveillance countermeasures*; in 5.2.1 and 5.4.4, *technical security countermeasures*. The standard form of the acronym is *technical surveillance countermeasures*.

**K-9 sweeps.** In close protection, dogs are often used to detect explosives, as a layer of physical protection. When to consider them:

- at times of high risk;
- before events with large crowds, such as major sporting events or concerts;
- at high-risk locations;
- as an ongoing measure at places recognized as high-value targets.

**Other services.** Covert surveillance, specialized driving techniques, or even training in a sport the principal practices. Before hiring them, it is advisable to have the agreement of stakeholders in the organization: legal, finance, and vendor management.

### Key Terms
