Rowley Training Center · protective service operations (PSO) · route surveys · vehicle ambush recognition · emergency medical technician (EMT) · tabletop exercises · de-escalation · residential security · crime prevention through environmental design (CPTED) · back-up and rollover · shadowing · roving posts · face page · force multiplier · security advances · hold rooms · know before you go · clean devices · virtual private network (VPN) · advance team · risk, threat, and vulnerability assessments (RTVAs) · site surveys · remain overnight/hotel security plan · motorcade routes · arrival/departure areas · path of travel · access control list · run of show · trip itinerary · playbook · close protection teams · team leader · detail leader · box formation · diamond formation · V formation · point · "loose" protection · protective surveillance · covert protection · security driver vs. protection agent · working the principal · what if? · security–convenience continuum · cover and evacuate · protective security details (PSDs) · personal security detachments · Management System for Quality of Private Security Company Operations · flight operations · chartering · foreign FBO · chauffeur · livery · security driver · ramming · factory-armored vehicles · run-flat tires · sterile vehicle · safe havens · digital executive protection · doxing · spear phishing · technical security countermeasures · sweeping · K-9 sweeps

## Capítulo 6 — Security Awareness (págs. 219–232)

**Central idea:** Security awareness seeks conscious attention to the security program, not teaching skills. Each group (executive management, middle management, supervisors, employees, and nonemployees) needs a different approach. The chapter covers its purposes, how to design and deliver it, its obstacles, how to measure it, and how to involve employees in preventing losses.

### 1. Definition and Basic Concepts (introduction)

- **Definition:** awareness that a security program exists, of its relevance, and of the effect of one's own conduct on reducing risks.
- It is a **continuing attitude** that can move people to act in support of the organization's security.
- Two factors increase its success: linking the security strategy with the organization's strategy (showing how it supports the mission) and involving asset owners.

| Concept | What it does |
| --- | --- |
| Education | Conveys general knowledge. |
| Training | Develops specific skills. |
| Awareness | Seeks conscious attention. |

- Aware employees and nonemployees act as a **force multiplier** for the program.
- A **security culture** makes each person actively protect their own safety and the organization's assets (ASIS International 2020).

### 2. Levels of Awareness (6.1)

- Each category of employees and visitors needs a different level.
- Under enterprise security risk management (ESRM), the levels are decided by the **stakeholders**, with guidance from security professionals.

| Level | How it views security | What to focus awareness on |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Executive management (6.1.1) | CEOs, chief operating officers, and other senior officers decide on risk and resources. If they see the program as a cost with no return, they may cut or eliminate it. | Whether the program is worth it: (1) its benefits and (2) how reasonable its costs are against those benefits. For them, awareness = knowing the program's **financial contribution** to the bottom line. |
| Middle management (6.1.2) | They answer for their department's success and value security for its contribution to that goal. If they believe it does not support their unit's goals, they do not support it; the result is mismatches and tensions that cause failures elsewhere in the company. | Show that it supports their goals. They must demonstrate commitment and set an example, just like executive management. |
| First-line supervision (6.1.3) | They handle specific processes or activities: they see whether security helps or hinders their objectives. They receive most security complaints first; if there are many, they feel it consumes too much of their time. | Show that the time spent on compliance is worth it: it supports their core tasks and protects employees and the business. Remind them that they are the closest link to employees. |
| Individual employees (6.1.4) | Crucial to facility security every day. | Detect intruders who piggyback on their credentials or who use social engineering. In information technology (IT) they can leave the network vulnerable. Security is everyone's responsibility. |
| Nonemployees (6.1.5) | Vendors, suppliers, customers, service personnel, government representatives, and the public. They have less opportunity to learn the requirements. | At least some requirements. They comply more if the reason why is explained, even briefly or in advance. |

**Executive management (6.1.1), additional points**

- Their support and priority are crucial to the program's success.
- They must reinforce a culture in which everyone understands their security role and takes ownership of their own security. They must set an example of compliance and commitment.
- Without that commitment, a **culture of complacency** can arise, with unethical conduct and violation of security policies.

**Middle management (6.1.2): the laboratory case**

- The head of a sensitive research laboratory believes the requirements are unnecessary and allows the free exchange of information.
- Over time, the internal spread of sensitive data can end in unauthorized disclosure and loss of competitive advantage.
- Lesson: that possibility alone should lead him to support security, as long as the requirements do not harm the research.

**First-line supervision (6.1.3): cases and legal liability**

- Case of the head teller at a retail bank: may refuse to use bait money for silent holdup alarms at the teller windows, because it lengthens the end-of-day count.
- If an employee complains to the supervisor about an unsafe condition, conduct, or practice (for example, someone threatens violence or the parking lot is very dark at night), the situation becomes **foreseeable** and the company is **on notice**, even if the supervisor does not escalate it.
- The company must have training and procedures so that the supervisor recognizes the complaint and mitigates or escalates it.
- If an incident occurs, these questions will be asked:
  - What was done to mitigate the problem?
  - Did the supervisor address the reported vulnerability? If not, why not?
- If an injury or death was foreseeable and preventable, and the employer did not act despite being on notice, the company is exposed to a lawsuit.

**Individual employees (6.1.4), additional points**

- Most modern approaches to motivation assume the employee is willing and interested: he needs information and instruction, not coercion or pressure.
- In many companies, the only formal contact with the program is a brief mention on the first day. That conveys that security does not matter.
- If supervisors and managers disapprove of the program or show no interest, employees do not support it. If they support it, employees view it favorably and comply.
- Strategy: link the program with the employee's personal priorities and goals. Example: good cyber hygiene at work teaches them to protect their personal information at home and elsewhere.

**Nonemployees (6.1.5), additional points**

- Example: a vendor who will access sensitive proprietary information must know the procedures that protect and control it.
- Sometimes awareness is formalized with a **confidentiality or nondisclosure agreement**.
- Example: a visitor may not see the point of wearing the guest badge. He changes his mind if it is explained that the badge lets employees recognize him and help him right away.

### 3. Purposes of Awareness (6.2)

A person who receives awareness is better prepared for these twelve purposes:

1. **Protect the company's assets:** this is the **main purpose**; it teaches people to help protect assets and reduce losses. Everything else derives from it. It involves everyone in protecting people, property, and other assets.
2. **Understand the relationship between security and successful operations:** professionals must devote time and talent to demonstrating the program's value and profitability. A crucial point for **executive management**.
3. **Identify their obligations within the program:** for employees and nonemployees, presented as reasonable and necessary. They are taught through initial orientation and periodic refreshers.
4. **Recognize the connection between the program's objectives and the chosen measures:** important for **middle management**. Unit heads must take part in the measures and recognize (preferably accept) that they are appropriate and necessary.
5. **Know the sources of help:** the materials must cover how to apply the requirements. Example: if a rule requires locking certain spaces or containers, the employee must know how to get a padlock and key or access to the lock. If he does not know whom to turn to, he improvises or consults the wrong person and is delayed.
6. **Meet legal notice requirements** (by statute or *common law*), for employees and nonemployees:
   - Civil trespass to land: in general, unauthorized entry or presence on real property. To recover damages, the owner or responsible party must prove that the intruder intended to trespass. Physical, verbal, and symbolic indicators must make clear the boundary that may not be crossed.
   - Trade secret: the owner must take positive action to prevent its disclosure. One such action is telling the employees who know it that the information is secret and valuable.
   - Creating programs to give those notices and documenting them is part of awareness.
7. **Meet regulatory requirements:** in the U.S., employee orientation is required by the **Bank Protection Act of 1968**, Federal Reserve System regulations, and the requirements of the “Controller of the Currency.” Other agencies that require training and awareness: Drug Enforcement Administration, Department of Transportation, and Nuclear Regulatory Commission.
8. **Meet contractual obligations:**
   - U.S.: the **National Industrial Security Program Operating Manual** (contractor obligations regarding classified defense information) requires briefings, education and training, including awareness.
   - Collective bargaining agreements: often require that dismissal be for just cause and that employees be notified of the rules, including security rules.
   - Insurance (for example, kidnapping): requires adopting specific procedures and communicating them to designated officials.
   - Contract for use of another company's proprietary information: may require awareness training for the employees who use it.
9. **Comply with company policies and procedures:** for example, access control or “clean desk” and “locked workspace” initiatives to protect proprietary information.
10. **Prepare the organization for emergencies and business continuity:** it responds better to emergencies and nonroutine matters, such as reports of suspicious persons or fire alarms.
11. **Reduce the organization's legal liability:**
    - In a lawsuit it helps to show that the company is security-conscious and strives for a safe environment (Ahrens 2006).
    - Effective programs hold employees accountable for their actions (Whitman 2004).
    - A criminal charge against an employee is more likely to succeed if the company proves an effectively communicated program (Kovacich 2017).
12. **Communicate the value of the security department:** without trying to scare management or employees; showing the value of security to the mission.

**Exam note:** the correct title is **Comptroller** of the Currency (Office of the Comptroller of the Currency), not “Controller.”

**Exam note:** in U.S. tort law, intent to enter is enough for trespass; knowing that one is trespassing is not required. The idea the book wants to convey is different: the boundary must be clearly marked (notice).

### 4. Developing and Delivering an Awareness Program (6.3)

The program is designed according to the organization's needs. Departments and levels may need different content, but **all** employees must understand:

1. How security supports the organization's mission.
2. Why the organization needs protection strategies.
3. What value those strategies provide.
4. The organization's code of conduct.
5. What actions the protection of specific assets requires.
6. How to identify indicators of risk or danger, how to react, and whom to notify.

Possible specific topics (ASIS 2020; the book says the list is not exhaustive):

- Client or customer engagement requirements.
- Confidential reporting.
- Personnel security policies and procedures.
- Bullying and harassment.
- Workplace violence prevention.
- Travel security procedures.
- Personnel management.
- Physical security policies and procedures.
- Access control, visitor management, and other physical protection systems.
- Reporting of suspicious behavior or conditions.
- Emergency reporting or response.
- Security incident reporting and response management.
- Information security policies and procedures.
- Credential and password management.
- Records retention and data management.
- Social engineering, scams, *phishing*, and other online threats.
- Mobile device security techniques.
- Safe browsing.
- Incident management.

Difference from detailed training:

- Awareness material may not include task-specific information. It can refer to content available elsewhere and focuses on building support for the program.
- It must be engaging and interesting: the best tools involve staff and entertain them (Gips 2006).

#### 4.1 Techniques, Materials, and Resources (6.3.1)

Five common techniques and materials:

| Technique | Content and examples | Notes |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Written material | Instructional or advisory material, agreements and acknowledgments of receipt; written policies and procedures, posters, and informal reminders. Distributed as manuals, brochures, and department guides, or within other departments' materials. Formats: pocket cards, desk reference material, calendars, fact sheets, notepads, bookmarks, letter openers, mugs, pencils, rulers, key chains, newsletters, posters, refrigerator magnets, and stickers. | Can be integrated into the materials of professional organizations. Morris case (2002): a department added its material to the *Annual Patient Nursing Assessment for Private Duty Patients*, which deals with violence prevention. |
| Audiovisual material | Audio and video files, interactive CD-ROMs, films, software presentations, e-mail, and one's own and others' websites. | Do not post sensitive information where it is publicly accessible. |
| Formal security briefings | Before and after hiring, at orientation to a new assignment, and at promotions or transfers. | Individual or group. |
| Integration into line operations | Review awareness in performance evaluations, consider it when setting bonuses, and reinforce it at supervisor and manager meetings. Include security tasks in job descriptions, manuals, and employee standards. | It is advisable to collaborate with other departments. |
| Drills and exercises | Life safety drills, simulations, role-playing, and exercises: they give the employee confidence. | In cybersecurity, *phishing* and other attacks are simulated to see whether employees are vulnerable. |

Guides and checklists can cover 14 topics: organization assets, personal security, travel security, protection of information assets, terrorist threat, protection of classified material, counterintelligence, cybercrime, personnel security, foreign intelligence threats, operational security, responsible use of company equipment, access control procedures, and possible penalties for violating security rules.

Support for creating the program:

- Internal: the company's training and communications staff.
- External experts in communications, advertising, and public relations: they provide knowledge, experience, and credibility.
- External partners as guest speakers: police (incident response), security system vendors (system operation), and behavior experts (workplace violence prevention).

Delivery methods:

- Variety is needed: some people learn well on their own with computer-based instruction; others, in class.
- Review each method for suitability, relevance, and compliance with legal and contractual obligations.
- COVID-19 case (2020): with much of the workforce at home, programs moved to online platforms. Topics changed too: business travel required less support and home-office cybersecurity became vital. Lesson: be flexible and adapt the program.

#### 4.2 Obstacles to an Effective Program (6.3.2)

Employee and management support is not automatic. Six obstacles (Roper 2006):

| Obstacle | Causes or manifestations |
| --- | --- |
| Low credibility of the security department | Staff's prior performance; new department with no track record; prejudices brought from other organizations; lack of professionalism; security staff who do not understand the company's functions. |
| Organizational culture | Negative “we've never done it that way” culture or mindset. Understanding how security contributes to success is key to a positive culture; if executive management does not understand it, implementing the program will be difficult. |
| Naiveté | Believing nothing bad will happen to them, especially if they were never victims. Believing employees will always protect assets and never knowingly harm the organization. Mistaken conclusion: the program is unnecessary. |
| Perception of a minimal threat | The employee believes the threat is insignificant or unlikely; that their assets are not a credible target and nobody would want to steal them. |
| Departmental or employee indifference | They do not see security as their responsibility: they are overloaded, have another agenda (for example, they steal from the company), or see it as unwanted extra work. Some believe protecting assets is only the security department's job. |
| Lack of reporting capability | An effective reporting system is essential. Its mere existence increases awareness and the number of reports (Kellogg 2006). With it, security can measure its effectiveness and report to executive management. |

#### 4.3 Measuring the Program (6.3.3)

- Awareness costs time and money and can briefly interrupt work. For that reason it may require management approval to start and continue.
- The organization must set metrics to measure the program and document its value.

Eight possible metrics:

1. Company losses before and after implementing the program.
2. Number of people briefed and sessions held in specific periods.
3. Topics covered, planned or actual completion dates, and delivery methods.
4. Cost of the session per employee.
5. Analysis of opinion surveys after the activities or individual study.
6. Suggestions for improvement from attendees.
7. Analysis of the response to emergencies and incidents.
8. Evaluation of regulatory and contractual compliance.

New program with no effectiveness data:

- Start with a limited budget and gain momentum over time.
- Create inexpensive material with desktop publishing.
- Partner with other departments, such as human resources, to inform employees during their regular training.
- Gather and evaluate data until the time comes to expand the program. Chapter 2 covers low-budget training options.

### 5. Involving Employees in Loss Prevention (6.4)

- Employees are among the most important stakeholders. Everyone must help protect assets, because security cannot be everywhere or see everything.
- Working with other departments and leading protection strategies increases cooperation. An involved employee is less likely to circumvent rules and measures.
- Knowing that a program exists is **not the same** as having an active role in loss prevention. Every department and employee helps identify, prevent, and reduce losses.
- Before designing the program, the security manager must know all elements of the business: which assets and processes to protect and from what risks.
- Losses that employees help reduce:
  - Traditional physical: theft of money or merchandise, misuse of equipment or facilities.
  - Others: contractual, legal, regulatory, insurance, and similar.
- Small losses can be costly. Example: stealing a laptop with a customer's personal data can cost a great deal, especially under the new privacy laws.

#### 5.1 Positive Security Contacts (6.4.1)

- Success depends on employee cooperation. To strengthen it, the department must maximize positive (helpful) contacts without neglecting its main mission.
- One avenue: promoting the personal safety of employees and their families at work, at home, and elsewhere. Eight examples:
  1. Home protection workshops.
  2. Lending of property-marking devices.
  3. Group purchases of anti-theft and fire devices.
  4. Personal protection programs.
  5. Cybersecurity awareness programs.
  6. Children's fire prevention poster contests, with prizes.
  7. Child identification kits for employees.
  8. Hazard preparedness programs, with kits handed out.
- Employees themselves can suggest other programs.
- Communication must be **two-way**: management guides and employees ask questions.
- To encourage it, security education must be light, nonpunitive, and appealing. That tone also mitigates **security fatigue**.

#### 5.2 Understanding Policies and Procedures (6.4.2)

- One of the most important missions of awareness is to make policies and procedures known.

| Concept | Function |
| --- | --- |
| Policy | Sets the rules. |
| Procedure | Explains how to comply with those rules. |

- Awareness helps spread them and helps the employee know what to do and what not to do in many situations. Not knowing them or not following them has serious and costly consequences.
- **Supermarket case (CBC News 2004):** two clerks and a uniformed guard detained an alleged thief. He died of accidental restraint asphyxia. The employees did not know the store's policy prohibiting the use of force on thieves. Lesson: a good policy is not enough; training, refreshers, and exercises are needed. Store managers, loss prevention professionals, security managers, and human resources must monitor incidents and retrain anyone who does not follow policies.
- Some fail to comply because they do not understand what they must do; others, because they decide not to cooperate.
- Two categories of users who violate IT policies (Mallery 2007):
  1. **Uneducated users:** they understand little about computers and the consequences of ignoring policies.
  2. **Arrogant users:** they believe other people's rules do not apply to them; they feel more powerful, intelligent, and sophisticated than everyone else.
- Anyone who refuses to comply, even after awareness, **must be disciplined**. Otherwise the company is needlessly exposed to losses and liabilities.

**Exam note:** the text cites “Mallery 2007,” but the chapter's reference list gives Mallery 2008. Also, Kellogg 2006, Gips 2006, and Morris 2002 are cited in the text but do not appear in that list.

### Key Terms

security awareness · continuing attitude · force multiplier · security culture · asset owners · enterprise security risk management (ESRM) · stakeholders · executive management · bottom line · culture of complacency · middle management · first-line supervision · bait money · silent hold-up alarm · foreseeable and preventable · individual employees · piggyback · social engineering · cyber hygiene · nonemployees · confidentiality or nondisclosure agreement · guest badge · civil trespass to land · trade secret · Bank Protection Act of 1968 · National Industrial Security Program Operating Manual · just cause · clean desk · locked workspace · business continuity · liability reduction · code of conduct · written material · audiovisual material · formal security briefings · integration into line operations · drills and exercises · phishing simulation · low credibility of security department · organizational culture · naiveté · perception of a minimal threat · departmental or employee indifference · lack of reporting capability · metrics · cost of briefing per employee · desktop publishing · positive security contacts · security fatigue · policies vs. procedures · restraint asphyxia · uneducated users · arrogant users

## Capítulo 7 — Workplace Substance Abuse (págs. 233–264)

**Central idea:** Substance abuse, alcohol included, harms productivity, safety, and the company's image. The chapter explains why it occurs at work, how it progresses, and which drugs are used. It also covers how supervisors and security should act: policy, investigation, intervention, assistance programs, and drug testing.

### 1. Basic Concepts and Cost to the Employer (introduction)

- **Drug:** a chemical substance that alters the physical, behavioral, psychological, or emotional state of the person who uses it.
- **Drugs of abuse:** psychoactive (mind-altering) substances that act on the central nervous system. They impair the ability to think and to process stimuli, and distort the perception of reality.
- They include legal and illegal substances, often used socially. In this chapter **alcohol is considered a drug**.
- Abuse harms physically, mentally, or emotionally. It usually leads to greater tolerance and then to addiction or chemical dependency. It creates personal, family, and financial problems.
- A country's prosperity depends on a healthy workforce, more than on technology, industry, or natural resources.
- Social consequences: birth defects, industrial accidents, company bankruptcies, and highway deaths.
- Worldwide drug trafficking is a multibillion-dollar, cross-border industry, which operates almost entirely in cash and enforces its rules with violence.

Generally accepted effects of employee substance abuse (nine):

- decreases productivity and morale;
- increases turnover and absenteeism;
- increases accidents;
- increases insurance costs;
- increases theft and dishonesty;
- increases unnecessary use of benefits;
- decreases profits;
- increases the company's potential legal liability;
- increases the risk of negative public exposure and image.

It also robs talent, vitality, and enthusiasm, destroys teamwork, and reduces competitiveness.

**Security's role:** in large organizations it is usually handled by human resources. Even so, managers and security staff must know the problem in order to detect signs and protect the organization.

### 2. Human Cost of Abuse (7.1)

Compared with those who do not abuse, employees who abuse:

- miss more work;
- file more sick-benefit claims;
- file more workers' compensation claims;
- are more likely to be laid off or fired.

Effects on the family:

- Nonalcoholic relatives of an alcoholic use more sick leave.
- They are more prone to prolonged illness, accidents, and divorce.
- Children of alcoholics are more likely to become alcoholics.

Dysfunctional relationships: they have trouble getting along with others, withdraw from their friends, and become secretive. They spend less time at home and at work, and avoid socializing with those who do not use.

At work: they perform and create less, and are often disciplinary problems. They deny and blame others. They may be **the 20% who consume 80% of management's time**.

### 3. The Employer's Role (7.2)

Abuse, once seen as a police or government problem, is also a workplace problem. To create a drug-free workplace, leaders must do nine things:

- make a commitment;
- set goals and objectives;
- assign responsibilities;
- formulate a comprehensive policy, with input from executive management and legal counsel;
- communicate the policy;
- apply it equitably;
- provide education and training;
- help anyone who asks, including psychological support;
- audit the process regularly.

### 4. Drug Use in the Workplace (7.3)

Two reasons drugs are used at work:

1. **Rationalization:** they believe they can use without affecting their productivity, and even present it as something noble.
2. **Opportunity:** they spend several hours a day at work, where opportunities arise to use, share, and buy illegal substances.

#### 4.1 Rationalization (7.3.1)

**Definition:** use of superficial and seemingly plausible explanations or excuses to justify one's own conduct.

Typical rationalizations:

- using is a constitutional right;
- addiction and dependency only happen to others;
- the drug improves their ability to perform, produce, and create;
- they can quit whenever they want;
- using at work is acceptable because it is common;
- selling to coworkers is a gesture of camaraderie;
- what they do in their free time does not affect their performance.

Effect: they abandon their values, evade responsibilities, and lose respect for others and for their property. They may lie to family, steal from friends and employers, and use without guilt.

#### 4.2 Opportunity (7.3.2)

| Reason | Explanation |
| --- | --- |
| Users know one another | Seller and buyer have regular contact that does not arouse suspicion. The workplace is often private property, outside the direct watch of the police: a relatively safe haven. |
| Better quality | The seller wants repeat customers, and quality brings them back. |
| Fairer quantity | Drugs are expensive and sold in minimal quantities, so exact weight matters. The seller gives the correct weight to keep customers. |
| Low risk | They see bosses as uninformed, untrained, and reluctant to confront. The barriers, fences, and locked doors that protect assets also hide them from surveillance. |
| High return on investment | An ounce of high-quality cocaine, cut (diluted with an impurity) and repackaged into small doses, leaves thousands of dollars in profit. |
| Buy and sell on credit | Drugs are “fronted” (*fronting*) and paid for later, with terms and consequences for nonpayment. The seller takes a small portion of the drug for himself, called a *pinch*; the practice is called *pinching*. |

### 5. The Path of Abuse at Work (7.4)

Nobody aspires to be an addict. Typical progression:

1. **Experimentation:** a friend or relative introduces the drug.
2. **Periodic use**, often social, if the effect is pleasing.
3. **New relationships** with friends or coworkers who use the same drug. The old ones weaken and may end.
4. **Personal changes** in appearance, behavior, interests, and relationships. They become secretive, suspicious, and paranoid.
5. **Change of attitude toward the drug:** they defend its benefits, its social value, and their right to use it. They think, study, and talk about it.
6. **Job deterioration:** attendance problems with recognizable patterns, less concentration, and more “personal problems.” More frequent and irresponsible use: drinking and driving, smoking marijuana while handling firearms, using in public with strangers.
7. **Use at work:** at first discreet, then often brazen. They enjoy testing the limits.

Examples of use at work: drinking in the parking lot at lunch and breaks; smoking marijuana in restrooms and locker rooms; cocaine or methamphetamine at the desk; drugs in company vehicles or on business trips; even with customers and vendors.

Where they keep or move the drug:

- in desks, lockers, or toolboxes;
- they use the mailroom or the shipping department to distribute and receive drugs and money;
- they hide it in safes, furniture, trash cans, hazardous materials containers, beverage containers, lunch boxes, briefcases, handbags, shoes, coats, raw materials, and finished product.

They are ingenious, shrewd, and deceptive.

Profile of the employee who sells (dealer):

- may sell in secret in front of nonusers, supervisors, and managers;
- socializes more than others and makes contacts nonstop, avoiding detection;
- is often absent or away from his workstation;
- avoids management;
- does not usually cause problems, but becomes belligerent if accused;
- supports employee causes and enjoys creating conflict between management and workers;
- resists teamwork, has hidden agendas, and despises authority.

Financial escalation: available income runs out → buys on credit → credit runs out → sells drugs (especially to colleagues) or steals. **The main victim of the theft is the employer.**

Progression of theft:

1. coworkers' food;
2. petty cash, cash receipts, office equipment, and coworkers' valuables;
3. unchecked, he steals “as far as the organization will allow”: scrap, raw materials, finished product, and intellectual property (customer lists, confidential information, and trade secrets).

Theft from customers and vendors:

- shipping short to an important customer and selling the difference;
- accepting kickbacks for miscounting, allowing overages, double shipping, approving improper credits, or diverting a vendor's delivery.
- Impact: destroyed relationships; vendors who withhold service or materials; customers who cancel, do not pay, or sue.

Other costs: more accidents and injuries; more health claims and benefit use; more expensive insurance; absences that force recruiting, hiring, and training replacements; more false claims and feigned workplace injuries.

Final cycle:

- When performance falls, discipline begins. The job may be the only stable thing in their life, and staying employed “proves” to them that they live normally.
- In extreme cases they give up family, children, home, and car, but fight to keep the job.
- Facing the risk of losing it, some quit the drug. The most common response is to **feign a workplace injury** to escape discipline.
- Benefits they expect from a long absence: relief from responsibilities; getting away from the environment with the most exposure and temptation; and a chance to quit and start over. These are rarely realized.
- Without the structure of work, their life falls apart: more use and more debt. When they return months later they are more dependent, perform less, and tend to file another claim.
- The cycle can repeat several times before dismissal. In the end everyone loses: employer, spouse, family, friends, and the user himself.

### 6. Drugs of Abuse (7.5)

Legal basis of the U.S. federal strategy: the **Comprehensive Substance Abuse Prevention and Control Act of 1970, Title II (CSA)**. It has four fundamental parts:

1. mechanisms to reduce the availability of controlled substances;
2. procedures for placing a substance under control;
3. criteria for setting control requirements;
4. obligations arising from international treaties.

It regulates the manufacture, purchase, and distribution of drugs according to their abuse potential. The Drug Enforcement Administration (DEA) enforces it and oversees the classification into schedules.

**Exam note:** the law's actual name is the *Comprehensive **Drug** Abuse Prevention and Control Act of 1970*; its Title II is the *Controlled Substances Act* (CSA).

| Schedule | Abuse potential | Accepted medical use in the U.S. | Dependence it can cause | Book examples |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| I | High | None | Not indicated | marijuana, heroin, methaqualone, lysergic acid diethylamide (LSD) |
| II | High | Yes, with severe restrictions | Severe psychological or physical | cocaine, morphine, amphetamine, opium |
| III | Lower than I and II | Yes | Moderate or low physical, or high psychological | codeine, Tylenol with codeine, benzphetamine |
| IV | Lower than III | Yes | Limited physical or psychological | Darvon, Darvocet, phenobarbital, Valium |
| V | Lower than IV | Yes | Lower than that of Schedule IV | low-potency prescription cold and pain medications, common in households |

**Exam note:** codeine alone is in Schedule II. Schedule III covers combination products with little codeine, such as Tylenol with codeine.

#### 6.1 Depressants (7.5.1)

- Small doses: calm. Medical uses: inducing sleep, relieving anxiety and muscle spasms, and preventing seizures.
- Larger doses: altered reflexes, slurred speech, loss of motor coordination, and uncontrollable drowsiness.
- They are often combined with other depressants or with stimulants.
- Risks: birth defects, overdose, and death.
- They include benzodiazepines, barbiturates, and sedatives. Examples: Valium, Quaalude, Xanax, Lunesta, Nembutal, and Seconal.
- **Alcohol is classified as a depressant.**

**Alcohol:**

- A fast-acting central nervous system depressant. It acts as an analgesic with sedative effects.
- A little: well-being and slightly altered reflexes. A lot: disorientation, disinhibition, loss of coordination, and irrationality.
- It is addictive. Prolonged abuse damages the brain, liver, and heart.
- It also causes sexual dysfunction, gastritis, ulcers, malnutrition, hypertension, cirrhosis, pancreatitis, cancer, and death.

Alcohol dependence or alcoholism: four symptoms.

| Symptom | Definition |
| --- | --- |
| Craving | A strong need or compulsion to drink |
| Loss of control | Inability to limit how much one drinks |
| Physical dependence | Withdrawal symptoms (nausea, sweating, shaking, anxiety) when stopping drinking after a period of heavy use |
| Tolerance | Need to drink more to achieve the desired effect |

- The craving can be as strong as the need for food or water.
- **Key difference between a social drinker and an alcoholic:** the alcoholic loses control over when, where, and how much he drinks.
- Some recover on their own, but most need help.
- Apparent causes: genetic and environmental components.

#### 6.2 Narcotics (7.5.2)

- Small doses: effects like those of depressants. Larger doses: sleep, unconsciousness, and vomiting.
- A large dose can cause severe respiratory depression and death.
- Intravenous use increases the risk of hepatitis and AIDS.
- **Tolerance:** the analgesia, euphoria, and sedation last less time and are less intense, so larger doses are needed.
- **Dependence:** alteration of the body's normal functions; the drug is needed to avoid withdrawal.
- Short-acting narcotics: shorter, more intense withdrawal. Long-acting: prolonged but less severe withdrawal.
- In the medical sense, narcotics = opiates (also opioids): opium, its derivatives, and its synthetic substitutes. Examples: morphine, heroin, and codeine.
- They are indispensable against pain, but highly addictive. They are taken orally or intravenously, and also smoked.
- **Uncommon at work:** they are expensive and their physical effects are usually evident.

#### 6.3 Stimulants (7.5.3)

- They make the employee seem more alert, enthusiastic, and productive. In reality it is wasted effort that causes errors.
- The user believes they improve his creativity and endurance, but they rob him of energy and rationality.
- They cause frequent, severe mood swings; the user becomes hard to direct and clashes with others.
- He tries to control his mood with another drug, almost always alcohol.
- Prolonged abuse: weight loss, induced psychosis, and addiction to several drugs.
- Stimulants used at work: cocaine, amphetamines, methamphetamine, methcathinone, methylphenidate (Ritalin), and anorectics (appetite suppressants).

**Cocaine (cocaine hydrochloride):**

- A white, crystalline substance extracted from the coca plant. It has some medical use as a topical anesthetic. It is considered highly addictive.
- Most commonly snorted; it is also injected or smoked.
- Immediate effects: dilated pupils, high blood pressure, elevated heart rate, and euphoria.
- *Crack* or *rock*: powder cocaine, baking soda, and water; usually smoked. The effect lasts minutes and leaves a craving for more.
- The effects appear almost immediately and disappear in minutes or hours.
- Small doses: euphoria, energy, talkativeness, and alertness; less need to eat and sleep. Some do simple tasks faster; others, the opposite.
- Large doses: strange, erratic, or violent behavior; tremors, vertigo, muscle spasms, paranoia, or toxic reaction. Also restlessness, irritability, and anxiety.
- Deaths are usually due to cardiac arrest or to seizures followed by respiratory arrest.
- Some develop tolerance. Others become more sensitive and can die from low doses.
- Bingeing: repeated and increasing doses. It causes irritability, restlessness, and paranoia, and in the end paranoid psychosis with auditory hallucinations.

**Methamphetamine:**

- A synthetic drug easy to make from common materials and simple equipment. Names: *crank*, *meth*, *crystal meth*, or *speed*.
- In many workplaces it **has replaced cocaine** as the preferred stimulant.
- It is smoked, snorted, swallowed, or injected.
- Smoked or injected: an intense, very pleasurable “flash” lasting a few minutes. Snorted or swallowed: euphoria without that intense flash.
- Time to effect: snorted, **3 to 5 minutes**; swallowed, **15 to 20 minutes**.
- High doses: dangerous or lethal body temperature, and seizures.
- Chronic abuse: addiction, violence, anxiety, severe mood swings, weight loss, irritability, confusion, insomnia, and general deterioration.
- Psychotic effects: paranoia, auditory hallucinations, mood disturbances, and delusions. Example: feeling insects walking on the skin (*formication*).
- The paranoia can cause homicidal and suicidal ideas.

#### 6.4 Hallucinogens (7.5.4)

- They drastically alter mood, sensory perception, and reasoning.
- Those from plants and mushrooms have been used for centuries in shamanic practices. Today even more potent synthetic ones exist.
- The most abused: LSD (*acid*); MDA (methylenedioxyamphetamine); MDMA (methylenedioxymethamphetamine, ecstasy); PCP (phencyclidine, *angel dust*); mescaline, from the peyote cactus; and certain mushrooms.
- Physiological effects: elevated heart rate and blood pressure, dilated pupils.
- Sensory effects: perceptual distortions. Psychic effects: thought disturbances about time and space.
- Time seems to stop and shapes and colors change. The experience can be pleasant or terrifying.
- Vivid hallucinations, panic attacks, and synaesthesia or sensory crossover: believing one “sees sounds” or “smells colors.”
- Their effects are unpredictable with each use.
- *Flashbacks*: partial recurrences of the experience weeks or months later, without taking the drug.
- Some are neurotoxic. The most common danger is impaired judgment: rash decisions, accidents, injuries, and death.

#### 6.5 Marijuana (cannabis) (7.5.5)

- **It is the second most abused drug at work, after alcohol.**
- Small doses: effects similar to alcohol; recovering alcoholics use it as a substitute. Large doses: hallucinations, memory loss, and lethargy.
- Data from the U.S. National Safety Council (NSC) (Gates 2019): employees who tested positive for cannabis had **55% more industrial accidents, 85% more injuries, and 75% more absenteeism** than those who tested negative.
- For the NSC, **no level of use is safe or acceptable** in safety-sensitive positions, because of the risk to the operator and to others.
- The amount of detectable THC **does not correlate directly** with the degree of impairment.
- Marijuana, hashish, and hashish oil derive from hemp, *cannabis sativa*.
- Main psychoactive component: tetrahydrocannabinol (THC). It is retained in body fat and accumulates because it is not easily eliminated.
- **Reverse tolerance:** because of that accumulation, the user needs less and less of the drug for the same effect. He smokes a smaller amount, but more often.
- At work it may come mixed with other drugs, sometimes an opiate or PCP, to gain potency and sales. Doses are never consistent and the user can become addicted to what is mixed in as well.
- Forms: traditional dried flower, vaporizer pens, edibles and beverages, concentrates, sprays, topicals, and capsules.
- Hashish: a THC-rich resin that is collected, dried, and pressed into balls, cakes, or cookie-like sheets.
- Hemp flower: little or no THC; it can be legal where marijuana is not (Klein 2021).

**Legal or illegal? (U.S.)**

- Challenge in states that have legalized medical or recreational use: federal law still classifies it in Schedule I (according to the book, 2021). It can be legal in the state and illegal in the country.
- The employer must clarify its policy and educate the workforce about what it expects.
- Zero-tolerance: seems easier, but can bring complications. Some components remain in the blood up to a month later.
- Alternative: an under-the-influence policy, which treats marijuana like alcohol. One may not arrive intoxicated or use on the premises or during work hours.
- Zero tolerance is common in the **safety and health sectors**. It must be clear that the job demands a clear mind.
- Essential: a specific written marijuana policy, covering drugs that are illegal under federal and state law.
- If one does not want to get into measuring impairment, prohibit any detectable illegal drug (Chapa 2015).

#### 6.6 Analogue or Designer Drugs (7.5.6)

- A synthetic preparation with effects and characteristics similar to those of a natural substance.
- They are made in a laboratory. Because their formula differs from the one they imitate, at first they are not classified as controlled, even if the imitated one is.
- Many are much more potent than the original; some have caused overdose deaths.

#### 6.7 Prescription Drugs (7.5.7)

- They are frequently abused at work, especially stimulants and sedatives.
- A doctor prescribes them, but they are overused or kept being taken without need. Physical or psychological dependence can arise.
- Without legal access, the user turns to illegal sources or to another drug.
- Those who sell them at work often believe they are doing a favor for a friend or coworker.
- The best sellers at work: **benzodiazepines**, depressants for anxiety, tension, and spasms. Examples: Librium, Xanax, and Valium.
- Their abuse is associated with teenagers and young adults seeking to get high: less inhibition and impaired judgment.
- Mixing them with alcohol or another depressant potentiates the effect and can be fatal.

### 7. Addiction and Chemical Dependency (7.6)

#### 7.1 Addiction (7.6.1)

- **Definition:** the disease of obsession and compulsion. One can be addicted to anything; it is usually understood as the repeated, uncontrollable use of a substance or a behavior.
- The addict also becomes hooked on the social behaviors surrounding the drug: the rituals for obtaining, preparing, and using it.

Signs of addiction or abuse (Mayo Clinic 2021):

| Sign | Examples |
| --- | --- |
| Problems at work | Frequent absences, disinterest, and performance failures |
| Physical health | Lack of energy and motivation, weight gain or loss, red eyes |
| Neglected appearance | Disinterest in grooming, clothing, or appearance |
| Behavior changes | Secrecy and changes in relationships with family, friends, and coworkers |
| Money problems | Borrowing, or stealing money or objects to sell them |

Consequences as addiction advances:

- arrests and convictions for driving while intoxicated;
- hangovers and blackouts;
- dysfunctional relationships;
- confrontations with family, friends, and employers;
- disciplinary actions;
- demotions;
- dismissals;
- loss of freedom (prison);
- danger to oneself and to others.

#### 7.2 Chemical Dependency (7.6.2)

- **Definition:** an integral component of addiction. It is the physiological craving caused by chemical changes in the body, which are mental and physical.
- The craving is relieved only by using. The dependent person can lose all rationality and do anything for the drug.
- **Tolerance:** the body gets used to it and asks for ever larger doses for the same effect.
- **Withdrawal:** painful physiological responses on stopping the drug. It is the body's attempt to adapt chemically without it and can be very violent.
- Withdrawal symptoms: irritability, vomiting, tremors, sweating, insomnia, and seizures.

#### 7.3 High-Functioning Abusers (7.6.3)

- They seem to manage their dependence. But if the use is obsessive, they need the drug just to function “normally.”
- They look like everyone else: stable job, regular schedule, family, and apparent happiness.
- They lead two lives, one visible and one secret. They usually use **daily**.
- At work they seem productive; without the drug they are completely different people.

#### 7.4 Denial (7.6.4)

- **Definition:** a mental state in which the person refuses to believe or acknowledge that his behavior harms him and those around him.
- He rationalizes his behavior and minimizes its impact. He denies that the drug affects his health, work, and family, and denies his relationship with the drug and its growing cost.

Typical phrases of the user in denial:

- "I can quit whenever I want."
- "It doesn't affect me like it does others."
- "What I do in my free time is my business."
- "I've never hurt anyone."
- "I don't use enough to become an addict."
- "It doesn't affect my work."
- "My wife (or husband) doesn't mind."
- "I can control it."

Denial by friends and coworkers: they deny the problem or justify it as temporary or deserved. That way they present it as normal or expected and encourage continued use. Naive friends may even discourage therapy, treatment, or abstinence.

Denial by bosses and organizations: bosses deny the problem even in the face of obvious evidence. Organizations in denial do not create sound policies, do not enforce the ones they have, and do not respond to incidents.

Typical phrases of managers in denial:

- "We don't have a drug problem here."
- "If we had one, we would see it."
- "We're only concerned about the sellers, not the users."
- "We know some people smoke marijuana at lunch, but what harm does it do?"
- "If we enforced our policies, we wouldn't find anyone to work here."
- "This industry doesn't have that kind of problem."
- "That kind of people doesn't work here."
- "We don't need psychological support for our employees."

Out of fear of facing the truth, the organization denies help and takes part in the advance of the disease.

#### 7.5 Enabling (7.6.5)

- **Definition:** allowing or encouraging, consciously or unconsciously, another person's destructive behavior. It usually stems from denial.
- The enabler protects the user from the consequences and maintains the illusion that he is fine.
- The family enables when it calls in sick for him, makes excuses to the boss, or lies to spare him discipline. Also when it forgives broken promises again and again.
- Bosses enable when they cover for him and accept excuses for attendance or poor performance. Also when they believe rationalizations such as:
  - "I have a lot of problems at home."
  - "It won't happen again."
  - "I can control it. Just give me more time."
  - "I'm not the only one with problems here."
  - "I promise you that…" (incomplete phrase in the book).
- It is easier to enable than to face reality. Dealing with these employees is unpleasant and even frightening.
- Bosses doubt their judgment and fear for their careers, and the user takes advantage of it.

To avoid enabling, supervisors and managers must:

- know the substance abuse policy and how it is applied;
- know the symptoms of abuse and when to ask for help;
- consider open or anonymous channels that encourage reporting;
- document performance accurately;
- recognize enabling behaviors and stop them;
- communicate expectations and demand accountability;
- document efforts and results;
- communicate with executive management.

Breaking the cycle requires confronting the problem honestly.

#### 7.6 Codependency (7.6.6)

- **Definition:** letting another person's behavior eclipse one's own values and judgment; not defending what one knows is right. It almost guarantees the destruction of the relationship.

The codependent typically:

- believes he must do more work than his share to maintain the relationship;
- lives worried and absorbed by the dependence of his partner or coworker;
- fears expressing how he feels about the user;
- accepts that the user does not keep promises or commitments;
- fears disciplining an employee for fear that he will leave and have to be replaced.
- Associated feelings: anger, isolation, guilt, fear, shame, despair, and loss of control.
- By not “rocking the boat,” they give the user support to keep using.
- Roles they adopt: rescuers, caretakers, complainers, and adjusters.
- Sometimes they become outstanding performers to set an example; other times, they join in the use.

To avoid codependency, supervisors and managers must:

- focus on performance, without letting manipulation eclipse what is right;
- set limits and tolerate only what is acceptable;
- ask for help from internal resources: human resources and the employee assistance program (EAP);
- refer the employee to resources that can help him;
- document efforts and results;
- communicate with executive management.

Bosses must understand addiction and dependence and know how to recognize the three destructive behaviors. Not confronting them is a lack of compassion and cruelty.

| Destructive behavior | What it is | Who shows it according to the book |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Denial | Refusing to acknowledge the harm the behavior causes | User, friends, coworkers, bosses, and the organization |
| Enabling | Allowing or encouraging the destructive behavior, protecting the user from the consequences | Family, supervisors, and managers |
| Codependency | Letting the other person's behavior override one's own values and judgment | Partner, coworkers, and bosses |

### 8. Role of Supervisors and Managers (7.7)

#### 8.1 Drug-Free Workplace Policy (7.7.1)

A policy is absolutely necessary. It must be practical, functional, and enforceable. It must be written, well communicated, **signed for receipt by every employee**, and applied equitably.

An effective policy must:

1. State the goal of a drug-free workplace and why it matters to everyone.
2. Declare drug and alcohol abuse at work unacceptable. Prohibit the use, sale, possession, and offer of sale of controlled substances at work or during work hours.
3. Define on-the-job impairment.
4. Describe how and when testing will be done, what counts as a positive, and what happens if a sample is not provided.
5. Define what an infraction is and its consequences.
6. Recognize that abuse is treatable and explain the treatment and rehabilitation options.
7. Define the role of the EAP and how to access it.
8. Answer any question about abuse, the policy, or its enforcement.

Other points:

- **Avoid the term “under the influence.”** Only alcohol has a legal definition of that state. For other drugs there is no standard, so it cannot be proven.
- After creating the policy, allow a waiting period to educate employees.
- On the effective date, bosses declare their willingness to enforce it. It is one of the most important and least recognized deterrents.
- Bosses are not expected to “hunt” for those who use or sell. They must **watch performance** and correct it when it is not adequate.
- The U.S. Substance Abuse and Mental Health Services Administration (SAMHSA) offers guides for creating a drug-free workplace (SAMHSA 2020).

**Exam note:** internal contradiction. In 7.5.5 the book proposes an *under-the-influence* policy for marijuana; in 7.7.1 it says the policy should avoid that term.

#### 8.2 Investigation and Documentation (7.7.2)

- When a policy violation occurs, management responds quickly and well, often with an investigation.
- **Workplace investigation:** a fact-finding process, ideally separate from decision making. If it cannot be separated, the investigator must not let prior biases influence it.
- It can be simple and informal (confront the suspect and ask) or complex and formal.
- Minimum requirements: fair and impartial; fact-based and objective; thorough and well documented; and protective of the rights of suspects and witnesses.
- It must also not violate the law, policy, labor agreements, or privacy. It must be confidential: evidence, notes, reports, and conclusions only for those who need to know.
- Executive management, the legal department, and human resources must always take part.
- Discipline comes only after a detailed review of the findings by qualified management.
- If the findings do not justify discipline, the appropriate response is **intervention**.

#### 8.3 Employee Hotlines (7.7.3)

- After the corporate scandals of the late 1990s and early 2000s, organizations sought new reporting channels.
- Fear of retaliation holds back reports, even anonymous ones. Modern anonymous systems have reduced it in part.
- “Open door” policies are not enough to prevent and detect abuse.

Legal mandate:

- The Sarbanes-Oxley Act of 2002 requires every publicly traded company to provide a confidential means of anonymously reporting questionable accounting or auditing practices. According to the book, it serves employees, customers, and vendors.
- The company must ensure the “receipt, retention, and treatment” of complaints.
- Those same tools serve to obtain information about substance abuse.
- Multinational challenge: hotlines required in some countries are illegal in others. It reflects cultural attitudes toward whistleblowers.

**Exam note:** Section 301 of Sarbanes-Oxley requires anonymous, confidential submission for **employees**. Third-party complaints must be received, retained, and treated, but the law does not require anonymity for them.

Early warning systems:

- Anonymous hotlines detect all kinds of misconduct sooner, before major losses and reputational damage.
- Human resources and **security managers** have quickly seen their value for receiving reports of misconduct and substance abuse.
- Their cost is minimal compared with the losses they prevent.
- They show that the organization takes its employees and their safety seriously, and encourage action in the face of inappropriate conduct.

Advantages of outsourcing the hotline:

1. regulation may limit strictly internal mechanisms;
2. vendors have better technology;
3. they employ operators better trained to collect the key data.

#### 8.4 Intervention (7.7.4)

- **Definition:** a calculated interruption of the destructive behaviors of the user and of those around him.
- **It is not discipline.** It is a caring behavior in which one plans, prepares, and acts.
- It aims to make the user see the consequences, rescue him, and return him to work as a productive contributor.
- Requirements: documented performance; getting out of denial; not assuming he will improve on his own; not rationalizing or accepting poor performance or misconduct.
- It is often the user's only hope before discipline.

| Step | What to do |
| --- | --- |
| 1. Observe and document performance | Make sure the employee knows what is expected. Document inappropriate behaviors. Ask another boss for an opinion if in doubt. Act immediately if needed to prevent an accident or serious error. |
| 2. Confront the employee | Take him out of his area and confront him in private. Do not do or say anything that embarrasses him. |
| 3. Interview and talk | See the detail below. |
| 4. Document results | What each party said, the employee's attitude, and his response to the demand for improvement. Schedule the follow-up and make sure, in writing if necessary, that he knows the date. |
| 5. Communicate to executive management | Brief executive management and human resources thoroughly. Give them copies of notes and documents. Suggest, if appropriate, that human resources attend the next meeting. |
| 6. Follow up | Short, direct meeting, with the same attendees. Review progress. If he complied, thank and congratulate him. If not, apply the progressive action announced, set goals and a new date. |

Detail of step 3 (interview):

- Have a witness if possible, and a union representative if appropriate or required.
- If there was a prior investigation, share the appropriate information.
- Only concrete facts, never generalities. Show documentary evidence of the poor performance, such as attendance records or time cards.
- Describe in detail what is expected, citing the written policy whenever possible.
- Do not identify the investigation's witnesses unless essential.
- Do not accuse the employee or try to diagnose or rationalize his behavior.
- Give him the chance to explain himself, with an open mind but knowing he may be in denial. Document his responses.
- Ask him what the organization could reasonably do to help him. Empathize, but without committing: just listen.
- Suggest professional help, such as the EAP or community resources. Have phone numbers or brochures on hand.
- Reiterate the failures and the expectations. Define limits and concrete consequences. Example: "Your next failure will bring a final written reprimand."
- Indicate that he will continue to be monitored and when it will be reviewed. Example: "In 30 days we'll meet here to review your progress."
- Close on a positive note: he is expected to improve, he has the organization's support, and his success will be a “win-win.” Then return him to work.

Benefit: intervention corrects and shows concern, not punishment. In the long run it avoids unnecessary discipline, reduces turnover, and can save a life.

**When intervention fails:**

- Sometimes the addiction is too strong. The user may be resentful, indifferent, or angry. If he has “surrendered to the disease,” progressive discipline may be the only response.
- **Documented progressive discipline:** gradual escalation in the face of continued failures. Verbal reprimand → written reprimand → suspension → dismissal.
- Message to the user: his relationship with the drug has a cost.
- The job may be the last thing he has left, his “last bastion of normalcy.” That is why he clings to it.
- **Security risk:** he may lie, cheat, and steal to keep the job, and even resort to violence if he sees it threatened.

#### 8.5 Employee Assistance Programs (EAP) (7.7.5)

- They began in the **1940s** as occupational alcoholism programs, focused on alcohol.
- Today they cover alcohol and other substances, family problems, mental health, and other personal matters.
- They are provided by the employer and are free to the employee; they usually include family members. Both are called clients.
- Strict confidentiality: **not even the employer knows the clients' names**.
- How it works: community referral network; several telephone consultations (sometimes in person) to assess; names of several resources are given; the client decides whether to continue.
- The EAP can track progress as support, but treatment is given by outside, independent professionals. Everything is confidential.
- Health insurance may cover treatment, and leaves of absence are granted.
- In the U.S., the Americans with Disabilities Act (ADA) requires reasonable accommodation for employees and applicants **in recovery**. **Current users are not protected.**
- The EAP is a clearinghouse. It **does not investigate or do drug testing**: it only connects people with quality professional help.

| Access route | Characteristics |
| --- | --- |
| Voluntary | The employee seeks help on his own |
| Management referral | Gradual approach, like intervention and progressive discipline. Mandatory participation. Professional treatment. Management receives progress reports. |

In a management referral, after treatment (or during it):

- performance and conduct goals are negotiated;
- as a condition of employment, the employee agrees to be monitored and knows the consequences of noncompliance;
- monitoring usually includes periodic drug tests, and may include ongoing therapy;
- over time the monitoring and the condition of conditional employment are withdrawn;
- it must include a plan for reintegration into work.

#### 8.6 Role Modeling (7.7.6)

- It consists of setting an example with one's own behavior, as parents and teachers do.
- If enough people take part, the culture of the entire organization changes.
- Users prefer environments with others like them and resent the limits of a healthy culture. Above all they resent not being able to rationalize their use.
- Positive peer pressure forces them to confront their behavior: they can no longer lie or deceive.

#### 8.7 Reintegration of the Recovering Employee (7.7.7)

- Recovery is long and painful. Many return to the same environment where the abuse began.
- If the environment is controlled by users, staying clean will be difficult or impossible.
- In a healthy environment, the odds of recovery and lasting sobriety are good.
- Coworkers give encouragement, positive role models, and an environment free of temptation.
- Bosses demand accountability, set reasonable expectations, and reinforce achievements.

#### 8.8 Employee Education and Supervisor Training (7.7.8)

- Training at all levels as part of the program: accurate information on the dangers and on policies and expectations.
- Human resources must know how to identify user candidates in order to screen them out, as part of preemployment screening.
- Human resources must know all the policies, because it usually applies correction and discipline.
- Training of supervisors and managers is critical. **Only a trained health professional can diagnose** an abuse problem. Training gives bosses tools to enforce rules and policies.

### 9. Drug Testing (7.8)

- A prevention and detection tool. Preemployment testing detects possible users before hiring them.
- For active employees: for reasonable suspicion; after an accident or injury; randomly; on return after a violation; and as follow-up to treatment.
- It also deters, because it creates fear of being discovered.
- The employer has the **right and the duty** to promote a drug-free workplace.
- **Drug-Free Workplace Act of 1988 (U.S.):** companies that contract with the federal government and receive grants of more than $25,000 must certify that they have policies for a drug-free workplace.
- Other laws and regulations require periodic testing of certain transportation and public service workers.

Approach by country (Anderson 2015):

| Country or region | Situation |
| --- | --- |
| Canada | In 2013 the Supreme Court ruled that random alcohol testing of employees in safety-sensitive positions invaded their privacy |
| European Union | In some countries all testing of employees is illegal; in others it is permitted depending on the position |
| China, India, and many Latin American countries | Testing is not the norm, because fewer substances are considered illegal than in the U.S. |

#### 9.1 Methods (7.8.1)

- **Drug test:** scientific examination of a biological sample to detect a specific drug or its metabolite.
- **Metabolite:** chemical byproduct left when the body metabolizes the substance.
- Most-used sample: **urine**. Blood, hair, and saliva are also analyzed.
- Urine is preferred because its collection is not intrusive: the body does not have to be pricked, as when drawing blood.
- Collection follows protocols that protect privacy. The sample is sealed, labeled, and sent to the laboratory.
- If the preliminary test detects a drug or metabolite, a **confirmatory test** is done with more precise technology. It identifies the type of drug and its concentration.
- The confirmed positive is communicated confidentially to the employer or its representative, such as a medical review officer (MRO).
- Samples are labeled with a number, not a name: not even the laboratory knows whose they are.
- The employer's response to a positive depends on the circumstances and the policy.

#### 9.2 Accuracy (7.8.2)

- Tests are extremely accurate. A very small percentage give false positives, but that is what the confirmatory test is for.
- Laboratories are regulated and have rigorous requirements and quality assurance.
- Certification by the U.S. National Institute on Drug Abuse (NIDA) is difficult and expensive. Every sample, procedure, and test is documented, and control samples are analyzed frequently.
- NIDA data: of about **16 million** tests a year in its laboratories, **fewer than 16** come back positive with no drug present.

**Exam note:** the book calls NIDA the "National Institute on Substance Abuse"; its actual name is the *National Institute on Drug Abuse*.

#### 9.3 Strategy (7.8.3)

Many states regulate drug and alcohol testing, so differences by jurisdiction must be addressed. Questions the strategy must resolve:

**For which substances?**

- Laws, collective bargaining agreements, and contracts may limit it.
- Under U.S. federal regulations, one can test for alcohol and **five controlled substances: marijuana, cocaine, amphetamines, opiates, and PCP**.
- Some states require following the federal rules, whether or not the employer is federally regulated. Others permit more substances, or give financial incentives for including them.
- For additional substances, consider the ADA, which limits the employer's medical inquiries. It is advisable to test **only after a job offer**.
- Risk: an employee in a non-safety-sensitive position, disciplined for a substance outside the five, could file a claim under the ADA.
- Outside the U.S., consult legal counsel about what is lawful.

**When?**

- First review the applicable laws. The two most common times are **preemployment and random**.
- **Reasonable suspicion, also called for-cause:** when the supervisor has evidence or reasonable cause to suspect use.
- Triggers: physical evidence of substances, patterns of erratic or abnormal behavior, disorientation, or inability to perform routine tasks.
- Because it is discretionary, supervisors must be trained to apply it consistently across the entire workforce.

**What type of sample?**

- Urine, saliva, blood, hair, or breath. Laws vary on which tests are permitted or prohibited.
- **Split specimen:** some laws require dividing each sample in two. The first is analyzed; if it comes back positive, the second is offered to the employee for analysis at an independent laboratory.
- Impact: if it is not split where required, **the employee must be reinstated even if he tests positive**.

**Whom to test?**

- The employer does not always decide alone. With a collective bargaining agreement, it is decided bilaterally.
- In the U.S., the Federal Highway Administration requires commercial carriers to have a drug and alcohol testing program for all their commercial drivers.
- Anyone doing business with certain government agencies must follow their rules on testing.

**Exam note:** since 2000, testing of commercial drivers has been regulated by the Federal Motor Carrier Safety Administration (FMCSA), created out of the Federal Highway Administration.

General testing guide (always comply with applicable regulations):

| Whom | When |
| --- | --- |
| All workers | Preemployment |
| All workers | Reasonable suspicion |
| All workers | Post-accident/injury |
| All workers | Return to duty |
| All workers | Follow up |
| Only workers in safety-sensitive positions | Randomly |

**Who collects the samples?**

- Some jurisdictions require licensed medical professionals. Others, a “qualified” person.
- U.S. federal rules: only breath alcohol technicians perform alcohol tests. Only screen test technicians perform alcohol screening.

**Where?**

- At the workplace, or the employee goes to a medical facility.
- Instant test kits are not permitted in many places.
- Some laws permit only certain laboratories to analyze urine.
- U.S. federal employers: only laboratories certified by the Department of Health and Human Services (HHS) or by the College of American Pathology (CAP).
- Some collective bargaining agreements and contracts also require a facility certified by HHS or approved by CAP.

**Exam note:** the actual name of the CAP is the *College of American Pathologists*.

**Who receives the results?**

- In the U.S., federal and state laws require a confidential medical review before reporting to the employer.
- The **MRO** is a licensed physician. In a confidential interview, by telephone or in person, with the donor, he determines whether the result has a medical justification.
- That is, whether something other than an illegally used controlled substance caused the positive. If there is none, he reports the positive to the employer.
- The prudent course for any organization: treat results as confidential medical information. It protects privacy and avoids legal liability.

#### 9.4 Incentives for the Employer (7.8.4)

- Take advantage of state incentives, which are usually in workers' compensation laws.
- Forms: reduced annual premiums, or presumptive denial of benefits if the worker had used prohibited substances when injured.
- With few exceptions, the employer and insurer do not pay compensation under two conditions: that the worker violated a known safety rule and that his intoxication caused the injury.
- At trial, the employer often must prove that the alcohol or drug was **the cause** of the injury, not just a contributing factor.
- There may be immunity in certain employment lawsuits if the employee tested positive or refused to give a sample.

#### 9.5 Liability (7.8.5)

- A poorly managed program can end in costly lawsuits.
- If the company's own employees collect the urine, it must be done reasonably and according to proper procedures.
- **Chain of custody:** establishes who handled the sample from the time it was submitted until there was a result. If it is broken, the result is invalid.
- Poor record keeping allows the chain of custody to be challenged and puts the validity of the result at risk.
- **The greatest risk is borne by employers who collect their own samples.** Without strict procedures and well-controlled records, they cannot defend against broken-chain claims.
- Consequence: they lose the result and can end up in court.
- Benefits of testing: more safety and fewer injuries. Requirements: sound written policies and keeping up with changes in rules and regulations.

### Key Terms

drug · drugs of abuse · psychoactive · chemical dependency · drug-free workplace · rationalization · opportunity · cut · fronting · pinch · pinching · Controlled Substances Act (CSA) · Drug Enforcement Administration (DEA) · Schedule I · Schedule II · Schedule III · Schedule IV · Schedule V · depressants · benzodiazepines · barbiturates · alcoholism · craving · loss of control · physical dependence · tolerance · narcotics · opiates · opioids · withdrawal · stimulants · crack · bingeing · methamphetamine · formication · hallucinogens · synaesthesia · flashbacks · THC · reverse tolerance · hashish · hemp flower · safety-sensitive positions · zero-tolerance policy · under-the-influence policy · analogue · designer drugs · prescription drugs · addiction · high-functioning abusers · denial · enabling · codependency · on-the-job impairment · employee hotlines · Sarbanes-Oxley Act · whistleblowers · intervention · documented progressive discipline · employee assistance program (EAP) · occupational alcoholism programs · management referral · Americans with Disabilities Act (ADA) · reasonable accommodation · clearinghouse · role modeling · Drug-Free Workplace Act of 1988 · metabolite · confirmatory test · medical review officer (MRO) · NIDA · reasonable suspicion · for-cause testing · random testing · return to duty · split specimen · breath alcohol technicians · HHS · CAP · chain of custody · workers' compensation

## Capítulo 8 — Workplace Violence Prevention (págs. 273–292)

**Central idea:** Workplace violence (WPV) can come from inside or outside, and the employer is obligated to protect against it. The chapter explains how to set up a prevention and intervention program based on a risk assessment, a threat management team, and protocols for notification, assessment, intervention, and monitoring. It also covers the response to the incident and the recovery afterward.

### 1. History (8.1)

| Year | Milestone |
| --- | --- |
| 1970 | General Duty Clause of the OSHA law, Section 5(a): each employer must provide its employees with employment and a place of employment free from recognized hazards that cause or are likely to cause death or serious physical harm. It is the basis of the 1998 document. |
| 1986 | Interest grows in the U.S. after the murder of 14 U.S. Postal Service employees by another employee, who then killed himself. |
| 1998 | The Occupational Safety and Health Administration (OSHA) publishes *Recommendations for Workplace Violence Programs in Late-Night Retail Establishments*. It is one of the first concrete federal efforts. |
| 2004 | OSHA publishes another guideline, for health care and social service workers, with similar safety recommendations. |
| 2009 | The 1998 document is updated with an expanded study and new recommendations for late-night retail. |
| 2011 | The ASIS standard on workplace violence prevention is created and approved. |
| 2020 | The standard is revised and renamed *Workplace Violence and Active Assailant—Prevention, Intervention, and Response*. |

**Exam note:** the actual title of the 1998 OSHA document is *Recommendations for Workplace Violence **Prevention** Programs in Late-Night Retail Establishments*. The book omits the word *Prevention*.

**The 1998 OSHA document:**

- Used data from the Bureau of Labor Statistics (BLS).
- Also used WPV tracking from the National Institute for Occupational Safety and Health (NIOSH).
- Considered WPV a serious threat, risk, and hazard to employee health and safety. Especially in occupations that operate at night and in the early morning hours.
- Identified high-risk industries and occupations.
- Presented a detailed safety analysis for developing a mitigation and prevention program, with recommendations for security measures.

**Figures (OSHA and BLS):**

- Every year, almost 2 million workers are victims of WPV.
- Every year there are about 450 workplace homicides in the U.S.
- In 2018 there were 453 workplace homicides (Bureau of Labor Statistics 2019):

| Perpetrator of the homicide (2018) | Number |
| --- | --- |
| Robbers | 106 |
| Coworkers or work associates | 77 |
| Customers | 39 |
| Relatives or domestic partners | 33 |
| Acquaintances | 18 |

These categories add up to 273; the book does not break down the remaining 180.

**Role of the security professional:**

- Their leadership and experience have brought WPV to the center of organizations' thinking.
- They have gotten other departments (human resources, legal) to cooperate on solutions integrated into the overall security program.
- Security is the focal point for obtaining and documenting information about possible WPV incidents.
- In most cases, security will be the first responder to an incident.

**The ASIS standard (2020):**

- Gives guidance and best practices for creating or improving prevention programs.
- Failing to meet those best practices can create legal liability for the organization, with costly, slow, and reputation-damaging outcomes.
- State and local laws, government regulations, and contractual obligations may also apply.
- Even with a reasonable program of deterrence, prevention, and mitigation, injuries and deaths can occur. No organization is exempt or can entirely prevent WPV.
- It calls for a holistic, strategic approach to four phases: prevention, intervention, response, and aftermath.
- That approach makes use of internal resources and information, above all supervisors and concerned employees.

### 2. Sources and Types of WPV (8.2)

- WPV covers any threatening or disruptive behavior:
  - Overt acts of violence (homicide, robbery, physical assault).
  - Threats of those acts.
  - Other physical or verbal conduct that creates a reasonable concern of violence against employees (harassment, intimidation, bullying).
