- **Definition (ASIS standard):** a spectrum of behaviors—overt acts of violence, threats, and other conduct—that generates a reasonable concern for safety from violence. There must be a nexus between the behavior and the physical safety of employees, from any internal or external relationship.
- Threats and their risk can come from internal or external sources.

**Employer's obligation:**

- It must provide a safe workplace for employees, contractors, visitors, and guests on its premises.
- In general, that obligation does not distinguish between internal and external sources of danger.
- If the employer or its representative has reasonable cause to believe someone may commit violence, it must protect the potential victim.
- It applies to acts on its premises and against an employee acting within the scope of his duties elsewhere.
- It does not matter who the aggressor is: employee, spouse or partner of an employee, shareholder, contractor, supplier, vendor, customer, guest, or other third party.

**Consequences:**

- The most serious: loss of life and injuries, physical and mental.
- For the business: legal liability, productivity, morale, reputation, and associated costs.
- The business's main concern may be the cost of being found liable for negligence.
- Hundreds of thousands of dollars per incident can be lost in work group productivity. Causes: absenteeism, sick leave, slower work, distraction of executives and workers, and general disorder.
- Other costs: critical incident care, higher workers' compensation and health insurance premiums, turnover expenses, and shareholder value (Sheely 2019).

#### 2.1 Risk Assessment (8.2.1)

- Every organization has a unique profile.
- The foundation of every WPV program is the security risk assessment, also called a needs assessment.
- It examines and evaluates the types of threats, the vulnerabilities, and the consequences.
- It also reviews whether the organization is prepared to respond to concerning behaviors and incidents.
- Internal and external threat sources (ASIS International 2020):
  - Current and former employees.
  - Customers.
  - Vendors.
  - Contractors and others who work with the organization or on its behalf.
  - Friends, relatives, and intimate partners.
  - Visitors and guests.
  - People opposed to the organization's mission, goals, or objectives.

**Types of offenders** (Penfield 2017). They are classified by their relationship to the workplace, in order to design measures according to that status.

| Type | Relationship to the organization | Examples |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Type 1 | No legitimate relationship; criminal intent | Robbers, trespassers |
| Type 2 | Legitimate relationship with the organization | Customer, patient, visitor, service provider (delivery person) |
| Type 3 | Current or former employee, including contractors | Employee or former employee |
| Type 4 | May or may not have a relationship with the organization, but has a personal relationship, real or perceived, with an employee | Domestic partner, ex-spouse |
| Type 5 | Ideological, political, or religious view opposed to the organization | Extremists, terrorists |

- The elements of the workplace violence prevention and intervention (WVPI) program are an integral part of the overall security program.
- The assessment must consider each type of offender when planning, developing, designing, implementing, overseeing, maintaining, and evaluating the solutions.
- The vulnerabilities detected are addressed effectively and reasonably.

**Workplace culture.** A negative culture contributes to WPV through:

- Policies and procedures that are unfair to employees.
- Lack of adequate staff, including trained and motivated security personnel.
- Poor management practices.
- Reorganizations.

These conditions generate frustration, overwork, complaints, and stress. If executive management does not recognize and address them, the risk of an incident rises.

**Preparedness for response.** The assessment reviews current prevention and intervention practices:

- Existing policies and procedures.
- Behavior expectations and their consequences.
- WPV prevention training.
- Reporting protocols.
- Partnerships with outside organizations, such as the police and mental health groups.

**Security vulnerabilities.** A general threat assessment may reveal other factors:

- Lax access control: any offender enters easily and threatens workers.
- Alcohol consumption at a bar or restaurant: encourages aggressive behavior by customers.
- Facilities in high-crime areas: exposed to random or targeted crimes.

### 3. WPV Warning Signs (8.3)

- There is no exact profile of the offender.
- Recognizing the warning signs, called “red flags,” is key to preventing and mitigating. They are problem behaviors that precede violence.
- Most WPV events are preceded by stressors, precursors, and indicators.
- Sixteen examples of concerning behaviors in employees or other related persons:
  - Sudden and severe mood swings.
  - Signs of depression.
  - More anger or tantrums.
  - Increased alcohol and drug use.
  - Social isolation.
  - Threats, implied or overt.
  - Change in appearance or neglect of hygiene.
  - Growing, unexplained absenteeism.
  - Decreased work output.
  - Arguments with coworkers and supervisors.
  - Destruction, theft, or sabotage of property.
  - Suicidal ideas.
  - Increased interest in weapons, firearms, or violence.
  - Bullying or workplace harassment.
  - Changes in domestic relationships.
  - Changes in life insurance.
- Employees must know these signs and know exactly how to report them.
- They must report immediately: early identification greatly improves prevention.
- Prevention depends on prompt reporting, whether the employee is the offender or the victim.
- Channels: directly to security, to human resources, to a supervisor, or to another employee. Also anonymous reporting procedures or confidential hotlines.
- Security and human resources have the most impact and involvement in the entire WVPI program.
- Even so, all employees must be involved. They are a vital source of information, above all of warning signs, and can prevent an incident.

### 4. Developing a WVPI Program (8.4)

- The WVPI program reflects the components of the organization's security program.
- Its risk assessment considers the overall threat landscape and prior incidents in the organization and in similar organizations.
- It also considers general information on current WPV trends and cases.

#### 4.1 Organizational Commitment and Policy (8.4.1)

- Most important step at the start: create a policy backed by executive management.
- That backing spreads throughout the organization, so that no one doubts it is a serious priority.
- The WPV policy must be issued directly from the office of the chief executive officer (CEO).
- Executive management must provide the necessary resources, funds, and authorizations.
- The policy is communicated at hiring and throughout employment.
- It states the employer's commitment to a safe workplace. It describes a code of conduct that prohibits all violence, threats, and conduct interpretable as intent to harm (ASIS International 2020).
- The policy must:
  - Define the unacceptable conduct it prohibits.
  - Regulate or prohibit weapons, to the extent the law permits.
  - Require prompt reporting of any conduct or circumstance that raises a safety concern or violates the policy.
  - Offer several reporting channels.
  - Ensure that reports will be treated seriously and with discretion.
  - Commit to no retaliation against anyone who reports in good faith.
  - Require employees to report protective or restraining orders that are sought, requested, or issued to protect them.
  - Impose disciplinary sanctions for violating the policy, including false or misleading reports.
  - Avoid the term “zero tolerance,” because it reduces reporting and lowers safety.
- Employee participation from the start is as important as that of executive management. That way the program considers their needs and gains their support, cooperation, and commitment.

**Evolution of the teams** (the key players vary by industry):

1. WVPI policy working group (WG): multidisciplinary and broad. It brings together the security, human resources, legal, health and safety, bargaining, employee assistance, crisis management, risk management, and public relations units.
2. Stakeholders working group (SWG): smaller.
3. Threat management team (TMT): permanent and long-term. It assesses and responds to violent incidents or to reports of behaviors covered by the policy. It also oversees the WVPI program.

- The TMT is also called the threat assessment team (TAT) or incident management team (IMT). The three acronyms are interchangeable.

**Role of security personnel** (fundamental in the program). Tasks the organization may delegate to them:

- Conduct surveys, audits, and security risk assessments.
- Recommend additional security measures.
- Develop policies and procedures.
- Train employees in the WVPI.
- Respond to, investigate, and report WPV complaints or incidents.
- Coordinate with local police and emergency services.

#### 4.2 WVPI Planning (8.4.2)

Before formulating the plan, these steps must be done:

1. Identify threats and risks.
2. Obtain initial authorization from executive management.
3. Receive employee input.
4. Disseminate the WPV policy.
5. Form the stakeholders working group (SWG).

With the risk assessment as the basis, the WVPI allocates four security resources to prevent or mitigate an event:

| Resource | What is reviewed and how |
| --- | --- |
| Physical, electronic, and design security | Thorough survey, review, and audit of the measures at all locations. Done by security and/or an outside consultant. The consultant must have the education, training, experience, knowledge, skills, competencies, and industry-recognized certifications. |
| Security policies, procedures, and protocols | Current WPV practices, with further input from the SWG, above all from human resources and legal. Changes take operations into account and avoid delaying operational goals. Everything is written in detail, clearly defined, and widely communicated. Employee roles are delimited and explained, with initial and ongoing training. |
| Security personnel | Competence to deter, detect, delay, deny, and respond to an incident and its aftermath. Recruitment, selection, screening, training, equipment, supervision, evaluation, staffing, and deployment are reviewed. If training or staff is lacking, the organization must provide it. |
| Security and crime prevention awareness, education, and training | Covers the entire organization, from contract staff to executive management, at the start and on an ongoing basis. That for security personnel is more specialized and complete. Includes periodic drills and exercises with police and other emergency services (Appendix 8B: sample scenario). |

- The SWG evaluates the threats and their risk against current practices. In this way it decides on improvements and closes the vulnerability gaps.

**Exam note:** the book says the physical security audit is done by security or an outside consultant “as mentioned earlier.” But the chapter had not mentioned it earlier.

#### 4.3 Threat Management Team (TMT) (8.4.3)

- After disseminating the policy and completing the mitigation strategies, the SWG's experience is used to form the TMT.
- The TMT can rely on outside experts to complement its knowledge.
- It provides ongoing assessment and support to meet the policy's goals. It helps implement the prevention and response components.
- Areas the TMT handles:
  - Create incident management protocols to assess, investigate, handle, and resolve reports.
  - Create emergency protocols for threats of imminent violence.
  - Train all employees and levels of management and supervision, with emphasis on warning signs and immediate reporting.
  - Initiate anonymous reporting methods.
  - Create a confidential records system that tracks the reports received and the actions taken.
  - Create a thorough protocol for preemployment screening and background investigation for all employees and contractors. It includes references, criminal records, drug and alcohol use, and social media searches, within legal limits.
  - Use physical, electronic, and design measures: access, entry, and visitor control; barriers; signage; lighting; locks; intrusion and duress alarms; video surveillance.
  - Create a conflict resolution and mediation process for workplace issues. It includes training to prevent, nonviolently de-escalate, defuse, and resolve volatile situations and aggressive behaviors.
  - Provide information and assistance to employees involved in WPV incidents.
  - Create a code of conduct with clear expectations of ethical behavior.
- Crime prevention through environmental design (CPTED) concepts that guide the selection, installation, and use of the measures:
  - Natural surveillance and access control.
  - Territorial reinforcement.
  - Maintenance.
  - Target hardening.

### 5. WVPI Protocols (8.5)

- Specific procedures are needed to treat all parties fairly and to comply with the policy and external regulation.
- It is important to consult the legal department when developing them.

#### 5.1 Notification (8.5.1)

- Notification can come from inside or outside the company.
- The company needs policy, procedures, and training that direct reports to a specific person or group.
- That recipient does the initial intake and the first assessment of immediate risk.
- However it arrives (for example: observation, e-mail, postal mail, phone call, text message, or fax), everyone must pass it immediately to the person responsible.
- “Everyone” includes operators, receptionists, managers, supervisors, customer service representatives, and other employees.
- Recipients may need to be available 24 hours a day, 7 days a week, and be trained.
- Violence can escalate if management does not respond to the first warning signs.

#### 5.2 Assessment (8.5.2)

- Goal of every threat assessment: identify the threat and the mitigation avenues, for example judicial or mental health.
- Several levels are possible. They depend on whether the offender is known or unknown, and on the quantity and quality of the initial information.

| Offender | What can be done |
| --- | --- |
| Unknown (e.g., anonymous communications) | A valid violence risk assessment cannot be made. A preliminary behavioral analysis, of low validity, is possible. An investigation is conducted to identify him; the organization's response is governed by other policies or procedures. |
| Known, at least by name | Can proceed with a three-level assessment. |

**Three levels of assessment of the known subject.** Each level is done by one or more TMT members. They seek to decide which resources to use and to what extent.

| Level | Question it answers | What follows |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1. Initial assessment | Is there immediate risk of harm? | If there is a significant possibility of immediate harm: emergency procedures until the situation stabilizes. If not: move to the threshold level. |
| 2. Threshold assessment | Is further assessment needed, according to the risk thresholds set by the company, or is monitoring enough? | If the predetermined threshold is reached: comprehensive assessment. If not: monitoring only. |
| 3. Comprehensive assessment | Gathers additional information from internal and external sources | Serves as the basis for designing and applying a nonimmediate emergency resolution plan. |

**Exam note:** the book announces that the three levels are “explained in more detail below.” But the chapter does not develop them again.

- Threat intelligence: investigation, protection, social media monitoring, and tracking and analysis of trends in reports and suspicious activity.
- It gives better results when working with community partners (Breslin 2019).
- More liaison with police, mental health, and education, locally and over a wider geographic area, lets the TMT take a holistic approach to the threat.

#### 5.3 Intervention and Nonemergency Situational Resolution (8.5.3)

- Main goal: the safety of the identified target or targets, in the short and, ideally, long term.
- It seeks to divert the offender from aggression toward socially acceptable behaviors that resolve his perceived need for control.
- Primary consideration: first, do no harm to either the target or the offender.
- It is achieved when the offender voluntarily chooses to abandon the concerning behaviors.
- An involuntary resolution will probably be temporary, unless the offender dies.
- That is why restraining or protective orders, arrest, and criminal or psychiatric confinement are only short-term stabilizing interventions.
- If the offender's attitude toward the target does not change, those measures will eventually fail.

**Direct stabilizing intervention options** (Cawood 2020):

- Interviews by the investigator, both to assess and to intervene.
- Administrative or disciplinary actions, including dismissal.
- Cease-and-desist requests.
- No-trespass, restraining, or protective orders.
- Voluntary or involuntary mental health evaluations.
- Contact with the police, with or without arrest.

**How to choose and apply:**

- The choice depends on the offender's likely reaction and on whether the intervention can correct his perception of the target.
- Any communication or interaction with the offender, direct or indirect (through third parties), counts as intervention.
- The interview with the offender informs about his perception, emotional and cognitive level, impulsivity, and limits.
- It also lets one reframe his goals, raise alternative behaviors, discuss cause and effect and consequences, and look for other solutions.
- Restraining and protective orders create a boundary. They are only worth anything if the target reports violations and they are enforced promptly.
- One often starts with one technique while the TMT prepares others.
- Example: before interviewing the offender, the text and affidavits of a restraining order are drafted.
- A disciplinary reprimand or a dismissal package is also prepared.
- And the police are contacted to find out what crimes the conduct could constitute, how to report, and what response they might give.

#### 5.4 Monitoring (8.5.4)

- Monitoring new behavior is a critical part of violence risk assessment.
- It creates a behavioral feedback loop. It allows the assessment to be updated, the value of interventions to be tested, and the final resolution to be determined.
- The TMT can set up passive or active monitoring.

|  | Passive monitoring | Active monitoring |
| --- | --- | --- |
| How it works | The target and other possible witnesses report new behavior to the TMT in a timely way | The assessor actively seeks new behavioral information; the higher the risk, the more frequent the contacts |
| When it applies | Only in low-risk cases, where a delay in reporting would not create a significant risk of harm | Moderate to high risk, or when the target or witnesses cannot be relied on to report |
| Book case | The victim receives a single anonymous e-mail or voice message of the “I hate you and you'll pay for this” type. If the investigation finds no further grounds for concern, one “waits and sees” and the victim is asked to report new contacts. | Domestic violence risk: the offender calls the target at work, threatens to make her pay and to come confront her. The target says there is no danger. But the offender has a history of domestic violence, including a confrontation at a former partner's workplace. |

- Reasons the target or witnesses do not report well:
  - Shock, denial, rationalization, minimization, or other psychological defense mechanisms.
  - Fear of retaliation or revenge.
  - Mistakenly believing the target can handle it without help.
- In the domestic case, the target may hide or minimize contacts. Reasons: shame, fear of losing the job, or believing herself safe.
- What the TMT does in that case:
  - Identifies coworkers who might witness new contacts and who can be trusted to report them.
  - Consults them several times a day.
  - If they report contacts, asks the target for an update.
  - If the target denies the contact, tries to reduce her resistance to reporting.
  - Raises or lowers the frequency of monitoring according to the current risk of imminent violence.

#### 5.5 Review and Debriefing (8.5.5)

- Incident review is continuous, as new behavioral information arrives from any source.
- Cycle that repeats until the TMT, according to its standards, considers the situation resolved:
  1. Reassessment.
  2. Review of intervention options.
  3. Application of the interventions.
  4. Monitoring for new behavioral signs.
- The review continually refines operational and tactical processes to provide the greatest safety.
- Debriefing and lessons learned are critical to incident management and process improvement. They give a strategic view of how an incident can improve the process on a larger scale.
- Some companies hold short debriefings after the first round of assessment and intervention.
- Afterward they hold monthly, quarterly, semiannual, or annual debriefings to update cases and discuss improvements.
- Reviews, debriefings, or a mix of both allow continuous improvement of the case and of the overall process.

### 6. Implementing the WVPI Program (8.6)

- It is planned carefully and involves all TMT members.
- It proceeds step by step; the steps can happen at the same time or separately.

1. Designate a specific group, with particular TMT members, to develop and implement the program.
2. Define an implementation plan guided by legal requirements, standards, and the practices of similar organizations. In this step the requirements and recommendations of the ASIS standard are consulted.
3. Establish the incident management protocols.
4. Disseminate the policy to all employees and contractors by various means: onboarding, posters, mailings, e-mails, orientations, organization meetings, and training programs.
5. Provide documented, initial and ongoing training to all employees and groups that work with the organization. Topics:
   - Basic information on workplace violence.
   - Specific areas of the WVPI policy.
   - Warning signs of potential violent behavior.
   - Responses to the signs and related emergency actions.
   - Critical importance of reporting concerning behaviors immediately.
   - Thorough training for all TMT members in all aspects of workplace violence, delivered by qualified experts.
   - Specific training for managers and supervisors in identifying and reporting observed behaviors or information received.
6. Continuously monitor, evaluate, review, and improve the policy and the program, in addition to a formal annual review.

- Once under way, the program is maintained with open communication, training, and partnerships.
- Upon receiving a report, the TMT immediately activates the incident management process, because the potential for violence can grow quickly.

### 7. Special Cases (8.7)

| Case | What to do |
| --- | --- |
| Intimate partner violence (8.7.1) | If an employee is in an abusive relationship, the violence can carry over to the workplace and become the organization's problem. The TMT makes it easier for her to report and responds to her concerns. Possible responses: limit the alleged offender's access to the workplace; escort the employee to her car; change her work e-mail and phone; move her to a secure workplace; refer her to outside personal safety resources. |
| Employee with behavioral or mental disorders (8.7.2) | May appear to be a risk to others. Consult an attorney about the employer's obligations: certain regulations protect employees with some disabilities, and there are privacy issues. TMT options: encourage him to seek medical care; perhaps grant medical leave; conduct a fitness-for-duty examination, if the organization requires it. |
| Separation of employment (8.7.3) | When ending the employment or business relationship with a person of concern, the separation must be respectful and safe. It must follow established practices and policies on risk of violence and have legal counsel. Short- and long-term security measures for the facility and employees may be advisable. |

### 8. Response to Violence (8.8)

- When violence occurs there is no time to assess risks or to plan.
- The crisis management plan is developed and practiced in advance, so it can be executed smoothly.
- The TMT can create it with input from executive management, human resources, legal, and other key stakeholders.
- Areas to prepare for WPV or an active assailant. More detail in Annex A of the ASIS standard:
  - Roles, responsibilities, procedures, and training of key response personnel.
  - Emergency alert, warning, and notification systems through multiple means of communication.
  - Employee awareness, education, and training, with special attention to: warning signs, reporting procedures, and “Run. Hide. Fight.” protocols.
  - Emergency equipment and materials for key personnel: portable radios, flashlights, keys and access cards, building and floor plans, and first aid supplies.
  - Liaison with public safety agencies: police, fire, and ambulance.
  - Choosing an emergency operations center (EOC) and an incident command center (ICC). They serve to direct, coordinate, and communicate with internal and external responders.
  - Preserving the crime scene, when possible.
  - Planning communication during and after the event with employees, families, and the media:
    - A system to locate employees who may be in danger.
    - Watching employees and others for signs of psychological trauma.
    - Being able to contact the families of all employees for reunification and other assistance.
    - A media area and a family notification and reunification area, safe and away from ongoing operations.
    - Choosing and training a spokesperson to be the voice of the organization during and after the event.
- Depending on the nature and severity of the incident, the TMT activates the crisis management plan.
- In life-threatening emergencies, such as an active assailant attack, the plan must include these actions:
  - Alert others in danger.
  - Call the police immediately.
  - Cooperate with and assist the police.
  - Evacuate or shelter in place.
  - Keep others from entering the danger zone.
  - Defend against the assailant, if possible.
  - Account for employees and others present to know their status and whether they need help.

### 9. Post-Incident Recovery and Business Continuity (8.9)

- Once the crisis and response phase is over, the business recovery and business continuity plans are activated.
- They cover immediate, short-, and long-term timeframes.
- Depending on the nature and extent of the incident (especially with major property damage, injuries, or deaths), the recovery plan considers:
  - Priority: preserve life, protect essential assets (including reputation and image), and preserve evidence. Life safety is always the highest priority.
  - As soon as possible and safe: gather the facts, secure the area and the evidence, separate the witnesses, and keep watching to preserve lives and prevent another incident.
  - Coordinate the TMT with the crisis, recovery, and continuity teams.
  - Notify managers, employees, family members, regulatory agencies, and the media.
  - Maintain communication with everyone affected by as many means as is fitting: telephone, text, e-mail, social media, public address, digital displays, TV and radio, and press conferences. It is the main role of public relations.
  - Coordinate recovery assistance, mental health services, police, legal counsel, families, and other critical needs.
  - Resume operations when it is safe and appropriate.
  - Rebuild relationships with customers, vendors, and the public, as the incident requires.
  - Keep evaluating plans and responses to improve and draw lessons learned.

### 10. Chapter Closing Thoughts (8.10)

- The organization must provide a safe environment, free of reasonably foreseeable threats. The entire security program revolves around this principle, and the WVPI is a vital part of it.
- Most WPV affects one or a few people.
- Incidents with no death or serious injury are probably underreported.
- Not even the best program can prevent all crimes or WPV events.
- Serious incidents are rare. But a single one can cause deaths, disabling injuries, and trauma for the company and the community, with effects lasting years.
