## Apéndices con contenido de examen

The appendices to chapters 1, 3, and 4 are practical models: disciplinary policy, scope of service, orders, contract with the security company, and documents for hiring consultants. Their complete lists, deadlines, figures, and clauses are collected here.

### Appendix 1A — Sample Disciplinary Policy (pp. 62–67)

**What it is:** a model disciplinary policy for a fictitious security company (“XYZ Company”). It lists the infractions, their consequences, and the criteria for sanctioning fairly.

#### I. Policy (approach)

- The company demands high performance and good conduct: reliability, honesty, and trustworthiness.
- Discipline **is not punishment**. It is a means of improving performance through corrective counseling.
- That corrective approach is maintained until the conduct reaches the level of statutory misconduct.

#### II.A. Criteria for Deciding the Sanction (7)

The approach is “strict but fair and reasonable.” Sanctions, up to discharge for willful misconduct, are based on:

1. The seriousness of the situation.
2. The consequences of the act to the employer's detriment.
3. Mitigating or aggravating circumstances.
4. The employee's attitude.
5. His disciplinary history and prior warnings.
6. Similar prior cases.
7. The welfare of the organization and of coworkers.

**Forms of discipline (8):** oral reprimand; written warning; unfavorable performance evaluation; probation; suspension without pay; demotion; reassignment; discharge for willful misconduct.

The seriousness and the measure are decided, at its discretion, by the **management disciplinary committee**.

#### II.B. Who Applies Discipline and the Supervisor's Role

- Every hearing and disciplinary action is conducted by **two or more** members of executive management, always in accordance with this policy.
- Supervisor's role (5):
  1. Know and understand all company policies and guidelines.
  2. Report every infraction to management using the *Employee Warning Notice* form. It must include all details, the interim measures already taken, and the necessary follow-up.
  3. Investigate the facts of the observed or reported infraction.
  4. Recommend the seriousness of the infraction and the appropriate corrective measure.
  5. Apply minor corrective measures and emergency measures in serious situations that call for immediate remedy. Examples: sending home an officer under the influence of alcohol; reprimanding an officer who is not wearing the uniform.

#### II.C. Legal Definitions (aligned with state regulation)

The text refers to the employee as the “claimant.”

**(1) Misconduct** includes, but is not limited to:

- a) Willful or wanton disregard of the rights, title, and interests of the employer or a coworker.
- b) Deliberate violation or disregard of the standards of behavior that the employer has a right to expect.
- c) Carelessness or negligence that causes, or would probably cause, serious bodily harm to the employer or a coworker.
- d) Carelessness or negligence of such degree or recurrence that it shows intentional or substantial disregard of the employer's interest.

**(2) Acts considered misconduct**, because they involve willful or wanton disregard:

- a) Insubordination: deliberate refusal to follow the employer's reasonable instructions.
- b) Repeated and unjustifiable tardiness after warnings.
- c) Job dishonesty: falsifying company records, theft, deliberate deception or lying.
- d) Repeated and unjustifiable absences, including those that could have been announced in advance and were not.
- e) Deliberate illegal acts, which provoke violence or violations of law, or which violate the collective bargaining agreement. Exception: lawful union activity cannot disqualify anyone for misconduct.
- f) Violating a company rule, if the rule is reasonable and the employee knew or should have known it.
- g) Violations of law committed within the scope of employment that substantially affect performance or substantially damage the employer's ability to do business.

**(3) NOT misconduct:**

- a) Inefficiency, unsatisfactory conduct, or poor performance due to inability or lack of aptitude.
- b) Ordinary carelessness or negligence in isolated cases.
- c) Errors of judgment or discretion made in good faith.

**(4) Gross misconduct** is either of these two situations:

- A work-related criminal act for which the person was **convicted** in a criminal court, or which he **admitted** to having committed.
- Work-related conduct that shows a **flagrant and wanton** disregard of the rights, title, or interests of the employer or a coworker.

#### II.D. The 37 Grounds for Sanction, up to Discharge

Breaking any of these rules is considered **willful misconduct**. Reason: it damages the relationship with clients, the reputation in the industry and in the community, the interests of the business, and the welfare of other officers.

1. Excessive tardiness or absences.
2. Sleeping on duty.
3. Any dishonesty, including falsifying company or client records, and the employment application.
4. Watching television, using any multimedia device, or reading material unrelated to work, including use of a personal cell phone.
5. Social media activity or political activism, if it violates company policy, during work hours and with company equipment.
6. Accepting money, gifts, or other gratuities from the client's employees.
7. Discourtesy, aggressiveness, vulgarity, or rudeness toward the client's representatives, employees, visitors, or customers, or toward the public. Above all, verbal or physical altercations on duty.
8. Bringing any animal to the workplace.
9. Returning to the post or visiting a site while off duty.
10. Improper, incomplete, or dirty uniform.
11. Bringing or allowing visits from friends, guests, or family members at the site.
12. Taking a patrol vehicle home without written permission.
13. Raising personal or company problems with the client, or trying to get the client to hire him.
14. Reporting under the influence of drugs or alcohol; using or selling them on duty.
15. Insubordination: refusing to follow lawful or legitimate instructions from a supervisor or manager.
16. Failing to perform duties diligently, conscientiously, and to the best of his ability; or attending to personal matters on duty.
17. Failing to carry the security officer license whenever on duty, and failing to maintain the ability to obtain it.
18. Any damage to company or client property, by act or omission.
19. Appropriating company or client property for personal use (conversion).
20. Failing to abide by and enforce the client's rules firmly, fairly, and consistently.
21. Unauthorized use of company or client equipment or supplies, including telephones, computers, and information.
22. Smoking or eating in areas not designated for it.
23. Violating the Post Instructions or the client's orders.
24. Giving false testimony.
25. Failing to cooperate in a criminal or company investigation.
26. Carrying any unauthorized weapon (on the person, in a bag, or in a vehicle) on company or client property. Personal defense “weapons” require approval of the Company Chairman.
27. Conspiring to commit a crime or allowing another to commit one; any criminal act, including downloading copyrighted material onto any device.
28. Disclosing confidential information without authorization.
29. Misuse or theft of company or client data.
30. Unsafe practices or violation of safety rules.
31. Failing to submit correct and timely reports, payroll time sheets, logs, alarm reports, or other required written documentation.
32. Any unprofessional conduct that endangers the company's reputation, integrity, or interests, or the welfare of others.
33. Excessive use of force or projecting a threatening image.
34. Leaving the assigned post without relief or without properly authorized instruction.
35. Repeated minor infractions after warnings.
36. Violating laws or company rules necessary to operate safely or effectively.
37. Working for another security company under a contract that competes with the company.

#### II.E. The 17 Fairness Questions Before Sanctioning

The company seeks **fundamental fairness**. In every disciplinary case, these questions are asked:

1. Is the rule reasonable and necessary? Does it contribute to an orderly and efficient operation? Does it help meet goals? Does its violation harm the business, the relationship with clients, or the reputation?
2. Does it relate to the performance the company can reasonably expect of an employee?
3. Was it well communicated and distributed? Do employees know it and understand it?
4. Has there been significant and legitimate disagreement from more than one employee?
5. Has it been applied consistently?
6. Have other employees been sanctioned for the same offense across the company?
7. Was there a thorough and impartial investigation that proves the offense by a **preponderance of the evidence**?
8. Were there relevant mitigating or exculpatory circumstances?
9. How deliberate was the offense? Could the employee have believed the rule did not apply to that situation?
10. What is the employee's attitude toward the offense?
11. Did the company actively seek witnesses and evidence?
12. Are the planned measures appropriate to the offense?
13. Was it a crime under local, state, or federal law?
14. Does the employee have prior offenses?
15. What is his seniority?
16. Will the sanction adversely affect the company's operation?
17. How likely is it that the employee will make the necessary positive change?

**Exam note:** section D declares any violation of the 37 rules *willful* misconduct. But C(3) excludes ordinary isolated negligence and good-faith errors. For example, rule 18 (“any damage… by act or omission”) clashes with that exclusion.

**Takeaway:** chapter 1 says that, with a union and a “just cause” dismissal clause, a clear statement is needed to sustain severe sanctions. This model provides it: corrective discipline, legal definitions of *misconduct* and *gross misconduct*, and a fairness test.

### Appendix 1B — Company Fact Sheet on Employee Discipline (p. 68)

**What it is:** a simplified version of policy 1A, which the officer signs. It serves to verify that he knows the rules.

- Every organization (companies, sports teams, government) needs rules and policies. A security company even more so.
- Keys to success in security: organizational order, public safety, employee welfare, and respect for the employer's interests and rules.
- Goal: a fully professional, safe, productive, and satisfying workplace.
- Main focus: **employee development** (giving him the tools and removing obstacles).
- Discipline comes in only when the employee had a real opportunity to succeed and **intentionally chose** to violate rules, harming the employer or its relationship with clients.

**Progressive discipline** for most infractions, according to seriousness and mitigating factors. Possible measures:

1. Verbal counseling.
2. Remedial training.
3. Written note of corrective counseling.
4. Suspension without pay.
5. Demotion.
6. Last and final warning.

**Nonnegotiable infractions, which can cause immediate dismissal** because the harm is irreparable (9):

- Violations of the law.
- Dishonesty of any kind.
- Extreme negligence that causes physical harm to others.
- Post abandonment.
- Sleeping on duty.
- No-call no-shows.
- Repeated absences or tardiness.
- Insubordination.
- Intentional violation of any reasonable, necessary, and known work rule.

**Closing:** the officer is asked for full attention and his best effort; when in doubt, ask. Employee's signature with date and a witness's initials, as acknowledgment of understanding and agreement.

**Takeaway:** chapter 1 presents it as the short version of 1A for checking that the officer knows the rules. The signature with a witness documents that the rule was communicated (question 3 of the 1A fairness test).

### Appendix 3A — Sample Scope of Work (p. 121)

**What it is:** a sample scope of work for a guard services contract. It defines tasks, posts, schedules, and total hours.

**Tasks:**

- Routine fixed posts and foot patrols, according to the general and special orders.
- **Security control center** officers: monitor fire and intrusion alarms, receive calls, keep key control, and perform administrative tasks.
- Roving or mobile patrol officers: may carry an electronic patrol recorder and a two-way radio.

**Posts and weekly hours:**

| Post | Position | Schedule | Days | Hours/week |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| No. 1 — Control center | Supervisor | 0730–1600 | Monday through Friday | 42.5 |
| No. 1 | Officer | 0800–1600 | Saturday and Sunday | 16.0 |
| No. 1 | Officer | 1600–2400 | Monday through Sunday | 56.0 |
| No. 1 | Officer | 2400–0800 | Monday through Sunday | 56.0 |
| No. 2 — Roving/mobile patrol | Officer | 0800–1600 | Saturday and Sunday | 16.0 |
| No. 2 | Officer | 1600–2400 | Saturday and Sunday | 16.0 |
| No. 2 | Officer | 1500–2400 | Monday through Friday | 45.0 |
| No. 2 | Officer | 2400–0800 | “Saturday through Sunday” | 56.0 |
| **Total** |  |  |  | **303.5** |

Subtotals (own calculation): post 1 = 170.5 h; post 2 = 133.0 h.

**Officer complement:**

| Personnel | Shifts/week | Straight time (ST) hours | Overtime (OT) hours | Total |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 supervisor | 5 | 40.0 | 2.5 | 42.5 |
| 6.5 officers | 32 | 256.0 | 5.0 | 261.0 |
| **Total** |  |  |  | **303.5** |

Check: the supervisor's 8.5-hour shifts give 0.5 h of overtime daily (5 × 0.5 = 2.5). The 5 nine-hour shifts (1500–2400) give 5 h of overtime. And 32 shifts × 8 h = 256 h.

**Exam note:** the last shift of post 2 says “Saturday through Sunday” but adds up to 56.0 h. Two nights would give 16 h; 56 h is 7 nights. The totals (303.5 h and 32 shifts) only add up with 7 nights: it seems to mean “Monday through Sunday.”

**Exam note:** the chapter 3 analysis example speaks of about 308 hours per week (about 16,000 per year). This appendix adds up to 303.5. The book does not say they are the same example: do not mix figures.

**Takeaway:** according to chapter 3, the scope must state the tasks, the days and hours of service, and the total hours. It is given to all prospective contractors along with the orders and the proposed operating agreement.

### Appendix 3B — Sample General and Special Orders (pp. 122–128)

**What it is:** a model set of orders for the contract security force assigned to a company. It summarizes the required conduct, duties, disciplinary grounds, and special orders.

#### 1. Three Categories of Orders

| Type | Content |
| --- | --- |
| 1.1 General orders | General performance standards for **all** officers. |
| 1.2 Special orders | Duties of each specific assignment: routine tasks and prescribed courses of action. |
| 1.3 Special instructions | For special events (inspections, guided tours). Issued when needed, in the form of a memorandum. |

#### 2. Personal Conduct

- **2.1 Responsibilities:** honesty, alertness, loyalty, and intelligence.
- **2.2 Attitude:** total interest in protecting all property. It is reinforced by thinking and acting in terms of security, protection, and prevention.
- **2.3 Habits:** dignified conduct at all times. **Smoking is prohibited** on duty.
- **2.4 Discipline:** prompt and willing obedience to the orders of the designated representative.
- **2.5 Appearance:** personal cleanliness and neatness. Well-cut and groomed hair, clean nails and hands, presentable uniform.
- **2.6 Uniform and equipment:** provided by the employer; each officer cares for and maintains them.
  - 2.6.1 Uniform clean, in good condition, and pressed.
  - 2.6.2 Shoes well shined.
  - 2.6.3 Cap (if issued) worn at all times on duty and “straight”: neither tilted nor backward.
  - 2.6.4 Complete uniform and equipment on duty.
- **2.7 Post cleanliness:** no clothing, equipment, or personal items are stored. Loitering there is prohibited. Always clean and orderly.
- **2.8 Public relations:** the officer is often the visitor's first contact. His impression influences the opinion of the company and must be very favorable.
  - Firm and decisive, but courteous with employees and visitors.
  - Friendly greeting (“good morning,” “good evening”) to visitors, executives, and employees.
  - Courtesy and tact with employees. **Arguing** with them **is not tolerated**.
  - Every rule violation is reported immediately.
- **2.9 Telephones:** only for official business, never personal calls. When answering, identify himself by name and assignment. If the caller does not identify himself, ask for his name.

#### 3. Duties

- **3.1 General duties:** protect property and personnel, control traffic, and report rule violations.
- **3.2 Performance requires knowing (4):** the general procedures; the special orders and instructions of the assignment; the physical layout of the facility; all the rules he must enforce.
- **3.3 Familiarization with the patrol area.** Before the assignment he receives a general orientation. He must know (10):
  1. Where key personnel work.
  2. Where the telephones are, including emergency ones.
  3. Where the fire alarm pull boxes are.
  4. Where all the stairways and doors lead.
  5. Where materials are stored.
  6. Where all the fire-fighting equipment is.
  7. Where the light switches are.
  8. The control rooms and the shutoff switches for air conditioning and ventilation.
  9. All the valves that control water to the sprinklers.
  10. Fuse boxes, power switches, steam valves, hydrants, and other machinery controls.
- **3.4 Routine duties (5):**
  1. Patrol the exterior of the buildings and the boundaries of the premises.
  2. Patrol the interior.
  3. Control the entry and movement of pedestrians and vehicles.
  4. Prevent theft and damage to company and employee property.
  5. Report all matters of security interest: theft and pilferage, fire and its prevention, espionage and sabotage, and rule violations.
- **3.5 Patrolling:** all critical areas are checked. Marking the electronic checkpoints is **not** the main purpose. The main purpose is to report maintenance deficiencies and security or fire hazards observed on the round.
- **3.6 Prevention of theft, fire, and accidents:** protection is **primarily preventive**. Report any situation that could cause loss, damage, or injury, among them (12):
  1. Careless or suspicious activity where easily pilfered items are kept or handled.
  2. Tools or other property unattended at the end of the shift, or poorly stored.
  3. Employees in areas where they normally should not be, above all finished-goods warehouses or other property.
  4. People loitering near fences or windows.
  5. Company property in places where it is not normally found.
  6. Any deficiency in building security.
  7. Suggestions for improving security equipment or services.
  8. Defective or failing locks.
  9. Misuse of locks or keys, and their destruction or abuse.
  10. Doors that should be locked and are open.
  11. Opening of locks and doors to give access: report the person's name, date, time, and other details.
  12. Keys found unattended and recovered.
- **3.7 Attention to duty:** reading newspapers, books, or any other distraction on duty is prohibited.

#### 4. Disciplinary Action: Grounds for Transfer or Dismissal (11)

1. Leaving the post without adequate relief, for any reason.
2. Failing to notify the designated shift supervisor in time that he will not be able to report.
3. Theft or pilferage by the officer.
4. Accepting fees, gifts, or gratuities in the service.
5. Conduct unbecoming an officer or prejudicial to discipline.
6. Consuming alcohol, illegally using controlled substances, or being under their influence on duty.
7. Making false reports.
8. Sleeping on duty.
9. Intentional disobedience of orders or neglect of duty.
10. Unnecessary harshness, violence, or foul language.
11. Allowing, intentionally or through carelessness, violations of company or facility rules.

#### Special Orders (9)

1. **Emergency not provided for** in the general or post orders: notify the company representative or his designated alternate.
2. **Suspected crime or death:**
   - Do not destroy or diminish the value of evidence on bodies, belongings, and surroundings.
   - Keep unauthorized persons from entering until an authority arrives.
   - Meanwhile, immediately and thoroughly investigate the circumstances. If possible, note names and addresses of witnesses and every important detail.
3. Report every violation he sees or learns of.
4. Official matters or personal complaints against another officer: present them immediately to the company's designated representative.
5. **Firearms:** only in defense of himself or another person against the use or threat of **deadly force**, or against actual or threatened serious bodily harm.
6. Do not let unauthorized persons or items enter or leave.
7. **Items that may not enter without authorization (8):** firearms, ammunition, or other weapons; photographic equipment; explosives or incendiaries of any kind; telescopes or binoculars; recorders; electronic transmitting or receiving devices; controlled substances or alcoholic beverages; poisonous or corrosive solids, liquids, or gases.
8. **Items that may not leave (2):** company property without a signed and authorized **property pass**; plans, photographs, drawings, tracings, or any classified or sensitive document.
9. Security personnel treat as confidential the official matters of the department, its officers, and the company.

**Takeaway:** chapter 3 insists on preparing detailed general and special orders **before** bidding. With them, the scope and the proposed agreement, vendors can calculate costs and requirements.

### Appendix 3C — Sample Operating Agreement for Security Service (pp. 129–142)

**What it is:** a model operating agreement between the client company (“the Company”) and the security company (“the Contractor”). It takes effect at 12:01 a.m. It has three schedules: insurance, compensation, and billing rates.

#### A. General Provisions

| Clause | Content |
| --- | --- |
| A.1 | The Contractor protects all real and personal property of the complex (owned, leased, or in possession). Risks: vandalism, theft, trespass, fire, and other damaging events. Number of officers, assignments, and schedules: according to the attached *Statement of Work*. |
| A.2 | Officers arrive fully equipped with uniforms and necessary equipment. |
| A.3 | They are employees of the Contractor, which pays their wages, expenses, and taxes. |
| A.4 | Insurance per Schedule 1. Policies not cancelable except with **15 days'** written notice to the Company. Certificates before a set date and at each renewal. |
| A.5 | Officers' conduct is governed by agreed standard rules and by special written instructions from the Company, with a copy to the Contractor. This does not reduce the Contractor's responsibility. |

#### B. Physical and Training Requirements

1. **Experience:** officers, at least **1 year** of satisfactory prior employment in a similar function. Supervisors, at least **2 years** as an officer.
2. **Before accepting him:** background check (as the law permits) and interview with an authorized representative of the Company.
3. This is in addition to the Contractor's screening and investigation, the results of which are reported to the Company. A copy of the employment application is provided. No one is proposed who was previously removed from another assignment for disciplinary reasons or improper conduct.
4. **Health and physical condition:** good general health, with no defects that prevent normal duties. Standards:
   - a. Adequate proportion between height and weight.
   - b. Binocular vision correctable to **20/30 (Snellen)**.
   - c. Distinguish the standard colors.
   - d. Hear a normal conversation at **20 feet** and a whisper at **10 feet**, with each ear.
   - e. Moderate to strenuous physical exertion: (1) standing or walking the entire shift; (2) climbing stairs and ladders; (3) lifting or carrying up to **50 pounds**; (4) running short distances; (5) self-defense.
   - **Medical examination** within the **90 days** before entering service. **Annual** examination within **30 days** of the anniversary of the previous one.
   - Copy of the medical certificate to the authorized representative before the assignment, with the employee's **written consent** to the examination.
   - The final decision on acceptance, and the right to waive requirements, belong to the Company's authorized representative.
5. **Labor relations and training:**
   - The Contractor handles its own relations with any union and resolves disputes. It may freely sign collective bargaining agreements.
   - No collective bargaining agreement binds the Company to the Contractor's employees or its union, at the end of the agreement or ever.
   - In the event of an actual or potential labor dispute that threatens the service: immediate notice to the representative, with all the information.
   - **Prior training:** at least **8 h** in the classroom and **16 h** of supervised practice on the post (on-the-job training). The representative approves the training shifts.
   - **Supervisors:** at least **24 h** of supervised training in operating the control center, and full competence for all its functions.
   - All of this training, and any refresher the Contractor proposes, is **at the Contractor's expense**.
   - **Classroom topics (4):** a. legal limits of arrest, search, and seizure; b. fire control, detection, and reporting, including portable extinguishers and control of automatic sprinklers; c. how to report a violation of law to the authority (normally through the representative; in an emergency, direct contact; the procedures by type of emergency are in the special orders manual); d. appearance, attitude, conduct, and chains of command according to the officer's manual.
6. **Specialized training:** paid at a special base training rate, the same for all personnel. The Contractor pays whatever exceeds that rate.
7. **Continuity of service:** the Contractor protects plant, property, equipment, materials, and personnel during strikes, strike threats, work stoppages, or other interference. Officers continue to report, do not leave the post without relief, and do the extra tasks the representative sets. If it fails, the Company may hire another vendor during the contingency and **charge** the Contractor the additional costs.
8. **Quality and removal of personnel:** service by qualified, careful, and efficient employees, in accordance with best practices. On request, **with or without cause**, the Contractor removes any employee who may be guilty of improper conduct, is unqualified, or is not needed (the text says “in the Contractor's sole discretion”). If so instructed, it replaces him immediately.
9. **Control center equipment:** provided and maintained by the Company. Damage beyond normal wear is paid by the Contractor, as a credit on the next invoice.
10. **Inspections:** unannounced, daily, at least **once between 1800 and 0600 h**, by a Contractor supervisor not assigned to the site (non-resident). At the end an *Officer’s Daily Report* is completed and left in a sealed envelope for the representative. Supervisors do not go past the control center without authorization, except in a bona fide emergency.
11. **Arrests:** no detention or arrest without the representative's express consent or written instruction, unless the written orders provide for it. Officers **do not sign complaints** on behalf of the Company; if the police request it, they are referred to the representative.
12. **Schedules:** delivered at least **5 business days** before they take effect. No changes or substitutions without prior notice, except in a bona fide emergency.
13. **Rotation:** officers rotate shifts at a frequency **no greater than 6 weeks**. Permanent fixed shifts only with the representative's prior approval.

#### C. Equipment

1. The Contractor provides and maintains, under a **24-month lease-purchase**:
   - a. An electronic security tour management system or another acceptable one, with enough equipment for all personnel on duty at the same time.
   - b. A bicycle with its lock system.
   - c. Two two-way radios for the patrol.
2. The Company provides **one set of master keys**. They are not duplicated and never leave the premises. If they are lost or proven to have been duplicated, the Contractor pays for **rekeying** all exterior locks, doors, and gates.

#### D. Compensation

1. Pay to personnel per Schedule 2, plus benefits. Work-hour limits:
   - a. Full-time personnel do not work at another site served by the Contractor while assigned here.
   - b. Maximum **12 continuous hours** in a shift.
   - c. Maximum **48 hours** in a normal week (from **Saturday through the following Friday**).
   - d. Maximum **2 different shifts** in a normal week, unless a need is justified to the Company.
   - In an emergency, the designated representative may approve exceptions.
2. **Adjustment of compensation (penalties):**
   - For the listed failures, the Contractor's charge is equitably reduced, by mutual agreement.
   - While this is being agreed, the Company may deduct **50 dollars per occurrence, per day, and per incident**.
   - If there is no agreement within **2 months** of the deduction, the amount deducted is deemed accepted.
   - Written advance notice of the intent to deduct is always given.
   - This does not limit legal action if the actual damage exceeds what was withheld. Not demanding compliance once does not prevent demanding it later.

**Failures that trigger the deduction (11):**

- a. Failing to fill the number of officers for each assignment and shift of the *Statement of Work*.
- b. Failing to assign trained substitutes.
- c. Allowing more than **12 h in 24 h** or more than **48 h per week** without the representative's written consent.
- d. Failing to keep a complete record of each employee's hours.
- e. Failing to pay each employee the agreed minimum.
- f. Raising or lowering an employee's pay without prior notice to the representative.
- g. Failing to provide the documentation of the medical examination and investigation prior to assignment.
- h. Failing to assign an officer with the correct uniform and equipment.
- i. Failing to replace an employee within **8 hours** of the request.
- j. Failing to provide the training required before assignment (the text refers to a “Training” section; it is in section B).
- k. Failing to provide all necessary equipment and material.

#### E. Short-Term Supplemental Service

1. In emergencies: up to **5 additional officers within 4 hours** of notice, and more reasonable personnel within the following **8 hours**. If the Contractor does not respond and the Company covers with others, the Contractor pays the extra cost.
2. If **4 hours'** notice cannot be given, the “Short-Notice Supplemental Service” rate from Schedule 3 is paid. With sufficient notice, standard rates apply.

#### F. Audit and Payment

1. **Weekly** summary of hours (regular and special). **Monthly or biweekly** invoice. Payment within **30 days**.
2. The Company may periodically audit the hiring and training records.
3. Total payment: the bid rate corresponding to the wage of the employee who actually performs the service, for each hour of scheduled coverage (Schedule 3).

#### G. Term

- Begins at **12:01 a.m.** and expires at **12:00 midnight** on the dates set.
- Early termination: **30 days'** written notice by either party.
- Extension: in writing before expiration, on the same terms.
- Notices: by **prepaid, registered mail**.

#### Schedule 1 — Insurance (figures illustrative only)

| Coverage | Amount |
| --- | --- |
| A. Standard Workers’ Compensation and Employee Liability, self-insured | $100,000 |
| B.1 General liability: bodily injury | $5,000,000 |
| B.2 General liability: property damage | $5,000,000 |

#### Schedule 2 — Compensation (figures illustrative only)

| Category | Step | Seniority | Hourly pay |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Officer | 1 | 0 to 6 months | $10.25 |
| Officer | 2 | 6 to 12 months | $11.15 |
| Officer | 3 | 13 to 18 months | $12.05 |
| Officer | 4 | 19 to 24 months | $12.60 |
| Officer | 5 | More than 24 months | $13.20 |
| Supervisor or senior officer | 1 | Up to 24 months | $12.10 |
| Supervisor or senior officer | 2 | More than 24 months | $14.20 |
| Training rate | — | — | $10.25 |

- These are rates for discussion, not suggested salaries. The local wage market must be analyzed thoroughly.
- **Benefits (at a minimum):**
  - A. Work on a Contractor holiday: **time and a half** for everyone.
  - B. Vacation: **1 week (40 h)** at the end of each year, up to 5 years of continuous service. **2 weeks (80 h)** at the end of each year after **5 years**.
  - C. The employee chooses to take the vacation or be paid for it. It is **not carried over** to the following year.
  - D. Uniforms and equipment at no cost to the employee.

**Exam note:** steps 1 (“0 to 6 months”) and 2 (“6 to 12 months”) overlap at month 6.

#### Schedule 3 — Billing Rates (figures illustrative only)

- **I. Personnel.** The “straight time” and “overtime” columns are blank. Printed rates:
  - A. Officer: $15.10, $15.90, $13.35, $14.40, and $15.20 (five, like the five steps of Schedule 2).
  - B. Supervisor: $17.20 and $16.90.
  - C. Training: $14.85.
- **II. Lease-purchase equipment (12 months):** electronic tour system, one bicycle, and two portable radios. Each with full maintenance and monthly fee left blank.
- **III. Short-notice supplemental service:** hourly rate (blank) if there is no 4-hour notice. If there is, the standard rates in item I apply.
- **IV. Benefits (to be filled in):** A. life insurance; B. medical and health; C. long-term disability. For each: categories covered, amount or coverage, and employee contribution.
- **V. Number of officers in the company**, name of the bidding company, and signature of its authorized representative.

**Exam note:** clause C.1 and the chapter 3 example set the lease-purchase at **24 months**. Schedule 3 says **12 months**.

**Exam note:** if the rates are read in the order of the steps, they do not rise with seniority: they drop from step 2 to 3, and the supervisor rate drops from 1 to 2. Step 3 would bill $13.35 with a wage of $12.05. The book does not explain the correspondence and states that the figures are illustrative.

**Takeaway:** chapter 3 asks that the agreement reflect the client's needs according to its risk assessment, not the vendor's generic contract. The vendor's willingness to adapt it is a key selection criterion. Key figures: 50 dollars per failure, limits of 12 h and 48 h, 5 officers in 4 h, 30 days' notice, and payment at 30 days.

### Appendix 4A — Application for Consulting Assignment (pp. 163–164)

**What it is:** a form the company asks each consultant candidate to complete. It allows them to be compared using the same data.

**Consultant data (fields):**

- Consultant's name; name and address of his firm.
- Telephone, e-mail, and website.
- Main specialty.
- Last position before becoming a consultant; last employer; date he left.
- Start date as a consultant; total years practicing.
- Years of study; university.
- Professional or academic designations.
- Professional affiliations and memberships, and their length.
- Awards or recognitions in the security industry.
- Published works, if any.
- Basic consulting fee.

**Two references, with the same fields:** client name; contact person, with phone and e-mail; main purpose of the project; duration of the project.

**Attachments:**

- Certificates of **professional indemnity** insurance or equivalent.
- Certificates of **liability** insurance or equivalent.

**Takeaway:** it is step 2 of the five consultant selection steps in chapter 4 (a tailored application). Alternative: request letters of service and use the form as a checklist. The résumé is attached and, where required, proof of license or registration.

### Appendix 4B — Résumé (pp. 165–166)

**What it is:** a sample résumé of a fictitious consultant, “John Doe, CPP” (Certified Protection Professional). Its distinctive feature is the knowledge, skills, and abilities (KSAs) section.

- **Work history:** U.S. Army military police, 3 years; deputy sheriff, 26 years; university assistant professor, 13 years.
- **Education:** bachelor's degree (B.S., Bachelor of Science) in Police Science and Administration.
- **Publications (3):** the book *Training the Security Officer* (2021); the article “Why Security Counts” (August 2020); the chapter “Consultants as Trainers” in *Security Consulting* (2018).
- **Affiliations and achievements:** CPP designation; member of ASIS International; member of the Institute of Management Consultants; 2018 *Distinguished Service Accolade* from the IAPSC (International Association of Professional Security Consultants).
- **KSAs**, each with concrete evidence:
  - Analyzing security systems and improving processes: designed the security system and procedures for a new cannabis dispensary. In total, more than **40 systems** designed across several industries.
  - Accomplished trainer: created the program and trained officers in general security and **use of force** at **28 sites** of a company. Other organizations replicated his programs.
  - Knowledge of current design tools: courses from leading electronic security vendors (fictitious examples: ABC Corporation, Acme Camera Corporation, Loud Alarms). Certified in CAD/CAM (computer-aided design and manufacturing) and other software.

**Takeaway:** chapter 4 notes that there are many types of résumé and that this one includes a KSA section. There the consultant demonstrates his ability with concrete, measurable achievements.

### Appendix 4C — Professional Consulting Services Agreement (pp. 167–180)

**What it is:** a model contract between an individual consultant (“Consultant”) and the client company (“Company”). It includes supplementary agreements, time and expense forms, and the expense and travel policies.

#### Main Contract (10 clauses)

| No. | Clause | Content |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Retainer-Term | The consultant is an **independent contractor**, not an employee. He provides services from the contract date until a set end date. |
| 2 | Statement of Work | He does the work in the “Scope of Work” schedule when the company requests it, at places mutually agreed. |
| 3 | Payment | (a) Rate per unit of time, with a cap of days or hours (blank). Exceeding it requires a written amendment. Travel time requested by the company is paid at the same rate, whether during normal hours or not. (b) Travel and other expenses, per the “Consultant Expense” schedule. |
| 4 | Patents | Promptly disclose ideas, inventions, discoveries, and improvements (*Subject Inventions*), patentable or not. Applies to what is conceived or reduced to practice in the work and based on nonpublic company information. Keep a written record of technical activity. Records and inventions are the **exclusive property of the company**. During and after the contract, sign whatever is needed for the company to obtain patents. |
| 5 | Copyrights | All writings are the exclusive property of the company, with the exclusive right to assign the *copyright* in any country. The company will make every effort to give the consultant a **nonexclusive** right to publish them, if circumstances (including security regulations) permit. |
| 6 | Professional standards | Best effort and the highest professional quality. |
| 7 | Security (classified information) | The company notifies which information is *Classified* or *Restricted Data*. The consultant complies with the requirements of the U.S. Government or of the company. If he keeps classified material outside the company: he needs a **facility clearance**, signs a security agreement with the Department of Defense, and applies its industrial security manual (*Department of Defense Industrial Security Manual for Safeguarding Classified Security Information*). He promptly returns the classified material when the security agreement or this contract ends. |
| 8 | Risk of loss | The consultant assumes the risk of his personal injuries and of damage to or loss of his property. If he employs others at company facilities, he proves general commercial liability insurance for a minimum amount (blank). |
| 9 | Privileged or proprietary information | Do not disclose nonpublic information obtained from any source (the company, its customers, associates, or other contractors) without prior written consent. Exception: what the work itself requires. |
| 10 | Termination | Either party may terminate, in whole or in part, **at any time**, with written notice. |

**Useful comparison:** here termination requires no notice period. The operating agreement in Appendix 3C requires 30 days.

#### Consulting Security Agreement—Joint Certification

Consultant and company certify (3):

1. Classified information does not physically leave the company's premises.
2. The contract is performed on the company's premises.
3. The consultant and the certifying employees do not disclose classified information to unauthorized persons.

**Exam note:** in this form the company is called “Contractor”; in the main contract it is “Company.”

#### Conflict of Interest Statement

- The consultant warrants, to the best of his knowledge, that there are no facts that could create an **organizational conflict of interest** and that he has disclosed everything relevant.
- If one is discovered, he discloses it immediately and in writing to the **Contracting Officer**. It includes the measures taken or proposed to avoid or mitigate it.

#### Professional Services Log

- Header: consultant, contract number, period, number of days, and Requestor/Monitor.
- Instructions (4):
  1. Record on the same day the work is done.
  2. Cross-check against the consultant's Statement of Professional Services, for accuracy and completeness.
  3. Explain every charge for off-site work and every approval of a rented car. The invoice must match.
  4. If space runs out, use the back.
- Columns: date; time; work order; project or task with brief description and evaluation of the work (citing the consultant's documents).
- Signature of the requestor/monitor. **Retain at least 3 years.**

#### Statement of Professional Services

- Header: consultant, week ending, address.
- Instructions (4): 1. complete it fully (one per trip, except local consultants); 2. attach vouchers, receipts, and tickets; 3. consultant's signature; 4. keep a copy and send the original to the corporation's supervisor.
- Services: project, contracts, hours per day from Monday through Sunday, total hours or days, and amount.
- Transportation: origin, destination, dates, and cost (attach receipts).
- Other expenses: meals; lodging; car rental (1)(2); local taxi or bus (2); telephone (2); mileage in own car (3); parking (1); other (2)(4).
  - (1) Attach all receipts.
  - (2) Attach receipt if over **$5.00**.
  - (3) Mileage at the current rate.
  - (4) Explain.
- Dated signatures: consultant, “approved by,” and “audited by.”

#### Policy on Consultant’s Expenses

- Documented and approved expense report **within 30 days** after each trip.
- Original receipts for **everything except meals**. No receipt is needed where one is not normally given, such as taxi or bus **under $10.00 per trip**.
- Keep an **expense diary** and retain it **permanently** as a personal record, unless requested with the report.
- The requirements follow the documentation rules of the IRS (Internal Revenue Service, the U.S. tax authority). Complying with the policy equals complying with the IRS.
- **Travel expenses:** actual and necessary expenses for lodging, subsistence, incidentals, and transportation in **coach class** are reimbursed. Or mileage, if use of a personal car is authorized in place of air travel. Outside the local area, coach class is not exceeded, except for lack of schedules or space, or a different agreement.
- **Local consultants:** meals and mileage are not reimbursed. Authorized trips between work sites are. Daily commuting distance = the vicinity or a **50-mile radius** of the assigned work location.
- **Rental car** (out-of-town consultant, authorized on arrival): the company pays the charges necessary for the work. Mileage for personal use is paid by the consultant.
- **Telephone and telecommunications:** reasonable and necessary ones are reimbursed.
- **Other expenses:** reimbursed with written approval and the consultant's certification that they were necessary. Examples: use of computers and rental of test equipment.
- **Substantiation to the IRS:** adequate records and documentary evidence of 5 elements: (a) amount, (b) time, (c) place, (d) business purpose, (e) business relationship.

#### Consultant Travel Policy

The travel policy is for employees, but it is extended to consultants to judge whether expenses are reasonable and necessary.

**Mode of travel:**

- The most direct transportation available. By jet, normally a class below first.
- The consultant organizes the trip and reports it afterward in the Statement of Services. **No advances** of cash or tickets.
- a. Fares of more than **$10.00 per trip**: with receipt attached.
- b. **First class** or other luxury: requires documentation and only in 4 cases:
  1. Schedules or availability do not permit another class.
  2. Overnight departures between **9:00 p.m. and 6:00 a.m.** (local time) with a flight of **4 hours or more**.
  3. Physical disability that requires it, with a medical certificate or specific management approval.
  4. When line supervision judges that useful work can be done en route only in first class. Requires management approval.

**Personal vehicle:**

- Local consultant: no meals or mileage between home or office and the assigned site. Authorized trips between work sites are paid. Commuting distance: **50 miles** from the company facility.
- Use on domestic trips: mileage at the current rate, not exceeding the air fare in a class below first. The time recognized is limited to normal flight time; the excess is not paid.
- Mandatory minimum insurance:
  - a. Cars: bodily injury **$250,000 per person** and **$500,000 per accident**; property damage **$50,000 per accident**. The deductible is not reimbursed.
  - b. **Personal aircraft** (owned or rented): **prohibited** for company business.

**Car rental:**

- Permitted if it will be used at least **20 miles per day**. Normally not for local trips.
- Prior approval whenever possible. Only **standard or compact** models; the extra cost of sports or luxury cars is not paid.
- Personal mileage is not paid by the company. The **full collision insurance** bought from the rental company is reimbursed.
- Responsibilities: a. promptly report accidents with damage or injuries to the rental company, the local police, the consultant's supervisor, and the company's **security control center**; b. return the car to the rental company or its representative.

**Reporting:**

- Travel expenses reported **within 30 days** after the trip.
- Original receipts except meals. Not required for expenses that usually have none, such as taxi or bus.
- a. Unusual expenses (secretarial service, office equipment rental): explain and justify them each time.
- b. Dry cleaning and laundry: only on trips of **more than 4 days** or in unusual circumstances that are explained.
- c. Meals and lodging: actual expenses that are reasonable and necessary.
- d. Calls to company facilities: collect and over tie-lines when they exist.

**Caps, losses, and deviations:**

- Maximum lodging per night in the headquarters area, taxes included: blank. Cap on three daily meals: blank.
- The company is not responsible for loss of cash or for loss of or damage to personal property while traveling.
- Deviations from the policy are approved in specific cases with unusual circumstances. They must be documented and approved by the appropriate management.

**Exam note:** the receipt thresholds do not match. The form asks for a receipt from **$5.00**; the expense and travel policies use **$10.00 per trip**; the reporting section exempts taxi and bus without setting an amount.

**Exam note:** the daily commuting distance is measured from the “assigned work location” (expense policy) or from the “company facility” (travel policy). Both use 50 miles.

**Takeaway:** chapter 4 presents it as the contract signed after negotiating with the best candidate, with supplementary agreements. Its forms show the client the time and expense records that will be required. Key points: independent contractor, inventions and writings belong to the client, confidentiality, and free termination.

### Key Terms (appendices 1A–4C)

| Term | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| Misconduct | Willful or wanton disregard of the interests of the employer or coworkers, or serious or repeated negligence (1A). |
| Gross misconduct | Work-related crime with conviction or confession, or flagrant and wanton disregard of the rights of the employer or coworkers (1A). |
| Willful misconduct | Basis for dismissal in 1A. Includes violating any of the 37 rules. |
| Progressive discipline | Increasing sanctions according to seriousness and mitigating factors: from verbal counseling to the last warning (1B). |
| Preponderance of evidence | Level of proof the disciplinary investigation must reach (1A). |
| Fundamental fairness | Principle guiding the 17 questions before sanctioning (1A). |
| *No-call no-show* | Missing work without notice. Cause for immediate dismissal (1B). |
| Scope of work | Tasks, days, hours, and total hours of the service (3A). |
| ST / OT | Straight time / overtime hours. |
| General orders | Performance standards for all officers (3B). |
| Special orders | Duties of each specific assignment (3B). |
| Special instructions | Memoranda for special events, such as inspections or tours (3B). |
| Property pass | Signed authorization to remove company property (3B). |
| Adjustment of compensation | Reduction of the charge for noncompliance; provisional deduction of 50 dollars per occurrence, per day, and per incident (3C). |
| *Short-Notice Supplemental Service* | Special reinforcement rate when there is no 4-hour notice (3C). |
| Lease-purchase | Way of providing the patrol equipment in 3C. |
| Rekeying | Paid by the Contractor if it loses or duplicates the master keys (3C). |
| Independent contractor | The consultant's status in 4C: not an employee. |
| *Subject Inventions* | Consultant's inventions tied to the work, which become the company's (4C). |
| Facility clearance | Requirement to keep classified material outside the company (4C). |
| Organizational conflict of interest | Disclosed immediately and in writing to the Contracting Officer (4C). |
| KSAs | Knowledge, skills, and abilities on the résumé (4B). |

### Appendix 7A — Supervisor's Checklist (pp. 265–266)

**What it is:** a checklist for supervisors with behaviors and symptoms that may indicate substance abuse. It accompanies chapter 7 (workplace substance abuse).

**Rule of use (most likely to be asked):**

- That some indicators appear **does not necessarily mean** the person has a substance abuse problem.
- One must look for **groups or patterns** of behaviors and symptoms (*clusters*), not isolated indicators.
- The list is only an **aid** for detecting possible abuse; it is not for diagnosing.

The book divides the indicators into four blocks (17 + 21 + 13 + 17 = 68; own count).

#### 1. Tardiness and Absenteeism — 17

| # | Indicator |
| --- | --- |
| 1 | Frequent breaks |
| 2 | Long meals |
| 3 | Tardiness |
| 4 | Arriving late and leaving early |
| 5 | Being absent from the area or office |
| 6 | Going to the restroom an abnormal number of times |
| 7 | Unexplained absences |
| 8 | Absences due to accidents on and off the job |
| 9 | Missing work before and after payday |
| 10 | Missing work before and after holidays |
| 11 | Missing work on Mondays and Fridays |
| 12 | Using up vacation as soon as it is earned |
| 13 | Missing work during periods of heavy workload |
| 14 | Reporting sick after being denied vacation |
| 15 | Asking for vacation to be extended |
| 16 | Asking for sick leave to be extended |
| 17 | Extending sick leave again and again |

#### 2. Performance — 21

| # | Indicator |
| --- | --- |
| 1 | Repeated procrastination |

| 15 | Missed deadlines |

| 17 | Customer complaints |

| 20 | Sloppy work habits |
| 21 | Asset losses |

| # | Indicator |
| --- | --- |
| 1 | Inappropriate emotional outbursts |
| 2 | Mood swings at the beginning or end of the day |
| 3 | Overreaction to criticism |

| --- | --- |
| 1 | Inappropriate clothing |
