**Takeaway:** the supervisor observes work behavior (attendance, performance, dealings, appearance), not diagnoses. Only a pattern of several indicators justifies acting: it moves to the intervention in Appendix 7B and can ground the “reasonable suspicion” of 7C.

### Appendix 7B — Intervention Checklist (p. 267)

**What it is:** a brief list of what supervisors and managers should do, never do, and always do when intervening with an employee who may have a substance problem.

**Principle:** intervention serves to **correct, not punish**.

#### Recommended Steps (9, in the book's order)

1. Observe and document.
2. Confront the problem employee **in private**.
3. Discuss his performance and conduct.
4. Reaffirm expectations.
5. Offer reasonable accommodations.
6. Set goals.
7. Document the results.
8. Communicate with executive management.
9. Follow up.

#### Never vs. Always

The two columns are independent lists: there is no row-by-row correspondence.

| # | Should never — 7 | Should always — 8 |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Diagnose a personal problem | Monitor performance and conduct |
| 2 | Take responsibility for others' personal problems or matters | Document specifics |
| 3 | Generalize | Follow the organization's policy and procedures |
| 4 | Moralize | Talk with the employee in private |
| 5 | Cover up | Interview him with a witness |
| 6 | Self-treat the problem | Offer professional help |
| 7 | Enter into a confidential or protective relationship | Let him know you care |
| 8 | — | Let the employee make a choice |

**Exam note:** apparent internal contradiction between “talk with the employee in private” and “interview him with a witness.” The book does not explain how they are reconciled or who the witness should be (pending).

**Takeaway:** the “never” list (do not cover up, do not protect, do not carry the problem) is the practical way to avoid the denial, enabling, and codependency described in chapter 7. The supervisor focuses on performance, documents, and refers to professional help, such as the employee assistance program (EAP).

### Appendix 7C — Sample Substance Abuse Policy (pp. 268–272)

**What it is:** a model substance abuse policy for a fictitious company, “XYZ Company.” It covers prohibitions, drug and alcohol testing, rehabilitation, costs, and definitions.

#### Scope

- XYZ declares itself a drug-free workplace.
- It does not permit its employees to be impaired by drugs or alcohol on company time or property.
- Failing to follow the policy’s rules, procedures, requirements **or spirit** leads to corrective action.
- Possible corrective actions, depending on the circumstances:
  - termination;
  - suspension;
  - warning;
  - probation;
  - any lesser sanction;
  - other action, at the company’s discretion, proportionate to the problem.

#### Use or possession at work

Prohibited on company property, at the job, or when doing company business:

- using or possessing alcoholic beverages or illegal drugs;
- unlawfully manufacturing, distributing, dispensing, possessing, offering, or using a controlled substance;
- possessing drug paraphernalia or empty alcohol containers on company time or property.

**Only exception:** alcohol served at company-sponsored events (for example, a year-end party). There, responsible, moderate consumption does not violate the policy.

#### Impairment

- Reporting to work, or performing company duties or business, while impaired by alcohol or drugs is prohibited.
- If an employee is believed to be impaired, in addition to other measures, the employee may be suspended, sent home, or reassigned for safety reasons while the situation is evaluated.

#### Off-duty use

- **Alcohol** off duty and off premises: prohibited if it
  1. causes impairment at work;
  2. harms attendance or performance; or
  3. otherwise harms the company’s image.
- **Illegal drugs:** prohibited in all circumstances, on or off duty and on or off the premises.

#### Legal drugs

- Using over-the-counter or prescription medications as directed by the doctor or manufacturer is not prohibited.
- **Abuse** of legal drugs is treated the same as use of illegal drugs.
- If proper use may affect the ability to work safely and effectively, the employee must notify their supervisor **in advance**. That way, steps can be taken to protect safety and productivity.

#### Drug convictions

- Anyone convicted of any drug offense **that occurred in the workplace** must notify their supervisor **within five days** of the conviction.
- XYZ may be required to report to the government agencies with which it has contracts.
- Link to section 7.8: the Drug-Free Workplace Act of 1988 (US) requires companies with federal contracts and grants over $25,000 to certify that they have drug-free workplace policies.

#### Job applicants

XYZ will not knowingly hire an applicant who currently **abuses** alcohol or legal drugs, or who currently **uses** illegal drugs. Nuance: for alcohol and legal drugs, abuse is what counts; for illegal drugs, use alone is enough.

#### Right of inspection

- XYZ may inspect employees’ personal belongings on its premises at any time, with or without notice:
  - vehicles;
  - lunch containers;
  - bags;
  - boxes;
  - packages;
  - desks;
  - lockers;
  - other personal belongings.
- Purpose: to enforce this policy or for other safety and security reasons.
- The premises include all employee parking areas and parking lots designated by the company.

#### Drug and alcohol testing policy

Any employee or applicant may be required to take a breath and/or urine test, for drugs or alcohol, in **three** circumstances:

| Circumstance | Who | Key rules |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Preemployment | All applicants for the facilities or job categories that management determines from time to time | Anyone who fails is **ineligible for at least one year** |
| Reasonable suspicion | Any employee | Raised by a supervisor or higher-level manager and concurred in by the highest-ranking manager available at the affected facility or department |
| Universal | All employees of the facilities or departments that management designates from time to time | Randomization done by the testing provider using its own systems; the provider notifies XYZ whom to test; may be done without notice at any time of the year |

**Definition of reasonable suspicion:** reasonable suspicion by a supervisor or higher-level manager, concurred in by the highest-ranking manager available, that:

- the employee’s faculties are impaired at work; or
- the employee has used or possessed illegal drugs.

**Factors that may support it** (open list, *not limited to*):

1. Direct observation, or reports from coworkers or others reasonably believed to be reliable.
2. Possession of drugs or alcohol on the premises, or use at work, before work, or during breaks, such that the employee is impaired on the premises.
3. Conduct, speech, or other physical signs consistent with impairment.
4. A pattern of abnormal or erratic behavior with no other satisfactory explanation.
5. Unexplained accidents, work injuries, or property damage.
6. A combination of some of these factors and/or others, in management’s judgment.

Management’s determination is **discretionary and final**.

**Consequence:** refusing the test, failing to cooperate, or testing positive for illegal drugs or alcohol leads to **termination**. Exception: as provided in the rehabilitation section.

#### Testing process

- **Scope:** urine and/or breath analysis, as XYZ and the provider decide.
- **Substances it may detect** (open list):
  1. marijuana;
  2. cocaine;
  3. opiates;
  4. amphetamines;
  5. phencyclidine (PCP).
- XYZ may expand or reduce the list at any time, with or without notice.
- It may request separate specimens if several tests are performed.
- Testing levels and standards are set by XYZ and the provider.
- **Confirmation:** every initial positive is confirmed with a **second test**, in accordance with applicable law.
- **Specimen:**
  - It is collected at a facility designated by XYZ.
  - Applicants and selected employees (universal or reasonable cause testing) must report at the exact time and place indicated.
  - An employee tested for suspected impairment is **taken to the testing site by a supervisor** or other designated person.
  - The employee must sign the consent and provide the information requested. If they do not, they may be terminated or lose the opportunity for employment.
- **Injured employee:** if seriously injured and unable to provide a specimen at the time of the accident, the employee must authorize access to hospital reports and other documents. These are used to determine whether controlled substances or alcohol were in the employee’s system.
- **Notification of results:** employees and applicants are notified of positive results and may explain them. If they do not respond in time, the result may stand as an **uncontested positive verification**.

#### Rehabilitation

- **Purpose and responsibility:**
  - XYZ recognizes drug dependency and alcoholism as **health problems**.
  - At management’s sole discretion and case by case, it will try to support an employee who becomes dependent.
  - It will help the employee identify rehabilitation services, referral agencies, or other resources.
  - But it is the employee’s responsibility to ensure that their problem does not affect their performance or endanger others.
  - Everyone is encouraged to seek help before a personal problem becomes a work problem.
- **Evaluation and treatment:**
  - As an alternative to discipline or termination, a chemical dependency evaluation may be permitted.
  - It is offered case by case, at management’s sole discretion.
  - If the evaluation recommends it, enrolling in and successfully completing an approved treatment may be offered **only once**. It is an alternative to discipline and a condition of continued employment.
  - **It does not apply to applicants.**
  - Return to work and any special conditions are decided case by case. XYZ’s interest in safety and operational efficiency is weighed.

#### Costs

| Item | Who pays |
| --- | --- |
| Mandatory drug or alcohol tests | XYZ |
| Treatment | The employee, to the extent not covered by their health insurance |

#### Definitions

- **Impairment:** a condition caused by any drug, alcohol, or a combination of them, that affects the employee in a physically or mentally detectable way.
  - Its symptoms are not limited to misconduct or obvious impairment (slurred speech, difficulty keeping balance, smell of alcohol).
  - It may be determined by any supervisor or manager, a medical professional, a scientific test such as urinalysis, or sometimes a layperson.
  - In some cases, with no objective or subjective indicator at all, mere use may constitute impairment.
- **Illegal drug:** any drug that (a) cannot be obtained legally, or (b) can be obtained legally but was not obtained or is not used legally.
  - It includes prescription drugs obtained illegally or used for purposes other than those prescribed.
  - It includes prescription drugs shared with a coworker, **in any circumstance**.
- **Legal drug:** a prescription or over-the-counter medication, legally obtained and used for the purpose for which it was prescribed or manufactured.
- **Drug paraphernalia:** objects, tools, and devices normally used to prepare, store, or administer illegal drugs. Examples (open list):
  - rolling papers;
  - roach clips;
  - glass pipes;
  - water pipes and bongs;
  - vials;
  - straws and spoons;
  - in some cases, hypodermic syringes.
- **Serious injury:** any work injury that stops work and requires medical attention of any kind.

**Exam note:**

- **Reasonable suspicion vs. reasonable cause:** the policy defines “reasonable suspicion,” but then uses “reasonable cause” as a synonym. The defined term is *reasonable suspicion*.
- **Fewer testing situations than the chapter:** the general guidance in section 7.8 gives six testing moments. For all workers: preemployment, reasonable suspicion, post-accident/injury, return to duty, and follow up. Random testing, only for safety-sensitive positions.
- Model 7C regulates only three situations. Its random “universal” test covers all employees in the designated areas, not just safety-sensitive positions.
- In addition, it defines “serious injury” but does not provide for post-accident testing: it only requires authorizing access to hospital reports.

**Takeaway:** 7C is the practical template for chapter 7: it turns prohibitions, testing, and rehabilitation into written rules. Figures to remember: conviction notice within five days, one year of ineligibility, five substances, a second confirmation test, and treatment offered only once.

### Appendix 8A — Sample Policy for Workplace Violence (pp. 293–294)

**What it is:** a model workplace violence (WPV) policy for the fictitious company XYZ. It puts into practice what section 8.4 requires of the policy of the workplace violence prevention and intervention (WVPI) program.

#### Statement and removal of the offender

- XYZ is committed to the safety of its personnel.
- It will not tolerate violence by anyone, on company property, against employees, visitors, guests, or other persons.
- Anyone who violates the policy on XYZ property must be removed from the premises as quickly as safety permits.
- They must stay off the premises until the investigation of the incident is complete.

#### Conduct that violates the policy (7)

1. Causing physical injury to another person.
2. Making threats.
3. Displaying aggressive or hostile behavior.
4. Intentionally damaging property.
5. Possessing a weapon on company property.
6. Committing acts related to sexual harassment or domestic violence.
7. Making false or misleading reports about what others do.

#### Response if the investigation confirms the violation

A “decisive and appropriate” response, which may include (open list):

- suspension and/or termination of any business relationship;
- reassignment of duties;
- suspension or termination of employment;
- and/or requesting the arrest and criminal prosecution of those involved.

#### Prevention prevails over procedure

No existing policy, practice, or procedure may prevent decisions made to avoid:

- a threat being carried out;
- a violent act occurring; or
- a life-threatening situation developing.

#### Duty to report

- All personnel must notify the company of any threat, real or perceived, that they have witnessed or received.
- Also of those they are told another person witnessed or received.
- Channels (3):
  1. employee hotline;
  2. their superior;
  3. the designated management representative (DMR).
- Every report is handled with discretion, investigated, and the relevant parties are notified.
- **No retaliation** against anyone who reports in good faith.

#### Protective or restraining orders

Anyone who requests or obtains a protective or restraining order that lists company locations as protected areas must give the DMR:

1. a copy of the petition and of the statements used to request it;
2. a copy of any temporary order granted;
3. a copy of any order that becomes permanent.

- **Reason:** XYZ must provide a safe workplace and protect its employees from threats. It cannot do so effectively without knowing who is under an order from a court, or another legal entity, to stay away from its locations.
- XYZ recognizes that this information is sensitive. It has procedures to receive it, store it, and act on it while respecting the privacy of the employee who reports.
- Those procedures are in a separate document.

#### DMR details (blank fields in the model)

Name · Title · Phone · Email · Office mail.

#### Contrast with section 8.4 (what a WPV policy must include)

| The policy must… (ch. 8.4) | Does 8A cover it? |
| --- | --- |
| Define unacceptable conduct | Yes: 7 behaviors |
| Regulate or prohibit weapons, to the extent permitted by law | Prohibits possessing weapons, without the legal qualifier (see Exam note) |
| Require prompt reporting of any concerning behavior or circumstance | Yes: duty to report real or perceived threats (sets no deadline) |
| Offer several ways to report | Yes: 3 channels |
| Guarantee that reports are treated seriously and with discretion | Yes: discretion and investigation |
| Commit to no retaliation for good faith reports | Yes |
| Require that protective or restraining orders be reported | Yes: copies to the DMR |
| Sanction violations, including false or misleading reports | Yes: a false report is a violation |
| Avoid the term “zero tolerance” | Does not use the literal term (see Exam note) |

**Exam note:**

- **“Zero tolerance”:** section 8.4 asks to avoid the term *zero tolerance* because it reduces reporting and safety. Model 8A does not use that label, but it opens by saying violence “will not be tolerated.” On a question, the correct answer is to avoid the expression “zero tolerance.”
- **Weapons:** section 8.4 says to regulate or prohibit weapons to the extent permitted by applicable law. 8A prohibits them without that qualifier.

**Takeaway:** 8A is the policy template of chapter 8. It defines behaviors, provides several channels, guarantees no retaliation, centralizes protective orders with the DMR, and puts prevention ahead of any procedure.

### Appendix 8B — Workplace Violence Plan Scenario (pp. 295–296)

**What it is:** an example of how to apply the WPV plan to a specific threat: a potentially violent, terminated former employee re-entering the building. Chapter 8 cites it when discussing training with periodic drills and exercises with outside police and emergency services.

#### Setup

- The security threat and risk assessment identified this threat as a **priority**.
- The stakeholders working group (SWG) is tasked with applying mitigations that manage the threat and its risk well.
- According to chapter 8, the SWG ends up forming the permanent threat management team (TMT). The TMT is also called the threat assessment team (TAT) or incident management team (IMT).
- **Best protection method** against this threat: **access control** to the premises.

#### Diffusion of benefits

Robust access and entry control also prevents and mitigates other external WPV threats. The right-hand column is our own classification using the aggressor typology of section 8.2.

| Appendix example | Aggressor type (section 8.2) |
| --- | --- |
| Criminal planning a robbery | Type 1: no legitimate relationship, with criminal intent |
| Delivery person planning an assault | Type 2: legitimate relationship (customer, patient, visitor, service provider) |
| Customer planning to intimidate employees | Type 2 |
| Person in an employee’s circle planning to harass them | Type 4: personal relationship, real or perceived, with an employee |
| Terminated former employee (the scenario’s threat) | Type 3: current or former employee, including contractors |

#### Assessment: comprehensive security survey

The current access and entry control system is evaluated, specifically against the return of a terminated former employee. The building’s physical, electronic, and design elements are reviewed against unauthorized entry. The book groups what must be reviewed into four blocks.

**1. Physical, electronic, and design elements (8)**

1. Signage.
2. Fencing.
3. Exterior lighting.
4. Door and window alarms.
5. Video surveillance system.
6. Entry intercom with audio and video.
7. Reception that monitors the entry control system, with controls to open doors remotely.
8. Protective barriers at reception and a duress/panic alarm.

**2. Access control policy and procedure (5)**

1. List of terminated former employees, with photos, at reception.
2. Policy prohibiting terminated employees from entering the property.
3. Collection of keys and IDs from terminated employees.
4. Deactivation of access credentials and passwords.
5. Response procedure when a terminated employee is seen on or near the property.

**3. Security personnel (3)**

1. List of terminated employees available to security, with photos and vehicle information.
2. Patrol of the building exterior, including parking lots.
3. Response capability: radio and telephone communication with the TMT and with police, fire, and ambulance services.

**4. Security and crime prevention awareness, education, and training for directly involved employees (3)**

1. Under no circumstances is a terminated employee allowed to enter the property and/or the building.
2. Immediately report the presence of a terminated employee on or near the property.
3. Train reception staff in their response role and in the use of the available security measures.

**Exam note:** the scenario calls the terminated former employee an “external threat.” But in the typology of section 8.2, a current or former employee is a **Type 3** aggressor. They are “external” only because they no longer have a right of access. If asked about the aggressor type, the answer is Type 3.

**Takeaway:** against the return of terminated employees, the best mitigation is layered access control. Layers: physical and electronic barriers, exit policy (keys, ID, credentials), security personnel, and training. It shows the chain of chapter 8: risk assessment, an SWG that mitigates, and a TMT that responds.

### Appendix: Standards in Security (pp. 297–300)

**What it is:** a general appendix on security standards. It explains what they are, their types, their benefits, management system standards, and the continual improvement cycle.

#### Definition and origin

- **Standard:** a set of criteria, guidelines, and best practices that improves the quality and reliability of products, services, or processes.
- Thanks to standards, many parts, processes, and systems work regardless of who creates or runs them, who uses them, and where.
- They arise to meet a market need or fill a gap in existing standards.
- They are coordinated by accredited standards developing organizations (SDOs). These bring together stakeholders from the private and public sectors to identify best practices.
- They are the basis of innovation and competitiveness, and a common language that supports national and international trade.
- **Four development principles:** consensus, openness, due process, and transparency.
- In security, they help nations, communities, societies, organizations, and individuals protect assets and gain resilience against natural and human threats.

#### Types of standards

| Type | Obligation | What the book says |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Voluntary | Not binding by themselves, but widely adopted | Those of the International Organization for Standardization (ISO) and of national standards institutes. Some have been integrated into national regulatory frameworks. In the US they are also set by trade or professional associations, such as ASIS International |
| Statutory or regulatory | Binding by law | Enforced by formal authorities |
| Mixed | Blurred boundary | Voluntary standards incorporated into laws or regulations: fire, construction, and life safety. Or standards that are technically voluntary but mandatory in practice (see below) |

**Voluntary but mandatory in practice:**

- Insurers may condition the availability and cost of casualty insurance on the use of approved materials or standardized practices.
- Contracts may require standards as a requirement. The adoption of standards is increasingly client driven.

#### Areas of ISO standards relevant to asset protection (5)

- facility management;
- health, environment and safety;
- risk management;
- security and resilience;
- information technology (IT) and information security.

#### Issuing bodies cited

| Body | What it standardizes (according to the book) | Nature |
| --- | --- | --- |
| ISO | The areas above; management systems | Voluntary, widely adopted; some integrated into national regulations |
| National standards institutes | Not detailed | Voluntary |
| ASIS International | Security management; has a broad library of standards and guidelines | Voluntary (professional association) |
| National Fire Protection Association (NFPA) | Not detailed | Not all voluntary: some are incorporated into regulations, such as building codes |
| Underwriters Laboratories (UL) | Various standards on security equipment: locks, alarms, and access control systems | Cited among the voluntary ones |
| Illuminating Engineering Society | Lighting standards and practices | Trade or professional association |
| Electronic Industries Association | Electronic components and products | Trade or professional association |

#### Standards cited by designation

| Designation | Issuer | Year | What it covers |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| ISO 9001 | ISO | Not indicated | Quality management systems. Together with ISO 14001, the most prominent management system standard |
| ISO 14001 | ISO | Not indicated | Environmental management systems |
| ASIS ORM.1-2017 | ASIS International | 2017 | *Security and Resilience in Organizations and their Supply Chains – Requirements with Guidance*: security and resilience in organizations and their supply chains, requirements with guidance. It is the source of figure A.1 |
| *Workplace Violence and Active Assailant – Prevention, Intervention, Response Standard* | ASIS International | 2020 | Workplace violence and active assailant: prevention, intervention, and response. **It does not appear in this appendix**: it is the reference standard for chapter 8 (references 8.11) |

#### Benefits of standards for the security professional (9)

1. Codify best practices and processes, and share lessons learned.
2. Provide tools to assess threats, risks, vulnerabilities, criticalities, and impacts.
3. Define measurement methods.
4. Document equipment performance requirements, to ensure effectiveness and safety.
5. Set design requirements so that devices, systems, and infrastructure withstand threats.
6. Define effective methods to identify people.
7. Increase knowledge and competence.
8. Improve information sharing between jurisdictions and interoperability.
9. Provide consistency in services.

#### Management system standards

- They help improve how the organization delivers services and runs processes. They are accepted and used in many fields.
- The most prominent: ISO 9001 (quality) and ISO 14001 (environment).
- The approach calls for analyzing the requirements of the organization and of stakeholders, and defining the processes that lead to success.
- They provide a framework for **continual improvement**. They increase the likelihood of achieving strategic, operational, tactical, and reputational objectives, and strengthen resilience.

#### Plan-Do-Check-Act (PDCA) cycle

- It is the **operating principle** of management system standards.
- It is a structured problem-solving method focused on continual improvement.

| Phase | Appendix text | Label in figure A.1 |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Plan | Identify and analyze the organization’s problems and assets, and rank them by importance | Define and analyze a problem and its context |
| Do | Prioritize the next steps, develop a detailed action plan, and execute it | Devise a solution, develop a detailed action plan, and execute it systematically |
| Check | Examine whether the solutions produce results consistent with the plan | Confirm results against the plan; identify deviations and problems |
| Act | If the solutions resolve the problems, standardize them across the organization, review the list of problems, and define new ones. Here the cycle starts over | Standardize the solution; review and define the next problems |

**Figure A.1** (caption: ASIS ORM.1-2017; label: *Plan-Do-Check-Act Model*):

- Input: stakeholders and interested parties with their **requirements and expectations**.
- Center: **organizational resilience management systems**, driven by the PDCA cycle.
- Output: stakeholders with **managed risk**.

**Exam note:** the header of these pages says “Investigations — Maximizing the Investigation,” and that of the index, “Physical Security.” It appears to be text reused from other volumes of the *Protection of Assets* series. It is a layout error; the content on standards is still valid.

**Takeaway:** standards are tools for the security professional. You need to distinguish voluntary, regulatory, and mixed standards, and master the PDCA cycle of management systems. In this book, the policy and program of chapter 8 rest on the 2020 ASIS standard on workplace violence and active assailants.

### Key terms (appendices 7A–Standards)

| Term | English | What it means here |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Cluster of indicators | *cluster* | Several symptoms or behaviors together; only then do they provide guidance (7A) |
| Intervention | *intervention* | Supervisor action to correct, not punish (7B) |
| Reasonable accommodation | *reasonable accommodation* | Fifth step of the intervention (7B) |
| Drug-free workplace | *drug-free workplace* | Scope statement of the policy (7C) |
| Impairment | *impairment* | Detectable physical or mental effect of drugs or alcohol; sometimes mere use is enough (7C) |
| Reasonable suspicion | *reasonable suspicion* | By a supervisor or higher-level manager, concurred in by the highest-ranking manager available (7C) |
| Preemployment testing | *preemployment testing* | For applicants; anyone who fails is ineligible for at least one year (7C) |
| Universal testing | *universal testing* | Random selection by the provider, without notice, in designated areas (7C) |
| Confirmation test | *confirmation* | Second test after an initial positive (7C) |
| Uncontested positive verification | *uncontested positive verification* | Effect of failing to respond in time to the positive notice (7C) |
| Illegal drug | *illegal drug* | Not legally obtainable, or obtained or used illegally; includes shared prescriptions (7C) |
| Legal drug | *legal drug* | Prescription or over-the-counter, legally obtained and used for its purpose (7C) |
| Drug paraphernalia | *drug paraphernalia* | Objects to prepare, store, or administer illegal drugs (7C) |
| Serious injury | *serious injury* | Work injury that stops work and requires medical attention (7C) |
| Chemical dependency | *chemical dependency* | Evaluated as an alternative to discipline; treatment offered once (7C) |
| Designated management representative (DMR) | *designated management representative* | Receives reports and copies of protective orders (8A) |
| Protective or restraining order | *protective / restraining order* | If it covers company locations, copies go to the DMR (8A) |
| Good faith report | *good faith report* | Protected against retaliation (8A) |
| Stakeholders working group (SWG) | *stakeholders working group* | Applies the mitigations; basis of the future TMT (8B) |
| Threat management team (TMT) | *threat management team* | Also TAT or IMT; security communicates with it (8B) |
| Diffusion of benefits | *diffusion of benefits* | One measure against one threat also protects against others (8B) |
| Security survey | *security survey* | Comprehensive review of the property and building (8B) |
| Duress/panic alarm | *duress/panic alarm* | Reception protection element (8B) |
| Standard | *standard* | Criteria, guidelines, and best practices for quality and reliability (Standards) |
| Standards developing organization (SDO) | *standards developing organization* | Coordinates standards with public and private stakeholders (Standards) |
| Voluntary / statutory or regulatory / mixed standard | *voluntary / statutory or regulatory / mixed* | The three types of obligation (Standards) |
| Management system standard | *management system standard* | Continual improvement framework; examples ISO 9001 and ISO 14001 (Standards) |
| PDCA cycle | *Plan-Do-Check-Act* | Operating principle of management systems (Standards) |
